, and the whole shaded region is the liquid region
\Omega
.

Corollary 3.10
.

Fix real numbers
a<b
,
d>0
, and
A>0
; assume that
\mu_{a}=A\cdot\mu_{\semci}^{(d)}=\mu_{b}
. Then, the inverted height function
G^{\star}:[a,b]\times[0,A]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and density process
(\varrho_{t}^{\star})
associated with boundary data
(\mu_{a};\mu_{b})
are given by

(3.13)

\displaystyle\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)=A\cdot\varrho_{\semci}^{(d+\frac{A(b-t)(t-a)}{b-a+2\kappa})}(x);\qquad G^{\star}(t,y)=\bigg(d+\displaystyle\frac{A(b-t)(t-a)}{b-a+2\kappa}\bigg)^{1/2}\cdot\gamma_{\semci}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{y}{A}\Big),

where
\kappa=\kappa(a,b,d)>0
is defined by

(3.14)

\displaystyle\kappa=\displaystyle\frac{d}{A}+\displaystyle\frac{a-b}{2}+\bigg(\Big(\displaystyle\frac{b-a}{2}\Big)^{2}+\Big(\displaystyle\frac{d}{A}\Big)^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Proof.

This will follow from using
3.3
to restrict the limit shape from
Example

3.9
to a smaller interval. To implement this, let
(\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in[a-\kappa,b+\kappa]}
denote the bridge-limiting measure process on
[a-\kappa,b+\kappa]
with boundary data
(\delta_{0},\delta_{0})
. Then, for any
t\in[a-\kappa,b+\kappa]
, we have

(3.15)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\star}=A\cdot\mu_{\semci}^{(\frac{A(b+\kappa-t)(t-a+\kappa)}{b-a+2\kappa})};\qquad\displaystyle\frac{A(b+\kappa-t)(t-a+\kappa)}{b-a+2\kappa}=d+\displaystyle\frac{A(b-t)(t-a)}{b-a+2\kappa},

where the first statement follows from
Example

3.9
and the second from the choice (
3.14
) of
\kappa
. It follows that
\widetilde{\mu}_{a}^{\star}=\mu_{\semci}^{(d)}=\widetilde{\mu}_{b}^{\star}
, and so from
3.3
we find
\mu_{t}^{\star}=\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\star}
for each
t\in[a,b]
. Applied with (
3.15
), this yields the first statement of (
3.13
), which (together with (
3.3
), (
3.4
), and (
3.12
)) implies its second statement.
∎

3.2.
The Functions
(u^{\star},\varrho^{\star})

In this section we recall results from
[
35
,
36
]
stating that, under conditions on
\mu_{a}
and
\mu_{b}
, the associated bridge-limiting density process
\mu_{t}
gives rise to a pair of functions
(u_{t}^{\star},\varrho_{t}^{\star})
that satisfy certain equations (which will later be interpreted in terms of elliptic partial differential equations for the associated height and inverted height functions in
Section

3.3
below). For notational convenience, we at first restrict to the case of
3.1
when
(a,b)=(0,1)
and
A=1
(explaining how the more general case follows from it through scaling in
3.14
below).

The following result appears as stated below
7
7

7

Let us mention that, as stated, those results apply to Dyson Brownian motion (
2.16
) started at some
\bm{u}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
, conditioned to be
\bm{v}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
at time
1
. By
2.15
, this is equivalent to non-intersecting Brownian bridges of variance
n^{-1}
, starting at
\bm{u}
and ending at
\bm{v}
.
in
[
7
]
and was originally established in
[
35
,
36
]
(see
8
8

8

An alternative proof for the smoothness of
u^{\star}
and
\varrho^{\star}
(the second part of
3.11
) would proceed by first using
3.16
(see also
3.17
) below to show that the associated height function
H^{\star}
weakly solves the divergence-form elliptic partial differential equation (
3.27
). The bounds of
[
35
, Corollary 2.8(c)]
verify that this equation is elliptic (using
3.19
below), uniformly on compact subsets of
\Omega
. Together with regularity estimates for weak solutions to such elliptic differential equations given by
[
33
, Lemma 6.27, Theorem 8.20, Equation (13.41)]
and
[
5
, Chapter 3.6]
, this yields the smoothness of
H^{\star}
, implying that of
u^{\star}
and
\varrho^{\star}
by
3.13
below.

[
35
, Theorem 2.1]
). Here, we recall from
3.7
that, at a given time
t\in(0,1)
, any bridge-limiting measure process
\mu^{\star}
has a density
\varrho_{t}^{\star}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
; we also recall from (
3.8
) the liquid region
\Omega
associated with any
\varrho\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
.

Lemma 3.11

(
[
7
, Theorems 2.3 and 2.11]
)
.

Fix probability measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathscr{P}
with densities
\varrho_{0},\varrho_{1}
, respectively, such that

(3.16)

\displaystyle\text{$\varrho_{0}$ and $\varrho_{1}$ are compactly supported};\qquad\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\varrho_{0}(x)\big|<\infty;\qquad\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\varrho_{1}(x)\big|<\infty.

Let
(\mu_{t}^{\star})_{t\in[0,1]}\in\mathcal{C}\big([0,1];\mathscr{P}\big)
denote the bridge-limiting measure process on
[0,1]
with boundary data
(\mu_{0};\mu_{1})
. For each
t\in[0,1]
, denote the density of
\mu_{t}^{\star}\in\mathscr{P}
by
\varrho_{t}^{\star}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. There exists a measurable function
u^{\star}:[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, denoted by
u_{t}^{\star}(x)=u^{\star}(t,x)
, satisfying the following three properties.

(1)

We have
\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)+\partial_{x}\big(\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)u_{t}^{\star}(x)\big)=0
weakly (in the sense of distributions) on
(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
. More specifically, for any smooth, compactly supported function
\varphi:(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\partial_{t}\varphi(t,x)dxdt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)u_{t}^{\star}(x)\partial_{x}\varphi(t,x)dxdt=0.

(2)

The functions
\varrho^{\star}
and
u^{\star}
are smooth in
(t,x)
on the liquid region
\Omega
associated with
\varrho^{\star}
.

(3)

For each
(t,x)\in\Omega
, the pair
(u^{\star},\varrho^{\star})
satisfies

(3.17)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)+\partial_{x}\big(u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\big)=0;\qquad\partial_{t}\big(u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\big)+\partial_{x}\Big(u_{t}^{\star}(x)^{2}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)-\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)^{3}\Big)=0.

Although we will not make substantial use of it, the next lemma from
[
7
]
(we recall its quick proof in
Section

C.2
below) reformulates (
3.17
) as an equation for the associated
complex slope

f=f^{(\mu_{0};\mu_{1})}:(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{H}}
, defined by

(3.18)

\displaystyle f(t,x)=u_{t}^{\star}(x)+\pi\mathrm{i}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x).

Lemma 3.12

(
[
7
, Equation (2.16)]
)
.

Adopting the notation and assumptions of
3.11
, the associated complex slope
f
defined by (
3.18
) satisfies the complex Burgers equation,

(3.19)

\partial_{t}f(t,x)+f(t,x)\cdot\partial_{x}f(t,x)=0,\qquad\text{for all $(t,x)\in\Omega$}.

The following lemma expresses the derivatives of the height function (recall
Definition

3.4
) associated with non-intersecting Brownian bridges in terms of the
(u^{\star},\varrho^{\star})
above. The first statement of (
3.20
) follows from the definition (
3.3
) of the height function. Together with the equation
\partial_{t}\varrho^{\star}+\partial_{x}(u^{\star}\varrho^{\star})=0
, this formally implies the second statement of (
3.20
); its detailed proof is given in
Section

C.2
below.

Lemma 3.13
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
3.11
, and let
H^{\star}
denote the height function associated with
(\mu_{t}^{\star})
. Then
H^{\star}
is locally Lipschitz on
(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
and, for almost all
(t,x)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
(with respect to Lebesgue measure) we have

(3.20)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}H^{\star}(t,x)=-\varrho_{t}(x);\qquad\partial_{t}H^{\star}(t,x)=u_{t}(x)\varrho_{t}(x).

Remark 3.14
.

Using
3.5
, let us provide analogs of the above results when the parameters
(a,b)
and
A
in
3.1
are arbitrary. To that end, adopt the notation of
3.5
, and assume (analogously to in (
3.16
)) that
\mu_{a}
and
\mu_{b}
have densities
\varrho_{a}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
\varrho_{b}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with respect to Lebesgue measure, satisfying

(3.21)

\displaystyle\text{$\varrho_{a}$ and $\varrho_{b}$ are compactly supported};\qquad\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\varrho_{a}(x)\big|<\infty;\qquad\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\big|\varrho_{b}(x)\big|<\infty.

Letting
(\varrho_{t}^{\star})_{t\in[a,b]}
and
(\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in[0,1]}
denote the densities of
(\mu_{t}^{\star})_{t\in[a,b]}
and
(\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in[0,1]}
with respect to Lebesgue measure, respectively (guaranteed to exist by the first part of
3.7
), we have from the first statement of (
3.5
) that

(3.22)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\star}(x)=A^{-1/2}(b-a)^{1/2}\cdot\varrho_{a+(b-a)t}^{\star}\big(A^{1/2}(b-a)^{1/2}x\big),\qquad\text{for any $(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}$}.

By the second part of
3.7
, there exists
\widetilde{\nu}_{t}\in\mathscr{P}
with
\supp\widetilde{\nu}_{t}\subseteq\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{0}+\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{1}
and
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\star}=\widetilde{\nu}_{t}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t-t^{2})}
, for each
t\in(0,1)
. Hence, by
2.12
, the
\beta=b-a
case of
2.13
, and (
3.22
), for any
t\in(a,b)
there exists some
\nu_{t}\in\mathscr{P}_{\fin}
with
\nu_{t}(\mathbb{R})=A
, such that
\supp\nu_{t}\subseteq\supp\mu_{a}+\supp\mu_{b}
and
\mu_{t}^{\star}=\nu_{t}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{((t-a)(b-t)/(b-a))}
. This verifies that (the rescaled version) of the second part of
3.7
holds for
\mu_{t}^{\star}
.

Further setting (as in (
3.20
))

(3.23)

\displaystyle u_{t}^{\star}(x)=\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}H^{\star}(t,x)}{\varrho_{t}(x)},\quad\text{if $\varrho_{t}(x)>0$};\qquad\qquad\widetilde{u}_{t}^{\star}(x)=\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}_{t}^{\star}(x)}{\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\star}(x)},\quad\text{if $\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\star}(x)>0$},

we have from (
3.22
) and the second statement of (
3.5
) that

(3.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{t}^{\star}(x)=A^{-1/2}(b-a)^{1/2}\cdot u_{a+(b-a)t}^{\star}\big(A^{1/2}(b-a)^{1/2}x\big).

Defining (as in (
3.18
)) the functions
f(t,x)=u_{t}^{\star}(x)+\pi\mathrm{i}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)
and
\widetilde{f}(t,x)=\widetilde{u}_{t}^{\star}(x)+\pi\mathrm{i}\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\star}(x)
, we have from (
3.22
) and (
3.24
) that

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(t,x)=A^{-1/2}(b-a)^{1/2}\cdot f\big(a+(b-a)t,A^{1/2}(b-a)^{1/2}x\big).

Together with the fact that
\widetilde{f}
satisfies the complex Burgers equation (
3.19
) by
3.12
, this implies that
f
also satisfies (
3.19
) on the liquid region
\Omega
associated with
\varrho^{\star}
.

Remark 3.15
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
3.1
. Let
H^{\star}:[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
G^{\star}:[a,b]\times[0,A]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote the height and inverted height functions associated with
\bm{\mu}^{\star}
, respectively, and let
\varrho_{t}^{\star}
and
u_{t}^{\star}
be as in
3.11
(and
3.14
); denote the associated inverted liquid region (recall (
3.8
)) by
\Omega^{\inv}\subseteq(a,b)\times(0,A)
. By
Definition

3.4
, we have for
(t,y)\in\Omega^{\inv}
that
H^{\star}\big(t,G^{\star}(t,y)\big)=y
. Differentiating this equation in
y
or
t
, we find that (
3.20
) can equivalently be formulated in terms
G^{\star}
as

\displaystyle\varrho_{t}^{\star}\big(G^{\star}(t,y)\big)=-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\partial_{y}G^{\star}(t,y)},\quad\text{and}\quad u_{t}^{\star}\big(G^{\star}(t,y)\big)=\partial_{t}G^{\star}(t,y),\qquad\text{if $(t,y)\in\Omega^{\inv}$},

which defines
\varrho_{t}^{\star}
and
u_{t}^{\star}
everywhere on
\Omega
(since
G^{\star}
bijectively maps
\Omega^{\inv}
to
\Omega
).

3.3.
Elliptic Partial Differential Equations for the Height Function

In this section we reformulate (
3.17
) as elliptic partial differential equations satisfied by the height and inverted height functions associated with
\mu_{t}^{\star}
. The following lemma, which follows quickly from (
3.17
), implements this for the height function.

Lemma 3.16
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
3.11
, and let
H^{\star}
denote the height function associated with
(\mu_{t}^{\star})
. Recalling the liquid region
\Omega\subset(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
from (
3.8
), we have

(3.25)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in\{t,x\}}\mathfrak{b}_{ij}\big(\nabla H^{\star}(t,x)\big)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}H^{\star}(t,x)=0,\qquad\text{for any $(t,x)\in\Omega$},

where, for any
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have denoted

(3.26)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{tt}(u,v)=v^{2};\qquad\mathfrak{b}_{tx}(u,v)=-uv=\mathfrak{b}_{xt}(u,v);\qquad\mathfrak{b}_{xx}(u,v)=u^{2}+\pi^{2}v^{4}.

Proof.

Applying
3.13
and the second statement of (
3.17
) yields

(3.27)

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}H+\partial_{x}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}(\partial_{x}H)^{3}-\displaystyle\frac{(\partial_{t}H)^{2}}{\partial_{x}H}\bigg)=0,

or equivalently,

(3.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}H-\displaystyle\frac{2\partial_{t}H}{\partial_{x}H}\cdot\partial_{tx}H+\displaystyle\frac{(\partial_{t}H)^{2}}{(\partial_{x}H)^{2}}\cdot\partial_{xx}H+\pi^{2}(\partial_{x}H)^{2}\cdot\partial_{xx}H=0.

Multiplying this equation by
(\partial_{x}H)^{2}
then yields (
3.25
).
∎

Remark 3.17
.

The derivation of (
3.27
) does not require smoothness of
u^{\star}
and
\varrho^{\star}
. In particular, if one were to only assume that the second equation in (
3.17
) holds weakly (in the sense of distributions) on
\Omega
(as was shown in
[
35
, Property 2.9(4)]
), the proof of
3.16
shows that
H
is a weak solution to the divergence-form ellliptic partial differential equation (
3.27
).

The next lemma, which follows from changing variables in
3.16
, shows that the inverted height function
G^{\star}
satisfies (
1.2
); in view of
3.8
, this implies that the limiting trajectories of paths in a family of non-intersecting Brownian motions (without north or south boundary) is govered by solutions of (
1.2
); in the below, we recall the open set
\Omega^{\inv}
from (
3.8
).

Lemma 3.18
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
3.11
, and let
G^{\star}
denote the inverted height function associated with
(\mu_{t}^{\star})
. Then
G^{\star}
satisfies the equation (
1.2
) for
(t,y)\in\Omega^{\inv}
.

Proof.

Let
(t,y)\in\Omega^{\inv}
, and set
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
H(t,x)=y
; then,
(t,x)\in\Omega
. Differentiating the equation
G\big(t,H(t,x)\big)=x
, it is quickly verified that

\displaystyle\partial_{y}G=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\partial_{x}H};\qquad

\displaystyle\partial_{t}G=-\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}H}{\partial_{x}H};\qquad\partial_{yy}G=-\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{xx}H}{(\partial_{x}H)^{3}};\qquad\partial_{ty}G=\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}H}{(\partial_{x}H)^{3}}\cdot\partial_{xx}H-\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{tx}H}{(\partial_{x}H)^{2}};

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{tt}G=\displaystyle\frac{2\partial_{t}H}{(\partial_{x}H)^{2}}\cdot\partial_{tx}H-\displaystyle\frac{(\partial_{t}H)^{2}}{(\partial_{x}H)^{3}}\cdot\partial_{xx}H-\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{tt}H}{\partial_{x}H}.

where in the above the arguments of
H
and
G
(and their derivatives) are
(t,x)
and
(t,y)
, respectively. Dividing (
3.28
) by
\partial_{x}H
and applying the above equalities yields

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}G+\pi^{2}(\partial_{y}G)^{-4}\partial_{yy}G

\displaystyle=\partial_{tt}G-\pi^{2}(\partial_{x}H)\partial_{xx}H

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{2\partial_{t}H}{(\partial_{x}H)^{2}}\cdot\partial_{tx}H-\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{tt}H}{\partial_{x}H}-\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{(\partial_{t}H)^{2}}{(\partial_{x}H)^{3}}+\pi^{2}\partial_{x}H\bigg)\cdot\partial_{xx}H=0,

which establishes the lemma.
∎

Remark 3.19
.

The regularity properties of solutions to (
3.25
) and (
1.2
) depend on the
2\times 2
matrices

\displaystyle\bm{B}=\bm{B}(u,v)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathfrak{b}_{tt}(u,v)&\mathfrak{b}_{tx}(u,v)\\
\mathfrak{b}_{xt}(u,v)&\mathfrak{b}_{tt}(u,v)\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}v^{2}&-uv\\
-uv&u^{2}+\pi^{2}v^{4}\end{array}\right],\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\bm{D}=\bm{D}(u,v)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathfrak{d}_{tt}(u,v)&\mathfrak{d}_{ty}(u,v)\\
\mathfrak{d}_{yt}(u,v)&\mathfrak{d}_{yy}(u,v)\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&\pi^{2}v^{-4}\end{array}\right],

respectively; both are nonnegative definite. The two eigenvalues of
\bm{B}
are bounded above and below whenever
|u|
is bounded above and
v
is bounded above and below; the two eigenvalues of
\bm{D}
are bounded above and below whenever
v
is. In particular, that the latter only requires bounds on
v=\partial_{y}G^{\star}=(\partial_{x}H^{\star})^{-1}
, while the former requires those on both
\partial_{x}H^{\star}
and
\partial_{t}H^{\star}
.

Further regularity properties of solutions to (
1.2
) are provided in
Section

9
below.

Remark 3.20
.

Formally, one can interpret (
3.25
) and (
1.2
) as Euler–Lagrange equations for minimizers of the functionals

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}(F)=-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\big(\partial_{t}F(t,x)\big)^{2}}{\partial_{x}F(t,x)}+\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\big(\partial_{x}F(t,x)\big)^{3}\bigg)dxdt,\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{\inv}(F)=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\big(\partial_{t}F(t,x)\big)^{2}+\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}\big(\partial_{x}F(t,x)\big)^{-2}\Big)dxdt,\qquad\text{respectively}.

However, we will not make this fully precise, as we will not require this interpretation.

It will be useful to make use of invariances of the equations (
3.25
) and (
1.2
) under the below (linear and multiplicative) transformations.

Lemma 3.21
.

Fix a bounded, open subset
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and two functions
G,H\in\Adm(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})
; assume on
\mathfrak{R}
that
H
satisfies (
3.25
) and that
G
satisfies (
1.2
). Fix real numbers
\alpha,\beta>0
, and denote the open sets
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha;\beta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}=\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{\alpha}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}=\big\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle:(\alpha t,\beta x)\in\mathfrak{R}\big\};\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}=\big\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(t,x-\alpha t)\in\mathfrak{R}\big\}.

(1)

(a)

Define
\widetilde{H}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}})
by
\widetilde{H}(t,x)=\alpha\beta^{-2}H(\alpha t,\beta x)
. Then
\widetilde{H}
satisfies (
3.25
) on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}
.

(b)

Define
\widehat{H}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})
by
\widehat{H}(t,x)=H(t,x-\alpha t)
. Then,
\widehat{H}
satisfies (
3.25
) on
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}
.

(2)

(a)

Define
\widetilde{G}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}})
by
\widetilde{G}(t,y)=(\alpha\beta)^{-1/2}G(\alpha t,\beta y)
. Then
\widetilde{G}
satisfies (
1.2
) on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}
.

(b)

Define
\widehat{G}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})
by
\widehat{G}(t,y)=G(t,y)+\alpha t
. Then,
\widehat{G}
satisfies (
1.2
) on
\mathfrak{R}
.

Proof.

We only show the first part of the first statement of the lemma, as the proofs of the remaining ones are entirely analogous. To that end, fix
(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}
and set
(t,x)=(\alpha\widetilde{t},\beta\widetilde{x})\in\mathfrak{R}
. Then,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\partial_{t}\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})=\alpha^{2}\beta^{-2}\partial_{t}H(t,x);\qquad\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})=\alpha\beta^{-1}\partial_{x}H(t,x);

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})=\alpha^{3}\beta^{-2}\partial_{tt}H(t,x);\quad\partial_{tx}\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})=\alpha^{2}\beta^{-1}\partial_{tx}H(t,x);\quad\partial_{xx}\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})=\alpha\partial_{xx}H(t,x).

It follows from (
3.25
) and (
3.26
) that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in\{t,x\}}\mathfrak{b}_{ij}\big(\nabla\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})\big)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\widetilde{H}(\widetilde{t},\widetilde{x})=\alpha^{5}\beta^{-4}\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in\{t,x\}}\mathfrak{b}_{ij}\big(\nabla H(t,x)\big)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}H(t,x)=0,

verifying that
\widetilde{H}
satisfies (
3.25
) on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}
.
∎

Let us mention that, while the scalings provided by the
\alpha=\beta
cases of parts (
1a
) and (
2a
) of
3.21
are satisfied by any equation of the general form (
3.25
) (only assuming that the
\mathfrak{b}_{ij}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{ij}
are measurable), those provided by general
(\alpha,\beta)
are special to the explicit forms of the coefficients (
3.26
) and (
1.3
).

Remark 3.22
.

Let us extend the above statements to the case of general parameters
a<b
and
A>0
. Adopt the notation and assumptions of
3.14
. Since
\widetilde{H}^{\star}
satisfies (
3.25
), the second statement of (
3.5
) and the
(\alpha,\beta)=\big((b-a)^{-1},A^{-1/2}(b-a)^{-1/2}\big)
case of part (
1a
) of
3.21
implies that
H^{\star}
also satisfies (
3.25
) on the liquid region
\Omega
associated with
(\varrho_{t}^{\star})
. Similarly, since
\widetilde{G}^{\star}
satisfies (
1.2
), the third statement of (
3.5
) and the
(\alpha,\beta)=\big((b-a)^{-1},A^{-1})
case of part (
2a
) of
3.21
implies that
G^{\star}
satisfies (
1.2
) on the inverted liquid region
\Omega^{\inv}
associated with
(\varrho_{t}^{\star})
.

We conclude this section by mentioning that, as stated, the results of
Section

3.2
and
Section

3.3
largely imposed the assumption (
3.21
) (due to (
3.16
)). However, the following lemma indicates that many of these results continue to hold if we only assume that the boundary measures
\mu_{a}
and
\mu_{b}
are compactly supported (without imposing that they admit a bounded density, as in (
3.21
)). The proof is given in
Section

C.2
below, by combining the results of
Section

3.2
and
Section

3.3
with
3.7
and
3.3
(to verify (
3.21
) in the interior of the domain).

Lemma 3.23
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
3.1
, and assume that
\mu_{a}
and
\mu_{b}
are compactly supported. Denote the density process associated with
\bm{\mu}
by
(\varrho_{t}^{\star})_{t\in(a,b)}
; denote the associated height and inverted height functions by
H^{\star}:[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
G^{\star}:[a,b]\times[0,A]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, respectively; and denote the associated liquid and inverted liquid regions by
\Omega\subset(a,b)\times\mathbb{R}
and
\Omega^{\inv}\subseteq(a,b)\times(0,A)
, respectively. Then, the following statements hold.

(1)

The function
H^{\star}(t,x)
is smooth for
(t,x)\in\Omega
; moreover,
G^{\star}(t,y)
is smooth for
(t,y)\in\Omega^{\inv}
.

(2)

Defining
u^{\star}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as in
3.15
and the complex slope
f:\Omega\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{H}}
as in (
3.18
),
f
satisfies (
3.19
) on
\Omega
.

(3)

The function
H^{\star}
satisfies (
3.25
) on
\Omega
, and
G^{\star}
satisfies (
1.2
) on
\Omega^{\inv}
.

4.
Proof of the Concentration Estimate

In this section we reduce
1.4
to
4.4
below, which is an inductive statement that bounds the height function associated with the family
\bm{x}
of non-intersecting Brownian motion by a sequence of barrier functions.

4.1.
Proof of
1.4

The proof of
1.4
will proceed by first establishing the following variant of it for the associated height function instead of for the path trajectories
x_{j}(t)
. We begin with the following definition for a certain class of open sets.

Definition 4.1
.

For any real numbers
a<b
and functions
f,g:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
f<g
, we define the open set
\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}=\big\{(t,x)\in(a,b)\times\mathbb{R}:f(t)<x<g(t)\big\}.

We call any open set of the form
\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
for some
f,g:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
f<g
a
strip domain
. The
width
of
\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
is defined to be
b-a
.

Observe in particular that the non-intersecting Brownian motions from
Definition

1.1
all lie within the strip domain
\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
.

The following proposition, which quickly implies
1.4
, is established in
Section

4.2
below. In what follows we recall that any function
G:[a,b]\times[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, that is nonincreasing in its second coordinate, is associated with a unique height function
H:[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
through (
3.4
), namely, by setting
H(t,x)=\displaystyle\inf\big\{y\in[0,1]:G(t,y)\geq x\big\}
. We also recall the height function
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}
associated with a family
\bm{x}
of non-intersecting curves from (
3.6
).

Proposition 4.2
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
1.4
; assume that
\varkappa=0
and that
G(0,0)=0
. There exist constants
c=c(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>0
and
C=C(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that the following holds. Letting
H:[0,L^{-1}]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote the height function associated with
G
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\big|n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)-H(z)\big|>Cn^{2/m+\delta-1}\bigg]<Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}}.

In the below, we will use the fact that under the notation of
4.2
we have

(4.1)

\displaystyle-\varepsilon^{-1}<\partial_{x}H(t,x)<-\varepsilon,\qquad\text{for each $(t,x)\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}$},

which follows from (
3.4
) and the fact that
G\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})
(recall
1.3
). Also observe that there exists a constant
B=B(\varepsilon,B_{0},m)>1
such that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\mathfrak{P}_{f;g})}<B,

which follows from (
3.4
) and the facts that
G\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})
(so
H\big(t,G(t,y)\big)=y
for each
(t,y)
such that
G(t,y)\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
) and that
\|G\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq B_{0}
.

Proof of
1.4
.

We may assume by shifting in what follows that
G(0,0)=0
. Let us first address the case when
\varkappa=0
. By
4.2
, it suffices to show for any real number
C>1
the inclusions of events

(4.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Bigg\{\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1/L]}\big|x_{j}(t)-G(&t,jn^{-1})\big|\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}n^{2/m+\delta-1}\Bigg\}\\
&\subseteq\Bigg\{\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\big|n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)-H(z)\big|\leq Cn^{2/m+\delta-1}\Bigg\}.\end{aligned}

To that end, we begin by restricting to the event on the right side of (
4.3
). Fix
t_{0}\in(0,L^{-1})
, and apply that event twice, first with
z=(t_{0},y_{1})
and then with
z=(t_{0},y_{2})
, where
y_{1}=G\big(t_{0},jn^{-1}+Cn^{2/m+\delta-1}\big)
and
y_{2}=G\big(t_{0},jn^{-1}-Cn^{2/m+\delta-1}\big)
. Since (
3.4
) and the fact that
G\in\Adm(\mathfrak{R})
imply
H\big(t_{0},G(t_{0},y)\big)=y
for each
y\in(0,1)
, this yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}\big(t_{0},G(t_{0},jn^{-1}-Cn^{2/m+\delta-1})\big)\leq j\leq\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}\big(t_{0},G(t_{0},jn^{-1}+Cn^{2/m+\delta-1})\big),

and hence

\displaystyle G\big(t_{0},jn^{-1}+Cn^{2/m+\delta-1}\big)\leq x_{j}(t_{0})\leq G\big(t_{0},jn^{-1}-Cn^{2/m+\delta-1}\big).

Together with the fact that
G\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})
, this yields

\displaystyle G(t_{0},jn^{-1})-C\varepsilon^{-1}n^{2/m+\delta-1}

\displaystyle\leq x_{j}(t_{0})\leq G(t_{0},jn^{-1})+C\varepsilon^{-1}n^{2/m+\delta-1},

confirming (
4.3
). This establishes the theorem if
\varkappa=0
.

If
\varkappa\neq 0
, then define the
n
-tuples
\bm{u}^{-},\bm{u}^{+},\bm{v}^{-},\bm{v}^{+}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
and the functions
f^{-},f^{+},g^{-},g^{+}:[0,L^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting

\displaystyle u_{j}^{\pm}=G(0,jn^{-1})\pm\varkappa;\quad v_{j}^{\pm}=G(L^{-1};jn^{-1})\pm\varkappa;\quad f^{\pm}(s)=f(s)\pm\varkappa;\qquad g^{\pm}(s)=g(s)\pm\varkappa,

for each index
\pm\in\{+,-\}
and pair
(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,L^{-1}]
. Sample non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{x}^{-}=(x_{1}^{-},x_{2}^{-},\ldots,x_{n}^{-})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,L^{-1}]\big)
and
\bm{x}^{+}=(x_{1}^{+},x_{2}^{+},\ldots,x_{n}^{+})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,L^{-1}]\big)
under the measures
\mathfrak{Q}_{f^{-};g^{-}}^{\bm{u}^{-};\bm{v}^{-}}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{f^{+};g^{+}}^{\bm{u}^{+};\bm{v}^{+}}
, respectively. Observe that
\bm{u}^{-}\leq\bm{u}\leq\bm{u}^{+}
and
\bm{v}^{-}\leq\bm{v}\leq\bm{v}^{+}
by (
1.4
), and that
f^{-}\leq f\leq f^{+}
and
g^{-}\leq g\leq g^{+}
. Thus, by
2.3
, we may couple
(\bm{x}^{-},\bm{x},\bm{x}^{+})
such that

(4.4)

\displaystyle x_{j}^{-}(s)\leq x_{j}(s)\leq x_{j}^{+}(s),\qquad\text{for each $(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,L^{-1}]$.}

Then,
1.4
applies to both
\bm{x}^{-}
and
\bm{x}^{+}
, with the
G
there given by
G^{-}=G-\varkappa
and
G^{+}=G+\varkappa
here, respectively. It yields a constant
c=c(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>0
such that, on an event of probability at least
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log n)^{2}}
, we have
x_{j}^{-}(s)\geq G^{-}(s,jn^{-1})-n^{2/m+\delta-1}=G(s,jn^{-1})-\varkappa-n^{2/m+\delta-1}
and similarly
x_{j}^{+}\leq G(s,jn^{-1})+\varkappa+n^{2/m+\delta-1}
. This, with (
4.4
) and a union bound, yields the theorem.
∎

To establish
4.2
, we make use of the following initial estimate that provides a high-probability
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder bound, with associated constant
n^{\delta/2}
, for the path trajectories
x_{j}(t)
from
4.2
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
1.4
; assume that
\varkappa=0
. There exists a constant
C=C(\delta,B_{0})>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t<t+s\leq 1/L}\big|x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\big|\leq n^{\delta/2}s^{1/2}+n^{-5}\bigg]\leq Ce^{-n}.

Proof.

First observe by
1.3
that
\big|f^{\prime}(s)\big|+\big|g^{\prime}(s)\big|\leq 2\big\|G-G(0,0)\big\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq 2B_{0}
, for each
s\in(0,L^{-1})
. Thus we may apply the second part of
2.7
with the
(A,B,T)
there equal to
(2B_{0},C_{1},L^{-1})
here, for some sufficiently large constant
C_{1}>1
. Since
L\leq n^{\delta}
, this yields some constant
C_{2}>1
such that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\bigcup_{a\leq t<t+s\leq b}\bigg\{\big|x_{j}(t+s&)-x_{j}(t)-sL(v_{j}-u_{j})\big|\geq C_{2}s^{1/2}\big(|\log 9s^{-1}n^{\delta}|+B_{0}\big)^{2}\bigg\}\Bigg]\leq C_{2}e^{-n}.\end{aligned}

Since
\delta<\frac{1}{80}
, by
1.3
, there is a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(B_{0})>1
such that for
n>C_{3}
we have

\displaystyle C_{2}s^{1/2}\big(\log|9s^{-1}n^{\delta}|+B_{0}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}B_{0}^{2}s^{1/2}\Big((\log n^{21}+1)^{2}\cdot\textbf{1}_{s\geq n^{-20}}+\big(\log|sn|+1\big)^{2}\cdot\textbf{1}_{s\leq n^{-20}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq n^{\delta/4}s^{1/2}+n^{-5}.

Inserting this into (
4.5
), and using the facts that
s\leq L^{-1}\leq B_{0}
, that
|v_{j}-u_{j}|L\leq 2\big\|G-G(0,0)\big\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq 2B_{0}
(by
1.3
), and that
n
is sufficiently large, yields the lemma.
∎

4.2.
Proof of
4.2

In this section we establish
4.2
assuming
4.4
below. Define the real numbers
\rho,\omega>0
; fix a real number
\zeta
; and define the function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
so that

(4.6)

\displaystyle\rho=n^{\delta-1/m};\qquad\omega=n^{2/m+\delta-1};\qquad\zeta>20000(B^{5}+\varepsilon^{-5});\qquad\psi(t,x)=\Upsilon-e^{\zeta t}-e^{\zeta x},

where
\Upsilon>0
is chosen so that
\inf_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\psi(z)=2
. Set
J=\lfloor 3\omega^{-1}\rfloor-1
, and let
K
be the smallest integer so that
(1-K\rho^{2}n^{-\delta})\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\psi(z)\leq\frac{1}{4}
. Analogously to in
[
52
, Equation (52)]
, for any integers
j\in\llbracket 0,J\rrbracket
and real number (typically an integer)
k\in[0,K]
, define the functions
\varphi_{j;k}^{+},\varphi_{j;k}^{-}:\overline{\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by for
z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
setting

(4.7)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\varphi_{j;k}^{+}(z)=H(z)-(j+1)\omega+(1-k\rho^{2}n^{-\delta})\omega\psi(z)+3;\\
&\varphi_{j;k}^{-}(z)=H(z)+(j+1)\omega-(1-k\rho^{2}n^{-\delta})\omega\psi(z)-3,\end{aligned}

and by for
z\in\partial\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
setting
\varphi_{j;k}^{-}(z)=H(z)
and
\varphi_{j;k}^{+}(z)=H(z)
. Further define the events

\displaystyle\Omega_{j;k}^{+}(z)=\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)>\varphi_{j;k}^{+}(z)\big\};\qquad\Omega_{j;k}^{-}(z)=\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)<\varphi_{j;k}^{-}(z)\big\}.

The following proposition indicates that if
H
lies below (or above)
\varphi_{j;k-1}^{+}
(or
\varphi_{;k-1}^{-}
, respectively), then with high probability it also lies below (or above)
\varphi_{j;k}^{+}
(or
\varphi_{j;k}^{-}
, respectively); it will be established in
Section

6.2
below. Here, we denote the complement of any event
\mathscr{E}
by
\mathscr{E}^{\complement}
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
4.2
. For any index
\pm\in\{+,-\}
, integer pair
(j,k)\in\llbracket 0,J\rrbracket\times[0,K]
, and point
z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\Omega_{j;k}^{\pm}(z)\cap\bigcap_{w\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{j;k-1}^{\pm}(w)^{\complement}\Bigg]\leq Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}}.

Given
4.4
, we can quickly establish
4.2
.

Proof of
4.2
.

It suffices to show that

(4.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)\geq H(z)+2n^{2/m+\delta-1}\big\}\Bigg]<Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}};\\
&\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)\leq H(z)-2n^{2/m+\delta-1}\big\}\Bigg]<Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}}.\end{aligned}

We only establish the first bound in (
4.8
), for the proof of the latter is entirely analogous. To that end, for any integer
j\in\llbracket 0,J\rrbracket
, define the function
\Phi_{j}:\overline{\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{j}(z)=H(z)-j\omega+3.

and for any
z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
define the event

\displaystyle\Omega_{j}(z)=\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)>\Phi_{j}(z)\big\}.

Then, observe from (
4.7
) and (
4.6
) that

(4.9)

\displaystyle\varphi_{j;K}^{+}(z)\leq\Phi_{j}(z)\leq\Phi_{j-1}(z)\leq\varphi_{j;0}^{+}(z),

where the first bound follows from the fact that
(1-K\rho^{2}n^{-\delta})\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\psi(z)\leq\frac{1}{4}
; the second from the definition of
\Phi_{j}
; and the third from the fact that
\inf_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\psi(z)=2
.

Define the set
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
consisting of all points
(t,x)\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
such that
t\in n^{-15}\cdot\mathbb{Z}
and
x=G(t,y)
, for some
y\in n^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{Z}
; then,
|\mathcal{S}|\leq n^{20}
. Taking a union over
z\in\mathcal{S}
of
4.4
yields constants
c_{1}=c_{1}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>0
and
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
satisfying

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\bigcap_{w\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{j;k-1}^{+}(w)^{\complement}\setminus\mathscr{E}_{j;k}\right]\leq C_{1}e^{-c_{1}(\log n)^{2}},

where

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{j;k}=\bigcap_{z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\Omega_{j;k}^{+}(z^{\prime})^{\complement}\cap\bigcap_{w\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{j;k-1}^{+}(w)^{\complement}.

By
4.3
, there also exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\delta,B_{0})>1
such that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{F}]\geq 1-C_{2}e^{-n},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathscr{F}=\Bigg\{\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\displaystyle\sup_{0<t<t+s\leq 1/L}\big|x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\big|\leq n^{\delta/2}s^{1/2}+n^{-5}\Bigg\}.

Next, for any
j\in\llbracket 0,J\rrbracket
,
k\in[0,K]
,
s\in[0,L^{-1}]
, and
r\geq 0
, let

\displaystyle\gamma_{j;k}^{+}(r;s)=\sup\big\{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}:\varphi_{j;k}^{+}(s,\gamma)\geq r\big\}.

Fix
(i,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,L^{-1}]
, and let
s^{\prime}\in[0,L^{-1}]
be such that
\big(s^{\prime},G(s^{\prime},in^{-1})\big)\in\mathcal{S}
and
|s-s^{\prime}|
is minimal; then,
|s-s^{\prime}|\leq n^{-15}
. On the event
\mathscr{E}_{j;k}\cap\mathscr{F}
, we have for sufficiently large
n
that

\displaystyle x_{i}(s)\leq x_{i}(s^{\prime})+2n^{-5}\leq\gamma_{j;k}^{+}(in^{-1};s^{\prime})+2n^{-5}<\gamma_{j;k}^{+}(in^{-1};s)+4n^{-5}<\gamma_{j;k}^{+}(in^{-1}+n^{-4};s).

Here, the first inequality follows from restricting to
\mathscr{F}
; the second follows from restricting to
\mathscr{E}_{j;k}
; and the third and fourth follow from the facts that
\varphi_{j;k}^{+}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\mathfrak{P}_{f;g})
, that
\|\varphi_{j;k}^{+}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{P}_{f;g})}\leq C_{3}
for some constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,\delta,B,m)>1
, and that
n
sufficiently large (where these bounds follow from (
4.7
), since
H\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{P}_{f;g})
by (
4.1
),
\|H\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{P}_{f;g})}\leq B
by (
4.2
),
\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{P}_{f;g})}\leq 10\zeta e^{\zeta}
, and
\omega=n^{2/m+\delta-1}
). Since this holds for each
(i,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,L^{-1}]
, it follows on
\mathscr{E}_{j;k}\cap\mathscr{F}
that

\displaystyle n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)\leq\varphi_{j;k}^{+}(z)+n^{-4}<\varphi_{j;k-1/2}^{+}(z),\qquad\text{for each $z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}$, on the event $\mathscr{E}_{j;k}\cap\mathscr{F}$}.

Applying this bound (decreasing
k
by
\frac{1}{2}
at each point)
2K+2\leq 2n
times, and using (
4.10
) and (
4.11
), yields constants
c_{2}=c_{2}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>0
and
C_{4}=C_{4}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)>\varphi_{j;K}^{+}(z)\big\}\cap\bigcap_{w\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{j;0}^{+}(w)^{\complement}\Bigg]\leq C_{4}e^{-c_{2}(\log n)^{2}}.

By (
4.9
), we deduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{j}(z)\cap\bigcap_{w\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{j-1}(z)^{\complement}\Bigg]\leq C_{4}e^{-c_{2}(\log n)^{3}},

which upon applying
J\leq n
times implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{J}(z)\cap\bigcap_{w\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\Omega_{0}(z)^{\complement}\Bigg]\leq C_{4}ne^{-c_{2}(\log n)^{2}}.

With a union bound and the facts that
\Phi_{J}(z)\leq H(z)+2\omega
(since
J=\lfloor 3\omega^{-1}\rfloor-1
) and
\Phi_{0}(z)\leq H(z)+3
, this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)\geq H(z)

\displaystyle+2n^{2/m+\delta-1}\big\}\Bigg]

\displaystyle<\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}}\big\{n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)\geq H(z)+3\}\Bigg]+C_{4}ne^{-c_{2}(\log n)^{2}}.

Since
n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)\leq 1\leq H(z)+3
holds deterministically, this yields the first bound in (
4.8
) and thus the proposition.
∎

5.
The Local Behaviors of Height Profiles

In this section we explain how to approximate the first several derivatives (which we refer to as a “local profile”) of a limit shape by another one, associated with a family of non-intersecting Brownian bridges with known concentration bounds.

5.1.
Local Profiles

In this section we introduce the notion of a local profile, which constitutes information about the first several derivatives of a function. To that end, for any integer
k\geq 1
; open subset
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
; and function
F:\mathcal{C}^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
, and point
z\in\mathfrak{R}
, define the
k
-th order local profile

\bm{q}_{F}^{(k)}(z)\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
by

(5.1)

\displaystyle\bm{q}_{F}^{(k)}(z)=\big(\partial_{x}^{k}F(z),\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{k-1}F(z),\ldots,\partial_{t}^{k}F(z)\big).

For any integer
m\geq 1
, the
full-
m
local profile
is the sequence of vectors
\bm{Q}_{F}^{(m)}(z)=\big(\bm{q}_{F}^{(k)}(z)\big)_{k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket}
.

We next describe two properties that a full-
m
local profile
\bm{Q}_{F}^{(m)}
must satisfy, if some admissible
F
solves the equation (
3.25
) on some open subset
\mathfrak{R}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The first is that the second coordinate of
\bm{q}_{F}^{(1)}
must be negative, due to the admissibility of
F
. The second is that
F
must satisfy (
3.25
), which imposes a consistency relation between
\bm{q}_{F}^{(1)}
and
\bm{q}_{F}^{(2)}
. More generally, applying the differential operator
\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}
to (
3.25
) imposes a relation between
\big(\bm{q}_{F}^{(1)},\bm{q}_{F}^{(2)},\ldots,\bm{q}_{F}^{(i+j+2)}\big)
, for each
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
such that
i+j\leq m-2
. These admissibility and consistency properties are made precise through the following definition. Throughout, unless mentioned otherwise, we will denote sequences of
m
vectors
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)_{\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
by
\bm{q}^{(k)}=\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,q_{k}^{(k)}\big)
, for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.

Definition 5.1
.

A sequence of
m
vectors
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
is
admissible
if
q_{0}^{(1)}<0
. For any integers
i,j\geq 0
, define the function
\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{i+j+3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
so that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\bm{Q}_{F}^{(i+j+2)}(z)\big)=\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{t,x\}}\mathfrak{b}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\partial_{i^{\prime}}\partial_{j^{\prime}}F(z)\Bigg).

We say that the sequence
\bm{Q}
is
consistent
if it is admissible and

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)},\bm{q}^{(2)},\ldots,\bm{q}^{(i+j+2)}\big)=0,\qquad\text{for each $(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}$ with $i+j\leq m-2$}.

Example 5.2
.

Since

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{1,0}\big(\bm{Q}_{F}^{(3)}(z)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\partial_{ttt}F(z)+2\mathfrak{b}_{tx}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\partial_{ttx}F(z)+\mathfrak{b}_{xx}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\partial_{txx}F(z)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(\partial_{tt}F(z)\partial_{u}\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)+\partial_{tx}F(z)\partial_{v}\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\Big)\partial_{tt}F(z)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\Big(\partial_{tt}F(z)\partial_{u}\mathfrak{b}_{tx}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)+\partial_{tx}F(z)\partial_{v}\mathfrak{b}_{tx}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\Big)\partial_{tx}F(z)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(\partial_{tt}F(z)\partial_{u}\mathfrak{b}_{xx}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)+\partial_{tx}F(z)\partial_{v}\mathfrak{b}_{xx}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\Big)\partial_{xx}F(z),

we have, for any sequence
\bm{Q}^{(3)}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)_{k\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket}
with
\bm{q}^{(k)}=\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,q_{k}^{(k)}\big)
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,3\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{1,0}\big(\bm{Q}^{(3)}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)q_{3}^{(3)}+2\mathfrak{b}_{tx}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)q_{2}^{(3)}+\mathfrak{b}_{xx}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)q_{1}^{(3)}+\Big(q_{2}^{(2)}\partial_{u}\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)+q_{1}^{(2)}\partial_{v}\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)\Big)q_{2}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\quad+2\Big(q_{2}^{(2)}\partial_{u}\mathfrak{b}_{tx}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)+q_{1}^{(2)}\partial_{v}\mathfrak{b}_{tx}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)\Big)q_{1}^{(2)}+\Big(q_{2}^{(2)}\partial_{u}\mathfrak{b}_{xx}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)+q_{1}^{(2)}\partial_{v}\mathfrak{b}_{xx}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)\Big)q_{0}^{(2)}.

Remark 5.3
.

For any function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})\cap\Adm(\mathfrak{R})
satisfying (
3.25
), the sequence
\bm{Q}_{F}^{(m)}(z)
is consistent, for each integer
m\geq 1
and point
z\in\mathfrak{R}
; this follows from applying
\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}
to (
3.25
) (for each
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
with
i+j\leq m+2)
.

Observe that consistency imposes
\binom{m}{2}
relations (one for each
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
i+j\leq m-2
) between the
\binom{m+2}{2}-1
coordinates of
\bm{Q}
; this suggests that there are
\binom{m+2}{2}-1-\binom{m}{2}=2m
“free parameters” describing a consistent
\bm{Q}
. The following lemma indicates this to indeed be the case, taking these
2m
parameters to be the first two coordinates
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)_{k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket}
of each vector in
\bm{Q}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix an integer
m\geq 1
and
m
pairs of real numbers
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
with
q_{0}^{(1)}<0
.

(1)

There is a unique consistent sequence of
m
vectors
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
so that, for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, the first two coordinates of
\bm{q}^{(k)}
are
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)
.

(2)

For any real numbers
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
B>1
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
so that, if

(5.3)

\displaystyle q_{0}^{(1)}\leq-\varepsilon,\quad\text{and}\quad\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\Big(\big|q_{0}^{(k)}\big|+\big|q_{1}^{(k)}\big|\Big)\leq B,

then the consistent sequence
\bm{Q}
sastisfies
\big|q_{j}^{(k)}\big|\leq C
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
.

Proof.

Observe for any
(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in\{t,x\}}\mathfrak{b}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\partial_{i^{\prime}}\partial_{j^{\prime}}F(z)\Bigg)=\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\partial_{t}^{i+2}\partial_{x}^{j}F(z)+\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(\partial_{t}^{i^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{j^{\prime}}F(z)\big)_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\Big),

for some function
\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}
that depends polynomially (as the
\mathfrak{b}_{ij}(u,v)
from (
3.26
) are polynomials in
(u,v)
) only on those derivatives
\partial_{t}^{i^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{j^{\prime}}F(z)
such that either
i^{\prime}\leq i+1
or
i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}<i+j+2
. As such, for any sequence of
m
vectors
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(1)},\bm{q}^{(2)},\ldots,\bm{q}^{(m)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, we have

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)},\bm{q}^{(2)},\ldots,\bm{q}^{(i+j+2)}\big)=q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)+\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k}\Big),

where again
\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}
is a polynomial in those
q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}
with either
i^{\prime}\leq i+1
or
k^{\prime}\leq i+j+1
. Thus,

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)},\bm{q}^{(2)},\ldots,\bm{q}^{(i+j+2)}\big)=0,\quad\text{if and only if}\quad q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}=-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\Big)}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)}.

Hence since
q_{0}^{(1)}<0
, meaning
\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)>0
, the consistency of
\bm{Q}
provides a unique solution for
q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}
, in terms of all
\big(q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)
such that either
i^{\prime}\leq i+1
or
k^{\prime}\leq i+j+1
. In particular, given the pairs
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)
, this by induction on the lexicographic pair
(i+j+2,i+2)
determines a unique consistent solution for the remaining entries of
\bm{Q}
, establishing the first statement of the lemma.

To establish the second, observe from (
3.26
) that
\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)\geq\varepsilon^{2}
, as
q_{0}^{(1)}\leq-\varepsilon
. Thus, since the
\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}
are polynomials with coefficients and degrees only dependent on
i,j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, it follows by (
5.5
)  (
5.3
), and induction on the lexicographic pair
(i+j+2,i+2)\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket^{2}
that for each
(i,j)\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket^{2}
there exists a constant
C_{i,j}=C_{i,j}(\varepsilon,B)>1
such that
\big|q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}\big|\leq C_{i,j}
. This (together with the fact from (
5.3
) that
\big|q_{0}^{(k)}\big|+\big|q_{1}^{(k)}\big|\leq B
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
) yields the second statement of the lemma, with
C=\max_{i+j\leq m-2}C_{i,j}+B
.
∎

Definition 5.5
.

Adopting the notation of
5.4
, we call
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)
the
consistent extension
of the sequence of
m
pairs
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
.

We conclude this section with the following two lemmas. The first is a continuity bound, saying that if two sequences of real pairs are close, then their consistent extensions are as well. The second implies that, if
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}
is a sequence that “almost satisfies” the consistency relations (
5.2
), then there is a consistent sequence
\bm{Q}
close to it.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
and real numbers
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
B>1
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that the following holds. Let
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
and
\big(\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(k)},\widetilde{q}_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
be two sequences of
m
real number pairs such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max\big\{q_{0}^{(1)},\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(1)}\}\leq-\varepsilon;\qquad\displaystyle\max_{1\leq k\leq m}\Big(\big|q_{0}^{(k)}\big|+\big|q_{1}^{(k)}\big|+\big|\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(k)}\big|+\big|\widetilde{q}_{1}^{(k)}\big|\Big)\leq B.

Letting
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)
and
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}=\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(k)}\big)
denote the consistent extensions of
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)
and
\big(\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(k)},\widetilde{q}_{1}^{(k)}\big)
, respectively, we have

\displaystyle\big\|\bm{q}^{(k)}-\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(k)}\big\|\leq C\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\Big(\big|q_{0}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|+\big|q_{1}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{1}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|\Big),\qquad\text{for each $k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket$}.

Proof.

Throughout, we recall the notation from the proof of
5.4
. First observe from the second part of
5.4
that there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that

(5.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}\Big(\big|\bm{q}^{(k)}\big|+\big|\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(k)}\big|\Big)\leq C_{1}.

Next, due to (
5.6
); the definition (
3.26
) of
\mathfrak{b}_{tt}
; and the fact that
\max\big\{q_{0}^{(1)},\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(1)}\big\}\leq-\varepsilon
, there exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,m)>1
such that

(5.7)

\displaystyle\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)}\big)}\bigg|\leq C_{2}\big|\bm{q}^{(1)}-\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)}\big|;\qquad\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)}\bigg|+\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)}\big)}\bigg|\leq C_{2}.

Moreover, by (
5.6
); the fact that the
\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}
(satisfying (
5.5
)) are polynomials that only depend on the
q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}
with either
i^{\prime}\leq i+1
or
k^{\prime}\leq i+j+1
; and the fact that the coefficients and degrees of
\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}
only depend on
i,j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\bigg|\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\Big)-\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\Big)\bigg|\leq C_{3}\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i+1}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{i+j+2}\big|q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|+\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i+2}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{i+j+1}\big|q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|\Bigg);\\
&\bigg|\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\Big)\bigg|+\bigg|\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\Big)\bigg|\leq C_{3}.\end{aligned}

Thus, (
5.5
), (
5.7
), and (
5.8
) together yield a constant
C_{4}=C_{4}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
so that

\displaystyle\big|q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}-\widetilde{q}_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}\big|\leq C_{4}\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i+1}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{i+j+2}\big|q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|+\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i+2}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{i+j+1}\big|q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|\Bigg).

Induction on the lexicographic pair
(i+j+2,i+2)\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket^{2}
then gives a constant
C_{i,j}=C_{i,j}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
for any
(i,j)\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket^{2}
with
i+j+2\leq m
such that

\displaystyle\big|q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}-\widetilde{q}_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}\big|\leq C_{i,j}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{i+j+2}\Big(\big|q_{0}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|+\big|q_{1}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{1}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|\Big).

Summing over all such
(i,j)
with
i+j=k-2
, for any
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, we deduce the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
and real numbers
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
B>1
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that the following holds. Let
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
denote a sequence of
m
pairs of real numbers satisfying (
5.3
), and let
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)
denote its consistent extension. Let
\widetilde{\bm{Q}}=\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
be such that
\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(k)}=q_{0}^{(k)}
and
\widetilde{q}_{1}^{(k)}=q_{1}^{(k)}
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
. If
\big|\widetilde{q}_{j}^{(k)}\big|\leq B
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
, then

\displaystyle\big|\bm{q}^{(k)}-\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(k)}\big|\leq C\displaystyle\sum_{h=2}^{k}\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{h-2}\Big|\mathfrak{v}_{i,h-i-2}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)},\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(2)},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(h)}\big)\Big|,\qquad\text{for each $k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket$}.

Proof.

Throughout, we recall the notation from the proof of
5.4
. Following the beginning of the proof of
5.6
, we deduce the existence of a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that (
5.6
) holds (by the assumption of this lemma, as well as the second part of
5.4
); a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,m)>1
such that (
5.7
) holds (due to (
5.6
); the definition (
3.26
) of
\mathfrak{b}_{tt}
; and the fact that
q_{0}^{(1)}=\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(1)}\leq-\varepsilon
); and a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that (
5.8
) holds (by (
5.6
); the fact that the
\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}
are polynomials that only depend on the
q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}
with either
i^{\prime}\leq i+1
or
k^{\prime}\leq i+j+1
; and the fact the coefficients and degrees of
\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}
only depend on
i,j\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
).

Next, by (
5.4
), we have

\displaystyle q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}=-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\Big)}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)};\qquad\widetilde{q}_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}=\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)},\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(2)},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(i+j+2)}\big)}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)}\big)}-\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{u}_{i,j}\Big(\big(\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big)_{i^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\Big)}{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)}\big)}.

This, together with (
5.7
) and (
5.8
), yields a constant
C_{4}=C_{4}(\varepsilon,m)>1
so that

\displaystyle\big|q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}-\widetilde{q}_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}\big|\leq C_{4}\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i+1}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{i+j+2}\big|q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|

\displaystyle+\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i+2}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{i+j+1}\big|q_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}-\widetilde{q}_{i^{\prime}}^{(k^{\prime})}\big|

\displaystyle+\Big|\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)},\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(2)},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(i+j+2)}\big)\Big|\Bigg).

Induction on the lexicographic pair
(i+j+2,i+2)\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket^{2}
(together with the fact that
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)=\big(\widetilde{q}_{0}^{(k)},\widetilde{q}_{1}^{(k)}\big)
, for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
) then gives a constant
C_{i,j}=C_{i,j}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
for any
(i,j)\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket^{2}
with
i+j+2\leq m
such that

\displaystyle\big|q_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}-\widetilde{q}_{i+2}^{(i+j+2)}\big|\leq C_{i,j}\displaystyle\sum_{h=2}^{i+j+2}\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i+2}\Big|\mathfrak{v}_{i^{\prime},h-i^{\prime}-2}\big(\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)},\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(2)},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(h)}\big)\Big|.

Summing over all such
(i,j)
with
i+j=k-2
, for any
k\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket
(recall
\bm{q}^{(1)}=\widetilde{\bm{q}}^{(1)}
, addressing the case
k=1
), we deduce the lemma.
∎

5.2.
Concentrating Profiles

In this section we discuss families of (deterministic) height profiles that are limit shapes for systems of non-intersecting Brownian bridges that admit a (nearly) optimal concentration bound. They are given by the below definition.

Definition 5.8
.

Fix real numbers
a<b
and two functions
f,g:[a,b]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
f<g
. Denote the strip domain
\mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let
\mathfrak{H}:\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function. Given real numbers
C>1
and
c,\delta\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)
, we say that
\mathfrak{H}
is
(C;c;\delta)
-concentrating
if it is satisfies the equation (
3.25
) for each
z\in\mathfrak{S}
, and the following holds for any integer
n\geq 1
. There exists an integer
n^{\prime}\in[cn,n]
; starting data
\bm{u}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n^{\prime}}
; and ending data
\bm{v}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n^{\prime}}
on
\mathfrak{S}
so that, sampling
n^{\prime}
non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n^{\prime}})\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([a,b]\big)
from the measure
\mathfrak{Q}_{f;g}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}}(n^{-1})
and denoting the associated height function by
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{y}}
(recall (
3.6
)), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{S}}\big|n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{y}}(z)-\mathfrak{H}(z)\big|\geq n^{\delta-1}\Bigg]\leq Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}}.

We will be interested in height functions that are
(C;c;\delta)
-concentrating with small
\delta>0
. While it is plausible that essentially any solution of (
3.25
) on
\mathfrak{R}
has this property, it is not transparent to us how to show this directly. We instead show the following result stating that, if
\bm{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
is an admissible sequence of
m
vectors in the sense of
Definition

5.1
, then (for small
\delta
) there exists a
(C;c;\delta)
-concentrating height function
\mathfrak{H}
whose full-
m
local profile at
z=(0,0)
is given by
\bm{Q}
; see
Figure

5
for a depiction. It will be established in
Section

7.2
below.

Figure 5
.
Shown above is a depiction of
5.9
and
5.11
.

Proposition 5.9
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
and real numbers
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
B>1
, there exist constants
\nu=\nu(\varepsilon,B,m)\in(0,1)
,
c=c(\varepsilon,B,m)>0
, and
C=C(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that the following holds. Fix a sequence of
m
pairs
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
satisfying (
5.3
); denote its consistent extension by
\bm{Q}
. There is a strip domain
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of width
\nu
, such that
[0,\nu]\times[-\nu,\nu]\subseteq\mathfrak{S}\subseteq[0,\nu]\times[-C\nu,C\nu]
, and a function
\mathfrak{H}:\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, such that
\|\mathfrak{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}})}\leq C
and
\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{S}}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{H}(z)\leq-C^{-1}
, which satisfies the following two properties.

(1)

The full-
m
local profile of
\mathfrak{H}
satisfies
\lim_{z\rightarrow(0,0)}\bm{Q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(m)}(z)=\bm{Q}
.

(2)

For any real number
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,\delta,B,m)>1
such that
\mathfrak{H}
is
(C_{1};c;\delta)
-concentrating.

Observe that
5.9
fixes the local profile of
\mathfrak{H}
at the point
(0,0)
on the boundary of
\partial\mathfrak{R}
. However, it will be useful to (at least approximately) fix the local profile of
\mathfrak{H}
at some point in the interior of
\mathfrak{R}
, to which end we have the following lemma and corollary; see
Figure

5
for a depiction of the latter.

Lemma 5.10
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
and real numbers
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\nu\in(0,1)
, and
B>1
, there exist constants
c=c(\varepsilon,B,m)\in\big(0,\frac{\nu}{2}\big)
and
C=C(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that the following holds. Fix a consistent sequence
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
; denoting
\bm{q}^{(k)}=\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,q_{k}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, assume that (
5.3
) holds. Fix a real number
\rho\in(0,c)
and set

(5.9)

\displaystyle r_{0}^{(k)}=\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-k}\displaystyle\frac{(-\rho)^{i}}{i!}q_{i}^{(k+i)},\quad\text{and}\quad r_{1}^{(k)}=\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-k}\displaystyle\frac{(-\rho)^{i}}{i!}q_{i+1}^{(k+i)},\qquad\text{for each $k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket$}.

Let
\bm{R}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
denote the consistent extension of the sequence of
m
pairs
\big(r_{0}^{(k)},r_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
. If there exists a function
\mathfrak{H}:[0,\nu]\times[-\nu,\nu]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
3.25
) on
[0,\nu]\times[-\nu,\nu]
, with

(5.10)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}}\leq B,\quad\text{and}\quad\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow(0,0)}\bm{Q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(m)}(z)=\bm{R},

then
\big|\bm{q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(k)}(\rho,0)-\bm{q}^{(k)}\big|\leq C\rho^{m-k+1}
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, where
\bm{q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(k)}
is as defined in (
5.1
).

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we set
q_{0}^{(0)}=0
. First observe that, since (
5.3
) holds,
5.4
yields a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that

(5.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket}\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket}\big|q_{j}^{(k)}\big|\leq C_{1}.

This, together with the fact that
q_{0}^{(1)}\leq-\varepsilon
(by (
5.3
)) implies (by the definition (
5.9
) of
r_{0}^{(k)}
and
r_{1}^{(k)}
) that there exist constants
c_{1}=c_{1}(\varepsilon,B,m)>0
and
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that for
\rho\in(0,c_{1})
we have
r_{0}^{(1)}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
and
\big|r_{i}^{(k)}\big|\leq C_{2}
, for each
i\in\{0,1\}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
. Hence, the second part of
5.4
yields a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that

(5.12)

\displaystyle r_{0}^{(1)}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2};\qquad\displaystyle\max_{k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket}\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket}\big|r_{j}^{(k)}\big|\leq C_{3}.

Next, define the (not necessarily consistent) sequence of
m+1
vectors
\widetilde{\bm{R}}=\big(\widetilde{\bm{r}}^{(k)}\big)_{k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket}\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, where
\widetilde{\bm{r}}^{(k)}=\big(\widetilde{r}_{0}^{(k)},\widetilde{r}_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,\widetilde{r}_{k}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
for each
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, by

(5.13)

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{i}^{(k)}=\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{m-k}\displaystyle\frac{(-\rho)^{i^{\prime}}}{i^{\prime}!}q_{i+i^{\prime}}^{(k+i^{\prime})},

so in particular

(5.14)

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{0}^{(k)}=r_{0}^{(k)};\qquad\widetilde{r}_{1}^{(k)}=r_{1}^{(k)}.

Let us express the
q_{i}^{(k)}
through the
\widetilde{r}_{i}^{(k)}
. To that end, define the polynomials
A_{0},A_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

(5.15)

\displaystyle A_{0}(t,x)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\displaystyle\frac{q_{j}^{(k)}}{j!(k-j)!}t^{j}x^{k-j};\qquad A_{1}(t,x)=\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{r}_{j}^{(k)}}{j!(k-j)!}t^{j}x^{k-j}.

so that

(5.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} A_{0}(t-\rho,x)&=\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}\displaystyle\frac{(-\rho)^{j-i}}{(k-j)!i!(j-i)!}q_{j}^{(k)}t^{i}x^{k-j}\\
&=\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{m-i}\displaystyle\frac{t^{i}x^{j^{\prime}}}{i!j^{\prime}!}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{m-i-j^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{(-\rho)^{k^{\prime}}}{k^{\prime}!}q_{i+k^{\prime}}^{(i+j^{\prime}+k^{\prime})}=\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{m-i}\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{r}_{i}^{(i+j^{\prime})}}{i!j^{\prime}!}t^{i}x^{j^{\prime}}=A_{1}(t,x),\end{aligned}

where in the second equality we changed variables
(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})=(k-j,j-i)
(and used the fact that
q_{0}^{(0)}=0
), in the third we used the definition (
5.13
) of
\widetilde{r}_{i}^{(i+j)}
, and in the fourth we again changed variables
(i,j^{\prime})=(j,k-j)
. It follows (again since
q_{0}^{(0)}=0
) that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\displaystyle\frac{t^{j}x^{k-j}}{j!(k-j)!}q_{j}^{(k)}=A_{0}(t,x)=A_{1}(t+\rho,x)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}\displaystyle\frac{\rho^{j-i}}{(k-j)!i!(j-i)!}\widetilde{r}_{j}^{(k)}t^{i}x^{k-j}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{m-i}\displaystyle\frac{t^{i}x^{j^{\prime}}}{i!j^{\prime}!}\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{m-i-j^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{\rho^{k^{\prime}}}{k^{\prime}!}\widetilde{r}_{i+k^{\prime}}^{(i+j^{\prime}+k^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{k}\displaystyle\frac{t^{j}x^{k-j}}{j!(k-j)!}\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{m-k}\displaystyle\frac{\rho^{i^{\prime}}}{i^{\prime}!}\widetilde{r}_{j+i^{\prime}}^{(k+i^{\prime})},

where in the fourth equality we again changed variables
(j^{\prime},k^{\prime})=(k-j,j-i)
, and in the fifth we changed variables
(i,j^{\prime},k^{\prime})=(j,k-j,i^{\prime})
. Comparing coefficients of
t^{j}x^{k-j}
, we deduce

(5.17)

\displaystyle q_{i}^{(k)}=\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{m-k}\displaystyle\frac{\rho^{i^{\prime}}}{i^{\prime}!}\widetilde{r}_{i+i^{\prime}}^{(k+i^{\prime})},\qquad\text{for each $k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket$ and $i\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket$}.

Next, set
\bm{R}=\big(\bm{r}^{(k)}\big)
, where
\bm{r}^{(k)}=\big(r_{0}^{(k)},r_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,r_{k}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
. Due to (
5.10
) and a Taylor expansion, we have

\displaystyle\Bigg|\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}\mathfrak{H}(\rho,0)-\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{m-i-j}\displaystyle\frac{\rho^{i^{\prime}}}{i^{\prime}!}r_{i+i^{\prime}}^{(i+i^{\prime}+j)}\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}\mathfrak{H}(\rho,0)-\displaystyle\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{m-i-j}\displaystyle\frac{\rho^{i^{\prime}}}{i^{\prime}!}\partial_{t}^{i+i^{\prime}}\partial_{x}^{j}\mathfrak{H}(0,0)\Bigg|\leq B\rho^{m-i-j+1}.

Hence, by (
5.17
), it suffices to show

(5.18)

\displaystyle\big|r_{i}^{(k)}-\widetilde{r}_{i}^{(k)}\big|\leq C\rho^{m-k+1},\qquad\text{for any $k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket$ and $i\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket$}.

To that end, define
D:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by, for any
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, setting

\displaystyle D(t,x)=\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{t,x\}}\mathfrak{b}_{jk}\big(\nabla A_{1}(t,x)\big)\partial_{j}\partial_{j}A_{1}(t,x)=\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{t,x\}}\mathfrak{b}_{j,k}\big(\nabla A_{0}(t-\rho,x)\big)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}A_{0}(t-\rho,x),

were in the last equality we used (
5.16
). We will use a Taylor expansion to view
D
as an approximation of the left side of (
3.25
), where the
H
there is
A
here. For each integer
k\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
, set
\bm{Q}^{(k)}=\big(\bm{q}^{(1)},\bm{q}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
and
\widetilde{\bm{R}}^{(k)}=\big(\widetilde{\bm{r}}^{(1)},\widetilde{\bm{r}}^{(2)},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{r}}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
. Under this notation, we have from
Definition

5.1
(and the fact from (
5.15
) that
\bm{Q}_{A_{0}}^{(k)}(0,0)=\bm{Q}^{(k)}
and
\bm{Q}_{A_{1}}^{(k)}(0,0)=\widetilde{\bm{R}}^{(k)}
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
), that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}D(0,0)=\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\widetilde{\bm{R}}^{(i+j+2)}\big);\qquad\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}D(\rho,0)=\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\bm{Q}^{(i+j+2)}\big),

for each
(i,j)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
with
i+j\leq m-2
. Since
\bm{Q}
is consistent, we have from (
5.2
) and (
5.11
) (and the fact that all derivatives of
D
of order at most
2m
are uniformly bounded on compact sets) that there exists a constant
C_{4}=C_{4}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
with

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}D(\rho,0)=0,\quad\text{for any $(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}$ with $i+j\leq m-2$};\qquad\displaystyle\max_{i,j\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket}\displaystyle\sup_{|z|\leq 1}\big|\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}D(z)\big|\leq C_{4}.

Hence, it follows from a Taylor expansion that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{v}_{i,j}\big(\widetilde{\bm{R}}^{(i+j+2)}\big)\Big|=\big|\partial_{t}^{i}\partial_{x}^{j}D(0,0)\big|\leq C_{4}\rho^{m-i-j-1}.

This, with
5.7
, (
5.12
), (
5.11
), (
5.13
), and (
5.14
), implies (
5.18
) and thus the lemma.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

Adopting the notation and assumptions of
5.9
, there exist constants
\nu=\nu(\varepsilon,B,m)\in(0,1)
,
c=c(\varepsilon,B,m)>0
, and
C=C(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
; a strip domain
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
; and a function
\mathfrak{H}:\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, such that all statements but the first enumerated one in
5.9
hold. Moreover, the following variant of that first statement holds, for any real number
\rho\in(0,c)
.

1.

Denoting
\bm{Q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(m)}(\rho,0)=\big(\bm{q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(k)}(\rho,0)\big)_{k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket}
, we have
\big|\bm{q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(k)}(\rho,0)-\bm{q}^{(k)}\big|\leq C\rho^{m-k+1}
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
.

Proof.

Define the
m
real pairs
\big(r_{0}^{(k)},r_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
from
\bm{Q}
through (
5.9
), and let
\bm{R}
denote its consistent extension. By
5.9
(applied with the
\bm{Q}
there equal to
\bm{R}
here) and (
5.12
), there exist constants
\nu=\nu(\varepsilon,B,m)\in(0,1)
,
c=c(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
, and
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
; a strip domain
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
of width
\nu
, such that
[0,\nu]\times[-\nu,\nu]\subseteq\mathfrak{S}\subseteq[0,\nu]\times[-C_{1}\nu,C_{1}\nu]
; and a function
\mathfrak{H}:\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\|\mathfrak{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}})}\leq C_{1}
and
\inf_{z\in\mathfrak{S}}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{H}(z)\leq-C_{1}^{-1}
, satisfying
\lim_{z\rightarrow(0,0)}\bm{Q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(m)}(z)=\bm{R}
and the following property. For any real numbers
\delta\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,\delta,B,m)>1
such that
\mathfrak{H}
is
(C_{2};c;\delta)
-concentrating. This
\mathfrak{H}
satisfies the last condition of the corollary, as
5.10
yields a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that
\big|\bm{q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(k)}(\rho,0)-\bm{q}^{(k)}\big|\leq C_{3}\rho^{m-k+1}
, for each
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and sufficiently small
\rho
.
∎

6.
Proof of Concentration Bounds

In this section we establish
4.4
.

6.1.
Local Approximations of
\mathfrak{H}

In this section we will establish various statements needed to show
4.4
in
Section

6.2
below; we only establish the bound in that statement on
\Omega_{j;k}^{+}(z)
, as the proof of the bound on
\Omega_{j;k}^{-}(z)
is entirely analogous. Throughout, we recall the notation from
Section

4.2
; fix
(j,k)\in\llbracket 0,J\rrbracket\times[0,K]
and a point
z\in\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}
; and set (recall (
4.7
))

\displaystyle\varphi=\varphi_{j;k-1}^{+};\qquad\Omega=\bigcup_{w\in\mathfrak{P}}\Omega_{j;k-1}^{+};\qquad\kappa=1-(k-1)\rho^{2}n^{-\delta};\qquad\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{f;g}.

Throughout this section, we further restrict to the event
\Omega^{\complement}
.

Let us now briefly outline how we will establish
4.4
. We will proceed by locally comparing the Brownian bridges
\bm{x}
in a neighborhood of
z
with a family
\bm{y}
of non-intersecting Brownian bridges, whose associated normalized height function
n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{y}}
is known to concentrate around its limit shape
\mathfrak{H}
. By
5.11
, there are many choices for this limit shape
\mathfrak{H}
; we will make its second derivatives slightly smaller than those of
\varphi
(while making its gradient match that of
\varphi
), so as to ensure that
\varphi_{j;k}^{+}(z)>\mathfrak{H}(z)
while
\varphi|_{\partial\mathfrak{B}_{\rho}(z)}<\mathfrak{H}|_{\partial\mathfrak{B}_{\rho}(z)}
. Since the normalized height function
n^{-1}H
of
\bm{x}
is bounded above by
\varphi
on the event
\Omega^{\complement}
, comparing
\bm{x}
to
\bm{y}
using monotonicity (
2.3
) and applying the concentration bound for
\bm{y}
(
5.11
) will yield
n^{-1}\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}(z)\leq\mathfrak{H}(z)<\varphi_{j;k}^{+}(z)
with high probability, establishing
4.4
.

To implement this, we must fix the derivatives of the function
\mathfrak{H}
, to which end we require some additional notation. Recalling the
\mathfrak{b}_{ij}
from (
3.26
), define for any
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{<0}
the vector

\displaystyle\bm{p}(u,v)=\big(\mathfrak{b}_{xx}(u,v),2\mathfrak{b}_{tx}(u,v),\mathfrak{b}_{tt}(u,v)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Observe under this notation, and recalling
\bm{q}_{F}^{(2)}
for any function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{P})
from (
5.1
), the constraint (
3.25
) is equivalent to
\bm{p}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{F}^{(2)}(z)=0
.

Lemma 6.1
.

The following two statements hold for
n
sufficiently large.

(1)

For any
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\varphi(z_{0})\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2};\qquad\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})}\leq 2B.

(2)

For any
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z_{0})}{\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{\psi}^{(2)}(z_{0})}\bigg|\leq\displaystyle\frac{\kappa\omega}{2}.

Proof.

From the definition (
4.7
) of
\varphi=\varphi_{j;k-1}^{+}
, we have for any
z_{0}=(t,x)\in\mathfrak{P}
that

(6.1)

\displaystyle\big|\nabla\varphi(z_{0})-\nabla H(z_{0})\big|\leq\kappa\omega\big|\nabla\psi(z_{0})\big|\leq 2\kappa\omega\zeta\max\{e^{\zeta t},e^{\zeta x}\}.

where in the second inequality we used the definition (
4.6
) of
\psi
. Together with the fact that any
z_{0}=(t,x)\in\mathfrak{P}
satisfies
|x|+|t|\leq 3B_{0}
(as
|x|+|t|\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}}+L^{-1}\leq 2\|G\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}}+B_{0}\leq 3B_{0}
) and the definition (
4.6
) of
\omega
, this implies for sufficiently large
n
that

(6.2)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{x}\varphi(z_{0})-\partial_{x}H(z_{0})\big|\leq 2\kappa\omega\zeta e^{3B_{0}\zeta}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Since (
4.1
) gives
\partial_{x}H(z_{0})\leq-\varepsilon
, this yields the first part of the first statement of the lemma; the proof of the second part of the first statement is entirely analogous.

To establish the second, first observe from (
6.2
) and (
4.2
) that
\big|\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big|\leq\big|\nabla H(z_{0})\big|+\varepsilon\leq 2B
for each
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}
. Moreover, by (
3.26
), each
\mathfrak{b}_{ij}(u,v)
is
100B^{3}
-Lipschitz on the region where
\max\big\{|u|,|v|\big\}\leq 2B
. Thus, since
\bm{p}\big(\nabla H(z_{0})\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z_{0})=0
(by the fact that
H
solves (
3.25
)), we have for any
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}
that

(6.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Big|\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z_{0})\Big|&=\bigg|\Big(\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)-\bm{p}\big(\nabla H(z_{0})\big)\Big)\cdot\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z_{0})\bigg|\\
&\leq 200B^{3}\big|\nabla\varphi(z_{0})-\nabla H(z_{0})\big|\big|\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z_{0})\big|\leq 200B^{4}\big|\nabla\varphi(z_{0})-\nabla H(z_{0})\big|,\end{aligned}

where in the last bound we used (
4.2
). Again by (
3.26
) and the first part of the lemma, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{z_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}}\min\big\{\mathfrak{b}_{tt}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big),\mathfrak{b}_{xx}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)\big\}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{16}.

Since (
4.6
) gives for
z_{0}=(t,x)\in\mathfrak{P}
that
\bm{q}_{\psi}^{(2)}(z_{0})=(-\zeta^{2}e^{\zeta x},0,-\zeta^{2}e^{\zeta t})
, this implies

\displaystyle\Big|\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{\psi}^{(2)}(z_{0})\Big|\geq\displaystyle\frac{\zeta^{2}\varepsilon^{4}}{16}\displaystyle\max\{e^{\zeta t},e^{\zeta x}\}.

Together with (
6.3
) and (
6.1
), this yields

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{z_{0}\in\mathfrak{P}}\displaystyle\frac{\Big|\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z_{0})\Big|}{\Big|\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z_{0})\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{\psi}^{(2)}(z_{0})\Big|}\leq\displaystyle\frac{6400B^{4}}{\varepsilon^{4}\zeta}\kappa\omega,

which by (
4.6
) yields the lemma.
∎

Now let us proceed to prescribe the derivatives of
\mathfrak{H}
at
z
, which will be a higher order variant of what was done in
[
52
, Equation (72)]
. They will be set to be near an admissible, consistent profile
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
, defined as follows. Denoting
\bm{q}^{(k)}=\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,q_{k}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
for each
k\in\llbracket 3,m\rrbracket
, set

(6.4)

\displaystyle\bm{q}^{(1)}=\nabla\varphi(z);\quad\bm{q}^{(2)}=\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z)-\displaystyle\frac{\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z)\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{H}^{(2)}(z)}{\bm{p}\big(\nabla\varphi(z)\big)\cdot\bm{q}_{\psi}^{(2)}(z)}\bm{q}_{\psi}^{(2)}(z);\quad q_{0}^{(k)}=\partial_{x}^{k}\varphi(z);\quad q_{1}^{(k)}=\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{k-1}\varphi(z).

Observe from the first part of
6.1
that
q_{0}^{(1)}<0
. Thus, the sequence of
m
pairs
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)\in(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{m}
admits a consistent extension,
9
9

9

Recall in
Definition

5.5
that consistent extensions were defined on sequences of pairs, while
\bm{q}^{(2)}
above is a triple. Our choice of
\bm{q}^{(2)}
ensures that this introduces no conflict in notation. Indeed, (
6.4
) implies that
\bm{p}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)}\big)\cdot\bm{q}^{(2)}=0
, which by (
3.25
) and (
5.2
) gives
\mathfrak{v}_{0,0}\big(\bm{q}^{(1)},\bm{q}^{(2)}\big)=0
. In particular, the second vector of the admissible extension
\bm{Q}
of
\big(q_{0}^{(k)},q_{1}^{(k)}\big)
must indeed be
\bm{q}^{(2)}
.
which we denote by
\bm{Q}=\big(\bm{q}^{(k)}\big)
.

By
5.11
(and the first statement of
6.1
), there exist constants
\nu=\nu(\varepsilon,B,m)>0
and
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
; a strip domain
\mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{P}_{\mathfrak{f};\mathfrak{g}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
[0,\nu]\times[-\nu,\nu]\subseteq\mathfrak{S}\subseteq[0,\nu]\times[-C_{1}\nu,C_{1}\nu]
; and a function
\mathfrak{H}:\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying (recall (
5.1
))

(6.5)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}})}\leq C_{1};\quad\displaystyle\inf_{z\in\mathfrak{S}}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{H}(z)\leq-\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{1}};\quad\text{$\big|\bm{q}_{\mathfrak{H}}^{(k)}(\rho,0)-\bm{q}^{(k)}\big|\leq C_{1}\rho^{m-k+1}$, for each $k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket$},

and the below property. There exist constants
c_{0}=c_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,B,m)>0
and
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,\delta,B,m)>1
so that
\mathfrak{H}
is
\big(C_{2};c_{0};\delta\big)
-concentrating, meaning (recall
Definition

5.8
) that
\mathfrak{H}
satisfies (
3.25
) and the following. There exist an integer
n^{\prime}\in[c_{0}n,n]
, entrance data
\overline{\bm{u}}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n^{\prime}}
and ending data
\overline{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{W}_{n^{\prime}}
on
\mathfrak{S}
