0\leq y_{0}-\frac{(n^{\prime})^{1+2\omega}}{n}-\frac{n^{\prime}}{2n}\leq y_{0}+\frac{(n^{\prime})^{1+2\omega}}{n}-\frac{n^{\prime}}{2n}\leq 1
(as
\min\{y_{0},1-y_{0}\}\geq\dist\big((t_{0},y_{0}),\partial\mathfrak{R}\big)\geq n^{-\delta}
, as stipulated in
1.5
). Next, define the density
\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
by setting

(8.18)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(x)=-\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}(0,x),\qquad\text{if $\widetilde{G}(0,1)\leq x\leq\widetilde{G}(0,0)$};\\
&\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(x)=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{if $x<\widetilde{G}(0,1)$ or if $x>\widetilde{G}(0,0)$.}\end{aligned}

Observe in particular that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(x)dx=\widetilde{H}\big(0,\widetilde{G}(0,1)\big)-\widetilde{H}\big(0,\widetilde{G}(0,0)\big)=1
. Denoting the measure
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}=\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(x)dx
, we then find that
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{0}
, that is, it is a probability measure. Denote its free convolution with the rescaled semicircle law (recall
Section

2.2
) by
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}=\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
for any
t>0
; also denote the associated density by
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, which satisfies
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}(dx)=\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)dx
. Denote the classical locations (recall
Definition

2.16
with
n
replaced by
n^{\prime}
) with respect to
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}
by
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(t)=\gamma_{j;n^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mu}_{t}}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

We define the analogous quantities associated with
\widetilde{G}^{-}
and
\widetilde{G}^{+}
very similarly. In particular, for each index
\pm\in\{+,-\}
, we define
\widetilde{H}^{\pm}(x)=\sup\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}:\widetilde{G}^{\pm}(y)\geq x\big\}
, so that for any
x\in[-n^{\omega},n^{\omega}]
we have that
\widetilde{H}^{\pm}(x)
is the unique value of
y\in\big[-(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/2},(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/2}\big]
such that
\widetilde{G}^{\pm}(y)=x
. We further define the density
\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}^{\pm}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
by setting
\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}^{\pm}(x)=-\partial_{x}\widetilde{H}^{\pm}(x)
if
\widetilde{G}^{\pm}(1)\leq x\leq\widetilde{G}^{\pm}(0)
, and
\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}^{\pm}(x)=0
otherwise. Then,
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}^{\pm}=\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}^{\pm}(x)dx
is a probability measure. Denote its free convolution with the semicircle law by
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\pm}=\widetilde{\mu}_{0}^{\pm}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(t)}
and the associated density by
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\pm}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Also denote the classical locations with respect to
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\pm}
by
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}^{\pm}(t)=\gamma_{j;n^{\prime}}^{\tilde{\mu}_{t}^{\pm}}
.

The next lemma provides bounds on this density
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}
, as well as on the classical locations
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(t)
(and their analogs for
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\pm}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}^{\pm}(t)
); it is established in
Section

8.4
below.

Lemma 8.6
.

There exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that the following hold for each integer
n\geq C
and index
\pm\in\{+,-\}
.

(1)

If
(t,x)\in[0,n^{-\omega}]\times[-C^{-1},C^{-1}]
, then
-4\varepsilon^{-1}\leq\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
and
-4\varepsilon^{-1}\leq\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\pm}(x)\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
.

(2)

If
i\in\big\llbracket-(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega},(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega}\big\rrbracket
and
t\in[0,n^{-\omega}]
, then
\big|\widetilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}(t)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}(0)\big|\leq Ct\big(\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+\frac{|i|}{n^{\prime}}+t\big)
and
\big|\widetilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}^{\pm}(t)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}^{\pm}(0)\big|\leq Ct\big(\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+\frac{|i|}{n^{\prime}}+t\big)
.

We also require the below lemma indicating a monotone coupling for Dyson Brownian motion, established in
Section

8.4
below.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
M\geq 1
and
k\geq 2
be integers, and fix
k

M
-tuples
\bm{\lambda}^{(1)},\bm{\lambda}^{(2)},\ldots,\bm{\lambda}^{(k)}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{M}
with
\bm{\lambda}^{(1)}\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(k)}
. For each integer
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, let
\bm{\lambda}^{(j)}(s)=\big(\lambda_{1}^{(j)}(s),\lambda_{2}^{(j)}(s),\ldots,\lambda_{M}^{(j)}(s)\big)
denote Dyson Brownian motion run for time
s
with initial data
\bm{\lambda}^{(j)}
. Then it is possible to couple the
\big\{\bm{\lambda}^{(j)}(s)\big\}
such that
\bm{\lambda}^{(1)}(s)\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(2)}(s)\leq\cdots\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(k)}(s)
, for each real number
s\geq 0
.

Now we can establish
8.5
.

Proof of
8.5
.

By (
8.3
), we may restrict to the event
\mathscr{A}_{0}
in what follows. Define the two
n^{\prime}
-tuples
\bm{\lambda}^{-}=(\lambda_{1}^{-},\lambda_{2}^{-},\ldots,\lambda_{n^{\prime}}^{-})\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n^{\prime}}
and
\bm{\lambda}^{+}=(\lambda_{1}^{+},\lambda_{2}^{+},\ldots,\lambda_{n^{\prime}}^{+})\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n^{\prime}}
by setting

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}^{\pm}=\widetilde{G}^{\pm}\Big(\frac{2j-1}{n^{\prime}}\Big),\qquad\text{for each index $\pm\in\{+,-\}$ and integer $j\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket$}.

For each index
\pm\in\{+,-\}
, let
\bm{\lambda}^{\pm}(s)=\big(\lambda_{1}^{\pm}(s),\lambda_{2}^{\pm}(s),\ldots,\lambda_{n^{\prime}}^{\pm}(s)\big)
denote Dyson Brownian motion, run for time
s
, with initial data
\bm{\lambda}^{\pm}
. Since we have restricted to the event
\mathscr{A}_{0}
, we have by (
8.12
); (
8.4
) (with the definitions of
G^{-}
and
G^{+}
); and the fact from (
8.13
) that
\partial_{y}\widetilde{G}^{\pm}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, that
\bm{\lambda}^{-}-2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}\leq\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{-}(0)\leq\bm{\lambda}^{-}+2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}
and
\bm{\lambda}^{+}-2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}\leq\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{+}(0)\leq\bm{\lambda}^{+}+2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}
. Thus, by
8.7
, it is possible to couple the processes
\big(\bm{\lambda}^{-}(s),\bm{\widetilde{w}}^{-}(s)\big)
such that
\bm{\lambda}^{-}(t)-2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}\leq\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{-}(t)\leq\bm{\lambda}^{+}(t)+2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}
for each
t\geq 0
; similarly, it is possible to couple
\big(\bm{\lambda}^{+}(s),\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{+}(s)\big)
such that
\bm{\lambda}^{+}(t)-2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}\leq\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{+}(t)\leq\bm{\lambda}^{-}(t)+2(n^{\prime})^{3\omega/5-1}
for each
t\geq 0
. Hence, it suffices to show

(8.19)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}\Bigg[\mathscr{A}_{0}\cap\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in[0,\tilde{s}_{0}+\tilde{s}_{1}]\\
j\in\llbracket n^{\prime}/2-2n^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime}/2+2n^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\end{subarray}}\Big\{\big|\lambda_{j}^{-}(s)-\lambda_{j}^{-}(0)\big|\leq(n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-1}\Big\}\Bigg]\geq 1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log n)^{2}};\\
\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\mathscr{A}_{0}\cap\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in[0,\tilde{s}_{0}+\tilde{s}_{1}]\\
j\in\llbracket n^{\prime}/2-2n^{\prime\prime},n^{\prime}/2+2n^{\prime\prime}\rrbracket\end{subarray}}\Big\{\big|\lambda_{j}^{+}(s)-\lambda_{j}^{+}(0)\big|\leq(n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-1}\Big\}\Bigg]\geq 1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log n)^{2}}.\end{aligned}

We only show the first bound in (
8.19
), as the proof of the second is entirely analogous. Under this notation,
2.17
applies with the
(n,\bm{\lambda})
there given by
(n^{\prime},\bm{\lambda}^{-})
here and the
\mu
there given by
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}^{-}
here. It therefore yields a constant
c_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{E}_{1}^{-}]\geq 1-c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}(\log n)^{2}},

where we have defined the event

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{1}^{-}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\bigcap_{t\in[0,1]}\bigg\{\widetilde{\gamma}_{j+\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{-}(t)-n^{-1}\leq\lambda_{j}^{-}(t)\leq\widetilde{\gamma}_{j-\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{-}(t)+n^{-1}\bigg\}.

Observe on
\mathscr{E}_{1}^{-}
that

\displaystyle\big|\lambda_{j}^{-}(s)-\lambda_{j}^{-}(0)\big|\leq\widetilde{\gamma}_{j-\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{-}(s)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{j+\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{-}(0)+2n^{-1}.

This yields on
\mathscr{E}_{1}^{-}
a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that, for each pair
(j,s)\in\big\llbracket\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}-2n^{\prime\prime},\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}+2n^{\prime\prime}\big\rrbracket\times[0,n^{-\omega}]
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\lambda_{j}^{-}(s)-\lambda_{j}^{-}(0)\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}s\Big(\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime\prime}}{n^{\prime}}+s\Big)+\widetilde{\gamma}_{j-\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{-}(0)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{j+\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{-}(0)+\displaystyle\frac{2}{n}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{1}s\Big(\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime\prime}}+s\Big)+\displaystyle\frac{4(\log n)^{5}}{\varepsilon n^{\prime}}+\displaystyle\frac{2}{n},

where in the first inequality we used the second part of
8.6
; in the second inequality we used (
8.1
) and the fact that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}^{-}=-\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}^{-}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Together with the fact that
s\big(\frac{1}{n^{\prime\prime}}+s\big)\leq\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}
for sufficiently large
n
(since
n^{\prime\prime}=(n^{\prime})^{1/2}
,
s\leq\widetilde{s}_{0}+\widetilde{s}_{1}\leq 2(n^{\prime})^{6\omega-1}
, and
\omega\leq\frac{1}{20}
), this implies the first statement of (
8.19
) and thus the lemma. ∎

8.4.
Proofs of
8.6
and
8.7

In this section we establish first
8.6
and then
8.7
.

Proof of
8.6
.

We only establish the estimates in the lemma for
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(t)
, as the proofs of the analogous bounds for
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}^{\pm}
and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}^{\pm}(t)
are entirely analogous. We begin with the first statement of the lemma. To that end observe that, since
\widetilde{G}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}\big(\frac{n}{n^{\prime}}\cdot\mathfrak{R}\big)
(by (
8.13
)), we have

(8.20)

\displaystyle-\displaystyle\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\leq\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(x)\leq-\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\qquad\text{for each $x\in\big[\widetilde{G}(0,1),\widetilde{G}(0,0)\big]$}.

Again by (
8.13
), we also have that
[\widetilde{G}]_{k}\leq\frac{2B_{0}n^{\prime}}{n}
for each integer
k\in\llbracket 2,m\rrbracket
, from which it quickly follows from (
8.17
), (
8.18
), and (
8.20
) that there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
with

(8.21)

\displaystyle[\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}]_{k}\leq\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}n^{\prime}}{n},\qquad\text{on $\supp\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}$, for each $k\in\llbracket 1,m-1\rrbracket$}.

As such,
2.11
applies to
\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}
around any point in the interior of
\supp\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}
and yields (since
\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)=0
and
\partial\widetilde{G}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, by (
8.13
)) a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that

(8.22)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1/C_{2}]}\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 1/C_{2}}\big|\partial_{t}\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)\big|\leq C_{2};\qquad\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1/C_{2}]}\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 1/C_{2}}\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 1,m-3\rrbracket}\big|\partial_{t}\partial_{x}^{j}\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(x)\big|\leq C_{2}.

The first bound in (
8.22
), with the first two statements of (
8.13
), yields the first statement of the lemma for sufficiently large
n
(so that
4C_{2}n^{-\omega}\leq\varepsilon
).

To establish the second part of the lemma, first observe for
i\in\big\llbracket-(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega},(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega}\big\rrbracket
that

(8.23)

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}(0)\big|=\bigg|\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\frac{i}{n^{\prime}}\Big)\bigg|\in\bigg[-\displaystyle\frac{2i}{\varepsilon n^{\prime}},\displaystyle\frac{2i}{\varepsilon n^{\prime}}\bigg]\subseteq\bigg[-\displaystyle\frac{2}{\varepsilon(n^{\prime})^{\omega}},\displaystyle\frac{2}{\varepsilon(n^{\prime})^{\omega}}\bigg],

due to the first and third statements of (
8.13
). We then claim that there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that (recalling the Hilbert transform from (
2.7
))

(8.24)

\displaystyle\big|H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y)\big|\leq C_{3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+|y|+s\Big),\qquad\text{for each $(s,y)\in[0,n^{-\omega}]\times\big[-(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2},(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2}\big]$}.

Before verifying (
8.24
) let us confirm the second part of the lemma assuming it. To that end, observe for any
j\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)}^{\infty}\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)dx-\displaystyle\frac{2j-1}{n^{\prime}}=\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)}^{\infty}\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)dx-\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)}^{\infty}\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(x)dsdx

\displaystyle=\pi\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)}^{\infty}\partial_{x}\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(x)H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(x)\big)dxds

\displaystyle=\pi\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}\big(\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)\big)\cdot H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}\big(\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)\big)ds,

where the first statement follows from the definition of the classical location
\widetilde{\gamma}_{j}(0)
; the second from performing the integration in
s
; the third from integrating (
2.12
); and the fourth from performing the integration in
x
(using the fact that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(x)=0
for
x
sufficiently large). This with
j=\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}+i
for
i\in\big\llbracket-(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega},(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega}\big\rrbracket
, together with (
8.24
), (
8.23
), and the fact (from the first part of the lemma) that
\big|\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(x)\big|\leq 4\varepsilon^{-1}
for each
(s,x)\in[0,n^{-\omega}]\times[-n^{-\omega/8},n^{-\omega/8}]
, yields (since
|\gamma_{j}|\leq\frac{3|i|}{\varepsilon n}
, by the second and third statements in (
8.13
))

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\displaystyle\frac{i}{n^{\prime}}-\displaystyle\frac{8\pi C_{3}t}{\varepsilon}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+\displaystyle\frac{3|i|}{\varepsilon n^{\prime}}+t\Big)\leq\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}(0)}^{\infty}\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)dx\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}+\displaystyle\frac{i}{n^{\prime}}+\displaystyle\frac{8\pi C_{3}t}{\varepsilon}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+\displaystyle\frac{3|i|}{\varepsilon n^{\prime}}+t\Big).

Together with the facts that
\int_{\tilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}(t)}^{\infty}\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)dx=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2i-1}{n^{\prime}}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket
and that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}(x)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
for
|x|\leq n^{-\omega/8}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}(t)-\widetilde{\gamma}_{n^{\prime}/2+i}(0)\big|\leq\displaystyle\frac{30\pi C_{3}t}{\varepsilon^{3}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+\displaystyle\frac{|i|}{n^{\prime}}+t\Big),

which verifies the second part of the lemma.

It then remains to establish (
8.24
), to which end we will bound
\big|H\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(y)\big|
and
\big|\partial_{t}H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y)\big|
. First observe that
\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{0}=\big[\widetilde{G}(0,1),\widetilde{G}(0,0)\big]
and that

(8.25)

\displaystyle-2\varepsilon^{-1}\leq\widetilde{G}(0,1)\leq-\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\leq\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\leq\widetilde{G}(0,0)\leq 2\varepsilon^{-1};\qquad\big|\widetilde{G}(0,0)+\widetilde{G}(0,1)\big|\leq\displaystyle\frac{4B_{0}n^{\prime}}{n},

where the first statement follows from the first and third statements of (
8.13
), and the second follows from the first and last statement of (
8.13
). We next have that

(8.26)

\displaystyle\big|H\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(y)\big|=\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(w)dw}{w-y}\Bigg|=\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(w)-\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(y)\big)dw}{w-y}\Bigg|.

Thus, for
y\in[-n^{-\omega/8},n^{-\omega/8}]
, we have

(8.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|H\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(y)\big|&\leq\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{G}(0,1)}^{\tilde{G}(0,0)}\displaystyle\frac{\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(w)-\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(y)\big)dw}{w-y}\Bigg|+\pi^{-1}\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}(y)\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\tilde{G}(0,1)}\displaystyle\frac{dw}{w-y}+\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{G}(0,0)}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{dw}{w-y}\Bigg|\\
&\leq\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}n^{\prime}}{n}\big|\widetilde{G}(0,0)-\widetilde{G}(0,1)\big|+\varepsilon^{-1}\Big(\big|\widetilde{G}(0,0)+\widetilde{G}(0,1)\big|+2|y|\Big)\cdot\displaystyle\max_{w\notin[\tilde{G}(0,1),\tilde{G}(0,0)]}|w-y|^{-2}\\
&\leq\displaystyle\frac{4C_{1}n^{\prime}}{\varepsilon n}+\displaystyle\frac{256B_{0}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}+|y|\Big),\end{aligned}

where the first bound follows from (
8.26
) and the fact that
\supp\widetilde{\varrho}_{0}=\big[\widetilde{G}(0,1),\widetilde{G}(0,0)\big]
; the second follows from (
8.21
) and (
8.20
); and the third follows from (
8.25
) (using the fact that
|w-y|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}
for
|y|\leq n^{-\omega}
and
w\notin\big[\widetilde{G}(0,1),\widetilde{G}(0,0)\big]
, due to the first statement of (
8.25
)).

Next, we claim that there exists a constant
C_{4}=C_{4}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that

(8.28)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,n^{-\omega}]}\displaystyle\sup_{|y|\leq(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2}}\big|\partial_{t}H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y)\big|\leq C_{4}.

Together with (
8.27
), this would yield (
8.24
) and thus the lemma. To that end, observe that

(8.29)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{t}H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y)\big|=\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{t}\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(w)dw}{w-y}\Bigg|=\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{x}\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(w)H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(w)\big)dw}{w-y}\Bigg|,

where in the second equality we used (
2.12
). Further observe for
s\in[0,n^{-\omega}]
that

(8.30)

\displaystyle\supp\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}\subseteq\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{0}+\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{\semci}^{(s)}\subseteq\big[\widetilde{G}(0,1)-2t^{1/2},\widetilde{G}(0,0)+2t^{1/2}\big]\subseteq[-4\varepsilon^{-1},4\varepsilon^{-1}],

where the first holds since
\widetilde{\mu}_{s}=\widetilde{\mu}_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(s)}
, and the second holds by (
2.9
), (
2.8
), and the fact that
\supp\widetilde{\mu}_{0}=\big[\widetilde{G}(0,1),\widetilde{G}(0,0)\big]
; and the third holds by (
8.25
). Thus, due to (
8.29
), (
8.30
), (
8.20
), and (
8.21
), to verify (
8.28
) it suffices to show the existence of a constant
C_{5}=C_{5}(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that

(8.31)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,n^{-\omega}]}\displaystyle\sup_{|y|\leq 1/C_{5}}\big|\partial_{y}^{k}H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y)\big|\leq C_{5};\qquad\text{for each $k\in\{0,1,2\}$}.

To confirm (
8.31
), fix
k\in\{0,1,2\}
, and observe that

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{y}^{k}H\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y)\big|=\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\partial_{y}^{k}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(w)dw}{w-y}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-1/C_{2}}^{1/C_{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{y}^{k}\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y+w)dw}{w}\Bigg|+\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{|w-y|>1/C_{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(w)dw}{(w-y)^{k+1}}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-1/C_{2}}^{1/C_{2}}\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{y}^{k}\big(\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y+w)-\widetilde{\varrho}_{s}(y)\big)dw}{w}\Bigg|+(2C_{2})^{3}\leq 10C_{2}^{3},

where in the first statement we used (
2.7
); in the second we changed variables in the first integral by replacing
w
with
y+w
; in the third we used the facts that
k\leq 2
and that
\widetilde{\varrho}_{t}
is a probability measure; and in the fourth we used (
8.21
) and the second bound in (
8.22
). This verifies (
8.31
), which as mentioned above establishes the lemma.
∎

Proof of
8.7
.

Let
\bm{G}(s)=\bm{G}_{M}(s)
denote an
M\times M
Hermitian Brownian motion as described in
Section

2.3
, and for every
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
let
\Lambda^{(j)}
denote the
M\times M
diagonal matrix whose
(i,i)
diagonal entry is given by
\lambda_{i}^{(j)}(0)
, for each
i\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket
. Since
\bm{\lambda}^{(1)}\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(k)}
, we have
\Lambda^{(1)}\leq\Lambda^{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq\Lambda^{(k)}
, where we write
\Lambda\leq\Lambda^{\prime}
if
\Lambda^{\prime}-\Lambda
is nonnegative definite; in particular,
\Lambda^{(1)}+\bm{G}(s)\leq\Lambda^{(2)}+\bm{G}(s)\leq\cdots\leq\Lambda^{(k)}+\bm{G}(s)
. Together with the fact that (by the first part of
2.15
)
\bm{\lambda}^{(j)}(s)
has the same law as the eigenvalues of
\Lambda^{(j)}+\bm{G}(s)
for each
j
, jointly in
s\geq 0
, this yields
\bm{\lambda}^{(1)}(s)\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq\bm{\lambda}^{(k)}(s)
for each
s\geq 0
.
∎

9.
Regularity Properties of Solutions

In this section we state and establish various regularity properties of solutions to the equation (
1.2
) governing the limit shape for non-intersecting Brownian bridges. These include comparison principles (
9.1
below); derivative bounds (
9.2
and
9.4
below); and exponential decay of discrepancy estimates (
9.5
below). We also provide a result that explains how perturbing regular solutions to (
1.2
) preserves their regularity (
9.6
below), thereby showing that
1.3
is in a sense stable under smooth perturbations of boundary data.

9.1.
Regularity Estimates

In this section we state estimates for solutions to (
1.2
). In what follows, we recall the sets of functions
\Adm(\mathfrak{R})
and
\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})
from
Section

1.3
. For any real number
r>0
and point
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we recall that
\mathcal{B}_{r}(z)=\big\{z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:|z^{\prime}-z|<r\big\}
denotes the open disk of radius
r
centered at
z
; if
z=(0,0)
, we abbreviate
\mathcal{B}_{r}(z)=\mathcal{B}_{r}
. We begin with the below maximum and comparison principles for solutions of (
1.2
), which are quick consequences of the analogous principles for solutions to nonlinear elliptic partial differential equations; it is established in
Section

E.1
below.

Lemma 9.1
.

Fix an open set
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}
, and let
F_{1},F_{2},F\in\Adm(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})
be solutions to (
1.2
) on
\mathfrak{R}
.

(1)

If
F_{1}(z)\leq F_{2}(z)
for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}
, then
F_{1}(z)\leq F_{2}(z)
for each
z\in\mathfrak{R}
.

(2)

We have
\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|\leq\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|
. In particular,
\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\big|F(z)\big|=\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|F(z)\big|
.

We next have the following lemma indicating boundedness of the derivatives for a solution to (
1.2
); it is established in
Section

E.1
below, again as a consequence of its analog in the context of elliptic partial differential equations. In what follows,
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}
is a subset that accounts for boundary regularity, in that
F
is regular in the interior of
\mathfrak{R}
but up to the boundary of
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
; in particular, if
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is empty, the below lemma is an interior regularity estimate and, if
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{R}
, then it is a global one. Here, for any sets
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we let
\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}\setminus(\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
. See the left side of
Figure

9
.

Lemma 9.2
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
; real numbers
r>0
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
B>1
; and bounded open subsets
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
has a smooth boundary, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,r,B,m,\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>1
such that the following holds. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\mathfrak{R})
be a function satisfying
\|f\|_{0}\leq B
, and let
F\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})
be a solution to (
1.2
) on
\mathfrak{R}
with boundary data
F|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}=f
. Assume that there exists a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
with
\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})}\leq B
, such that
f(z)=\varphi(z)
for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\cap\partial\mathfrak{R}
. Letting

(9.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{r}=\big\{z\in\mathfrak{R}:\dist(z,\partial\mathfrak{R}\setminus\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>r\big\},

we have
\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}\leq C
.

The following lemma states that the solutions of (
1.2
) are real analytic (that is, they have an absolutely convergent power series expansion at each point in the interior of their domains); it is shown in
Section

E.1
below, as a quick consequence of classical results stating real analyticity for solutions of elliptic partial differential equations.

Lemma 9.3
.

Fix a real number
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and some open set
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
; let
F\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})
denote a solution to (
1.2
) on
\mathfrak{R}
. Then,
F
is real analytic
\mathfrak{R}
.

Figure 9
.
Shown on the left is depiction for
9.2
. Shown in the middle is a depiction for
9.5
stating, if
F_{1}(z)=F_{2}(z)
for each
z
on the blue sides of
\mathfrak{R}
, then
F_{1}-F_{2}
is exponentially small in the shaded region. Shown on the right is a depiction for
9.6
.

We next have the following lemma bounding the effect of a
\mathcal{C}^{0}
boundary perturbation on the derivatives of a solution to (
1.2
); its proof is similar to
[
2
, Proposition 2.13]
and is given in
Section

E.2
below.

Lemma 9.4
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
; real numbers
r>0
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
B>1
; and bounded open subsets
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
has a smooth boundary, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,r,B,m,\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>1
such that the following holds. For each index
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
F_{i}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})
denote a solution to (
1.2
) such that
\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{i}(z)\big|\leq B
. Assume that there exist functions
\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{m+5}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
such that
\|\varphi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+5}(\mathfrak{R})}<B
and
F_{i}(z)=\varphi_{i}(z)
for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\cap\partial\mathfrak{R}
. Then, letting
\mathfrak{D}_{r}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}
be as in (
9.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}\leq C\big(\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}+\|\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+2}(\mathfrak{R})}\big).

The following result is established in
Section

9.2
below. It states that, given two solutions
F_{1},F_{2}
to (
1.2
) on a tall rectangle of height
1
and width
L^{-1}
(with
L
bounded below), whose boundary data match on its west and east boundaries,
|F_{1}-F_{2}|
decays exponentially in
L
in the middle of the rectangle. As such, it can be viewed as indicating that the “discrepancy” between
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
(originating from their different boundary data along the north and south sides of the rectangle) decay exponentially around the middle of the rectangle. See the middle of
Figure

9
.

Proposition 9.5
.

For any real numbers
\varepsilon,r\in\big(0,\frac{1}{4}\big)
and
B>1
, there exists a constant
c=c(\varepsilon,r,B)>0
such that the following holds. Let
L>0
be a real number, and define the open rectangle
\mathfrak{R}=(0,L^{-1})\times(-1,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
F_{1},F_{2}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{5}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
be two solutions to (
1.2
) such that
\|F_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq B
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. If
F_{1}(t,x)=F_{2}(t,x)
for any
(t,x)\in\partial\mathfrak{R}
with
t\in\{0,L^{-1}\}
, then

\displaystyle\big|F_{1}(t,x)-F_{2}(t,x)\big|\leq c^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/8}},\qquad\text{for any $(t,x)\in[0,L^{-1}]\times[r-1,1-r]$}.

We conclude this section with the following lemma, which is a consequence of the above bounds and is established in
Section

E.3
below; it essentially states the following. Fix a solution
F
to (
1.2
), bounded in
\mathcal{C}^{m}
for some integer
m
on a rectangle
\mathfrak{R}
, as well some boundary data
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
on the two vertical sides on the rectangle; assume that
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
are small perturbations of
F
(restricted to those boundaries). Then, it is possible to find a solution
G
to (
1.2
) on a slightly shorter rectangle
\mathfrak{S}
, whose boundary data on the vertical sides of the rectangle are given by
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
(the first condition in the below lemma), and that is close to
F
(quantified through the second and third conditions of the below lemma). The second part of the lemma states that
F
and
G
are close in any
\mathcal{C}^{k}
norm in the interior of
\mathfrak{S}
, and the third part states that
F
and
G
are close in
\mathcal{C}^{m-5}
(that is, fewer derivatives than the original assumed bound on
F
) up to the boundary of
\mathfrak{S}
. See the right side of
Figure

9
.

Lemma 9.6
.

For any integers
m,k\geq 7
, and real numbers
\varepsilon>0
;
r\in\big(0,\frac{1}{4}\big)
; and
B>1
, there exist constants
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,B)>0
,
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,r,B,k)>1
, and
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
such that the following holds. Fix a real number
L>2
, and define the open rectangles

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}=\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\Big)\times(-1,1);\qquad\mathfrak{S}_{r}=\Big(\displaystyle\frac{r}{L},\displaystyle\frac{1-r}{L}\Big)\times\Big(\displaystyle\frac{r+1}{L}-1,1-\displaystyle\frac{r+1}{L}\Big);\qquad\mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{S}_{0}.

Let
F\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}^{m}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
denote a solution to (
1.2
) such that
\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq B
, and define the functions
f_{0},f_{1}:[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting
f_{i}(x)=F(iL^{-1},x)
for each
(i,x)\in\{0,1\}\times[-1,1]
. Further let
g_{0},g_{1}:[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote two functions such that
\|g_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(-1,1)}\leq B
and
\big|g_{i}(x)-f_{i}(x)\big|\leq\delta L^{-1}
for each
(i,x)\in\{0,1\}\times[-1,1]
. Then, there exists a solution
G\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\mathfrak{S})\cap\mathcal{C}^{m-5}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}})
to (
1.2
) satisfying the following three properties.

(1)

For each
i\in\{0,1\}
and
x\in\big[L^{-1}-1,1-L^{-1}\big]
, we have
G(iL^{-1},x)=g_{i}(x)
.

(2)

We have
\|F-G\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathfrak{S}_{r})}\leq C_{1}L^{k}\cdot\big(\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|f_{1}-g_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\big)
.

(3)

We have
\|F-G\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-5}(\mathfrak{S})}\leq C_{2}L^{m-5}\cdot\big(\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}+\|f_{1}-g_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}\big)
and
\|F-G\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-5}(\mathfrak{S})}\leq C_{2}L^{m-5}\cdot\big(\|f_{0}-g_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}^{3/m}+\|f_{1}-g_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}^{3/m}\big)
.

9.2.
Proof of
9.5

In this section we establish
9.5
. In what follows, we fix real numbers
\varepsilon>0
,
L>1
, and
L_{0}\in\big[\frac{L}{2B},2BL\big]
. We also denote the rectangular open set
\mathfrak{R}_{0}=(0,1)\times(-L_{0},L_{0})
and let
F_{1},F_{2}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}_{0}})\cap\mathcal{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}_{0}})
denote two solutions to the equation (
1.2
).

The next lemma bounds
\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|
, for a point
z\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
such that
F_{1}=F_{2}
on the part of
\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}
near
z
; we establish it in
Section

9.3
below.

Lemma 9.7
.

For any real number
B>1
, there exist constants
c=c(\varepsilon,B)>0
and
C=C(\varepsilon,B)>1
such that the following holds. Fix a point
z=(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, such that
[0,1]\times[x_{0}-2L^{1/2},x_{0}+2L^{1/2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{0}
. Let
A\leq B
be a real number, and assume the below three statements hold.

(1)

For any indices
i\in\{1,2\}
and
j,k\in\{t,x\}
, and point
w\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, we have
\big|\nabla F_{i}(w)\big|\leq B
and
\big|\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F_{i}(w)\big|\leq BL^{-1}
; we also have
\|F_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})}-\|F_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})}\leq B
.

(2)

For any point
w\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}
with
|w-z|\leq 2L^{1/2}
, we have
F_{1}(w)=F_{2}(w)
.

(3)

For any point
w\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, we have
\big|F_{1}(w)-F_{2}(w)\big|\leq A
.

Then,
\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|\leq C(L^{-1/8}A+e^{-cL^{1/8}})
.

We can quickly prove
9.5
by repeated application of
9.7
.

Proof of
9.5
.

We may assume throughout this proof that
L
is sufficiently large (in a way only dependent on
\varepsilon
and
B
), for otherwise the lemma holds by altering the values of
c
and
C
(using the fact that
\big|F_{1}(t,x)-F_{2}(t,x)\big|\leq 2B
). Define the rescaled domain
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}=L\cdot\mathfrak{R}=(0,1)\times(-L,L)
and, for each index
i\in\{1,2\}
, set
\widehat{F}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})
by for each
w\in[0,1]\times[-L,L]
letting

\displaystyle\widehat{F}_{i}(w)=L\cdot F_{i}(L^{-1}w),\quad\text{so}\quad\nabla\widehat{F}_{i}(w)=\nabla\widehat{F}_{i}(L^{-1}w),\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\widehat{F}_{i}(w)=L^{-1}\cdot\partial_{i}\partial_{j}F_{i}(L^{-1}w),

for any indices
j,k\in\{t,x\}
. In particular, the
\widehat{F}_{i}
continue to solve (
1.2
).

It therefore suffices to show that
\big|\widehat{F}_{i}(t,x)-\widehat{F}_{2}(t,x)\big|\leq Ce^{-cL^{1/8}}
for each
(t,x)\in[0,1]\times\big[(r-1)L,(1-r)L\big]
. Denoting the open rectangle
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}=(0,1)\times(mL^{2/3}-L,L-mL^{2/3})
for any integer
m\geq 1
, we will more generaly show by induction on
m\in\llbracket 0,L^{1/8}\rrbracket
that there exist constants
c=c(\varepsilon,B)>0
and
C=C(\varepsilon,B)>1
such that

(9.2)

\displaystyle\big|\widehat{F}_{1}(t,x)-\widehat{F}_{2}(t,x)\big|\leq 2BL^{-m/9}+Cme^{-cL^{1/8}},\quad\text{for any $(t,x)\in\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}$},

from which the lemma would follow upon taking
m=\lfloor L^{1/8}\rfloor
; see the left side of
Figure

10
.

Let us first show that the
\widehat{F}_{i}
satisfy the first and third assumptions of
9.7
(with the
A
there equal to
2B
here). Since
\|F_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})}\leq B
, we have that
\big|\nabla\widehat{F}_{i}(w)\big|\leq B
and
\big|\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\widehat{F}_{i}(w)\big|\leq BL^{-1}
for each
w\in\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}
and that
\|\widehat{F}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}(\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m})}-\|\widehat{F}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m})}\leq B
; this verifies the first assumption. Moreover, since
F_{1}(t,x)=F_{2}(t,x)
for
t\in\{0,L^{-1}\}
, since
\big|\nabla F_{i}(w)\big|\leq B
, and since
\mathfrak{R}=(0,L^{-1})\times(-1,1)
we have
\big|F_{1}(w)-F_{2}(w)\big|\leq 2BL^{-1}
for each
w\in\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
. Hence,
\big|\widehat{F}_{1}(w)-\widehat{F}_{2}(w)\big|\leq 2B
for each
w\in\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}
, verifying the third assumption of
9.7
. This in particular confirms (
9.2
) at
m=0
.

We therefore assume in what follows that (
9.2
) holds at some value of
m\in\llbracket 0,L^{1/8}-1\rrbracket
and verify that it also holds with
m
replaced by
m+1
. For any integer
k\in\llbracket 0,L^{1/8}\rrbracket
, set

\displaystyle\varsigma_{k}=\sup_{w\in\mathfrak{R}_{k}}\big|\widehat{F}_{1}(w)-\widehat{F}_{2}(w)\big|.

Then the second assumption of
9.7
applies, with the
(\mathfrak{R}_{0},F_{1},F_{2},z)
there given by the
(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m},\widehat{F}_{1},\widehat{F}_{2},z)
with any
z\in\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m+1}
. Since
L-(m+1)L^{2/3}\geq\frac{L}{2B}+2L^{1/2}
for
m\in\llbracket 0,L^{1/8}\rrbracket
,
9.7
therefore yields constants
c_{1}=c_{1}(\varepsilon,B)>0
and
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,B)>1
such that

\displaystyle\varsigma_{m+1}=\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m+1}}\big|\widehat{F}_{1}(w)-\widehat{F}_{2}(w)\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}L^{-1/8}\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}}\big|\widehat{F}_{1}(w)-\widehat{F}_{2}(w)\big|+C_{1}e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}}

\displaystyle=C_{1}L^{-1/8}\varsigma_{m}+C_{1}e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}}\leq L^{-1/9}\varsigma_{m}+C_{1}e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}},

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
L
is sufficiently large. Applying this with (
9.2
), with the
(c,C)
there equal to
(c_{1},C_{1})
here yields

\displaystyle\varsigma_{m+1}\leq 2BL^{-(m+1)/9}+C_{1}mL^{-1/9}e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}}+C_{1}e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}}\leq 2BL^{-(m+1)/9}+C_{1}(m+1)e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}}.

This verifies (
9.2
) (with the
m
there replaced by
m+1
here), establishing the proposition.
∎

To establish
9.7
, we use a probabilistic interpretation of the
F_{i}
. To that end, for any real numbers
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in[-\varepsilon^{-1},\varepsilon]
, we recall from
3.19
the
2\times 2
matrix
\bm{D}=\bm{D}(u,v)
. Let
W:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
denote a standard two-dimensional Brownian motion. For any point
z\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
and index
i\in\{1,2\}
, define the process
Y_{i}=Y_{i}^{z}:[0,\tau_{i}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
through the stochastic differential equation

(9.3)

\displaystyle dY_{i}(s)=\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\Big)^{1/2}\cdot dW(s),\qquad\text{with initial data $Y_{i}(0)=z$},

so that
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are coupled through the same Brownian motion
W(s)
; see the right side of
Figure

10
for a depiction. Here, the hitting time
\tau_{i}=\tau_{i;z}
is given by

\displaystyle\tau_{i}=\inf\big\{t\geq 0:Y_{i}(s)\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}\big\}.

That the stochastic differential equation (
9.3
) is well-defined follows from the fact that the coefficients
\bm{D}\big(\nabla F_{i}(t,x)\big)^{1/2}
of the equation are uniformly Lipschitz (as
F_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}_{0}})
).

Figure 10
.
Shown to the left is the rectangle
\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}_{m}
used in the proof of
9.5
. Shown to the right are the coupled stochastic processes
Y_{1}(s)
and
Y_{2}(s)
as in (
9.3
).

Lemma 9.8
.

For each index
i\in\{1,2\}
, the process
F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)
is a martingale. In particular,

(9.4)

\displaystyle dF_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)=\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\cdot\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\Big)^{1/2}\cdot dW(s).

Proof.

Since
\tau_{i}=0
if
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, we may assume that
z\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
. Then, denoting the Hessian of
F_{i}
by

\displaystyle\bm{H}(z)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{tt}F_{i}(z)&\partial_{tx}F_{i}(z)\\
\partial_{xt}F_{i}(z)&\partial_{xx}F_{i}(z)\end{array}\right],

we have by Itô’s lemma that

\displaystyle dF_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)

\displaystyle=\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\cdot\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\Big)^{1/2}\cdot dW(s)+\frac{1}{2}\Tr\bigg(\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\Big)\bm{H}\Big(F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\cdot\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)\Big)^{1/2}\cdot dW(s),

where in the last equality we used the fact that
F_{i}
satisfies (
1.2
) (with the
(t,y)
there equal to
Y_{i}(s)
here). This yields (
9.4
) and thus that
F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)
is a martingale.
∎

Hence, we must analyze properties of the process
Y_{i}
, to which end we have the following lemma.

Lemma 9.9
.

There exists a constant
c=c(\varepsilon)>0
such that, for any real number
A>1
, index
i\in\{1,2\}
, and point
z=(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, we have

(9.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{P}[\tau_{i;z}\geq A]\leq c^{-1}e^{-cA};\qquad\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,\tau_{i;z}]}\big|Y_{i}(s)-z\big|\geq A\Big]\leq c^{-1}e^{-cA}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

Denote
W(s)=\big(W_{1}(s),W_{2}(s)\big)\in\mathbb{R}
, so that
W_{1},W_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are two independent standard Brownian motions; also let
Y_{i}(s)=\big(Y_{i;1}(s),Y_{i;2}(s)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, by (
9.3
) and the fact that
\bm{D}(u,v)=\diag(1,\pi^{2}v^{-4})
, we have
Y_{i;1}(s)-t_{0}=W_{1}(s)
, for each
s\in[0,\tau_{i;z}]
. In particular we have for any
s\leq\min\{\tau_{1;z},\tau_{2;z}\}
that

(9.6)

\displaystyle Y_{1;1}(s)=Y_{2;1}(s)=W_{1}(s)+t_{0}.

Since
\mathfrak{R}_{0}\subset[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
, it follows that
\tau_{i;z}\geq A
can only hold if
W_{i}(s)=Y_{i;1}(s)\in[-1,1]
, for each
s\in[0,A]
. Thus,

(9.7)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}[\tau_{i;z}\geq A]&\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcap_{s\in[0,A]}\big\{W_{1}(s)\in[-1,1]\big\}\Bigg]=\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcap_{k=1}^{\lfloor A\rfloor}\Big\{\big|W_{1}(k)-W_{1}(k-1)\big|\leq 2\Big\}\Bigg].\end{aligned}

Now observe that there exists a constant
c_{1}>1
such that
\mathbb{P}\big[W_{1}(k)-W_{1}(k-1)\leq 2\big]<1-c_{1}
, for any
k\geq 1
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcap_{k=1}^{\lfloor A\rfloor}

\displaystyle\Big\{\big|W_{1}(k)-W_{1}(k-1)\big|\leq 2\Big\}\Bigg]=\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{\lfloor A\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|W_{1}(k)-W_{1}(k-1)\big|\leq 2\Big]\leq(1-c_{1})^{\lfloor A\rfloor},

which together with (
9.7
) yields the first bound in (
9.5
).

Next, since
F_{i}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})
, we have
\partial_{x}F_{i}(t,x)\in[-\varepsilon^{-1},-\varepsilon]
for each
(t,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
; it follows that
\big|\bm{D}\big(\nabla F_{i}(t,x)\big)^{1/2}\big|\leq 5\varepsilon^{-2}
for each
(t,x)\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}
. By the definition (
9.3
) of
Y_{i}
(and the fact that
Y_{i}
is a time-changed Brownian motion by
[
48
, Theorem 3.4.6]
), this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,\tau_{i}]}\big|Y_{i}(s)-z\big|\geq A\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,A]}\big|W(s)\big|\geq 10\varepsilon^{-2}A\bigg]+\mathbb{P}[\tau_{i}\geq A]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\big|W(s)\big|\geq 10\varepsilon^{-2}A^{1/2}\bigg]+c^{-1}e^{-cA},

where in the last inequality we used the first bound in (
9.5
). This, together with the fact (see
[
48
, Chapter 4, Equation (3.40)]
) that there is a constant
c_{2}=c_{2}(\varepsilon)>0
such that
\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\big|W(s)\big|\leq 10\varepsilon^{-2}A^{1/2}
holds with probability at least
1-c_{2}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}A}
, implies the second bound in (
9.5
). ∎

9.3.
Proof
9.7

In this section we establish
9.7
.

Proof of
9.7
.

We may assume throughout this proof that
L>10
(for otherwise taking the constant
C=2
in the lemma gives
\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|\leq\sup_{z^{\prime}\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}}\big|F_{1}(z^{\prime})-F_{2}(z^{\prime})\big|\leq A\leq CL^{-1/8}A
, by
9.1
). Fix
M=L^{1/4}
; abbreviate
\tau_{i}=\tau_{i;z}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
; set
\tau=\min\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\}
; and define the event

(9.8)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}=\{\tau\leq M\}\cap\bigg\{\displaystyle\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,\tau_{i}]}\big|Y_{i}(s)-z\big|\leq M\bigg\},\qquad\text{so that $\mathbb{P}\big[\mathscr{A}^{\complement}\big]\leq c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}M}$},

for some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(\varepsilon)>0
, by
9.9
. Denote
W(s)=\big(W_{1}(s),W_{2}(s)\big)
and
Y_{i}(s)=\big(Y_{i;1}(s),Y_{i;2}(s)\big)
, for each index
i\in\{1,2\}
and real number
s\geq 0
. Recall from (
9.6
) that we have
Y_{1;1}(s)=W_{1}(s)+t_{0}=Y_{2;1}(s)
for each
s\in[0,\tau]
. Since on the event
\mathscr{A}
we have
Y_{i_{0}}(\tau)\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}
for some
i_{0}\in\{1,2\}
while
\big|Y_{i_{0};2}(\tau)-x_{0}\big|\leq L^{1/4}\leq L^{1/2}
, and since
(0,1)\times[x_{0}-2L^{1/2},x_{0}+2L^{1/2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, it follows that
Y_{i_{0};1}(\tau)\in\{0,1\}
(that is, the
t
-coordinate of
Y_{i_{0}}(\tau)
causes it to be on
\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}
). Hence,

(9.9)

\displaystyle Y_{1,1}(\tau)=Y_{2,1}(\tau)\in\{0,1\},\quad\text{and thus}\quad\tau_{1}=\tau_{2},\quad\text{on the event $\mathscr{A}$}.

By (
9.3
), we have

(9.10)

\displaystyle d\big(Y_{1}(t)-Y_{2}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(t)\big)\Big)^{1/2}-\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)\Big)^{1/2}\bigg)\cdot dW(t).

Thus, for any real numbers
t>0
and
p>1
, the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality
[
13
, Theorem 3.2]
(see also
[
14
, Theorem 11.2.1]
) yields

(9.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big|Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|^{2p}\bigg]\leq(72p)^{2p}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\bm{D}\big(\nabla F_{1}(Y_{1}(s)\big)^{1/2}-\bm{D}\big(\nabla F_{2}(Y_{2}(s))\big)^{1/2}\Big\|ds\bigg)^{2p}\Bigg].

To bound the integral on the right side of (
9.11
), first observe that since
\partial_{x}F_{1}(w),\partial_{x}F_{2}(w)\in[-\varepsilon^{-1},-\varepsilon]
that the
\bm{D}(\nabla F_{i})
are uniformly smooth (in a way dependent on
\varepsilon
) in
\nabla F_{i}
. Thus, there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon)>0
such that

(9.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigg\|\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{1}\big(&Y_{1}(t)\big)\Big)^{1/2}-\bm{D}\Big(\nabla F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)\Big)^{1/2}\bigg\|\\
&\leq C_{1}\Big|\nabla F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(t)\big)-\nabla F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)\Big|\\
&\leq C_{1}\bigg(\Big|\nabla F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(t)\big)-\nabla F_{1}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)\Big|+\Big|\nabla F_{1}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)-\nabla F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)\Big|\bigg)\\
&\leq C_{1}BL^{-1}\big|Y_{1}(t)-Y_{2}(t)\big|+C_{1}\Big|\nabla F_{1}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)-\nabla F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)\Big|,\end{aligned}

where in the last bound we used the first assumption of the lemma
\big|\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F_{i}(w)\big|\leq BL^{-1}
. Since we deterministically have
Y_{1}(t)-Y_{2}(t)\leq 4L+1\leq 5L
(as
\mathfrak{R}_{0}\subseteq[0,1]\times[-2L,2L]
) and
\nabla F_{i}(w)\in[-\varepsilon^{-1},-\varepsilon]
, we therefore have that

(9.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}\Bigg[\textbf{1}_{\mathscr{A}^{\complement}}\cdot\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\bm{D}\big(\nabla F_{1}(Y_{1}(s))\big)-\bm{D}\big(\nabla F_{2}(Y_{2}(s))\big)\Big\|ds\bigg)^{2p}\Bigg]&\leq\mathbb{P}\big[\mathscr{A}^{\complement}\big]\cdot(C_{1}t)^{2p}(5B+\varepsilon^{-1})^{2p}\\
&\leq(C_{2}t)^{2p}e^{-c_{1}M},\end{aligned}

for some constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,B)>1
, where in the last inequality we applied (
9.8
).

To bound the second term on the right side of (
9.12
), fix some neighborhood
\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{0}
of
Y_{2}(t)
with smooth boundary such that
[0,1]\times\big[Y_{2}(t)-1,Y_{2}(t)+1\big]\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq[0,1]\times\big[Y_{2}(t)-2,Y_{2}(t)+2\big]
. On the event
\mathscr{A}
, we have (since
M=L^{1/2}
) that
Y_{2}(t)\in[0,1]\times[x_{0}-2L^{1/2},x_{0}+2L^{1/2}]\subseteq\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, and so we may take
\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime}
to be of some fixed shape (for example, a rectangle of uniformly bounded height and width, with smoothened corners) up to translation, independently of
\varepsilon
,
A
,
B
, and
L
. Then,

(9.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \textbf{1}_{\mathscr{A}}\cdot\Big|\nabla F_{1}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)-\nabla F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(t)\big)\Big|&\leq\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime}}\big|\nabla F_{1}(w)-\nabla F_{2}(w)\big|\\
&\leq C_{3}\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime}}\big|F_{1}(w)-F_{2}(w)\big|\leq C_{3}\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}}\big|F_{1}(w)-F_{2}(w)\big|\leq C_{3}A,\end{aligned}

for some some constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,B)>1
, where in the second inequality we applied (the
m=1
case of)
9.4
(applied with both
\varphi_{i}=F_{1}|_{\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime}}
, which satisfy
\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}=0
and
\|\varphi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}(\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime})}-\|\varphi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R}_{0}^{\prime})}\leq\|F_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})}-\|F_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R}_{0})}\leq B
), in the third we applied
9.1
, and in the fourth we applied the third assumption of the lemma. Inserting this, (
9.12
), and (
9.13
) into (
9.11
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big|

\displaystyle Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|^{2p}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq(72p)^{2p}\Bigg((C_{2}t)^{2p}e^{-c_{1}M}+(2C_{1}C_{3}At)^{2p}+(2C_{1}BL^{-1})^{2p}\bigg(\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\big|Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|ds\bigg)^{2p}\Bigg).

By Markov’s inequality, we deduce the existence of a constant
C_{4}=C_{4}(\varepsilon,B)>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big|Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|\geq C_{4}pt(A+e^{-c_{1}M/2p})+C_{4}pL^{-1}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\big|Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|ds\Bigg]\leq 4^{-p}.

Taking
p=L^{1/8}
gives (after replacing
c_{1}
by
\frac{c_{1}}{2}
) with probability at least
1-4^{-L^{1/8}}
that

(9.15)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big|Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|\leq C_{4}L^{3/8}(A+e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}})+C_{4}L^{-7/8}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\big|Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|ds,

for any
t\in[0,L^{1/4}]
. Together with Gronwall’s inequality,
10
10

10

Here, we implicitly take a union bound so that (
9.15
) holds for all
t
in a
2^{-L^{1/8}}
-mesh of
[0,L^{1/8}]
, and apply the discrete Gronwall inequality
[
39
, Equation (8)]
.
this yields a constant
C_{5}=C_{5}(\varepsilon,B)>1
such that

(9.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,L^{1/2}]}\big|Y_{1}(s)-Y_{2}(s)\big|\geq C_{5}L^{3/8}(A+e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}})\bigg]\leq 2^{-L^{1/8}}.

Since by
9.8
the process
F_{i}\big(Y_{i}(s)\big)
is a martingale, we have

(9.17)

\displaystyle F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)=\mathbb{E}\Big[F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(\tau)\big)\Big].

Setting
z_{0}=z-(t_{0},0)=(0,x_{0})\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{0}
, Taylor expanding
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, and using the fact that
\big|\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F_{i}(w)\big|\leq BL^{-1}
, we find that

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(

\displaystyle\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(\tau)\big)\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[\nabla F_{2}(z_{0})\cdot\big(Y_{1}(\tau)-Y_{2}(\tau)\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\nabla F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(\tau)\big)-\nabla F_{2}(z_{0})\Big)\cdot\big(Y_{1}(\tau)-Y_{2}(\tau)\big)\bigg]\bigg|\leq\displaystyle\frac{B}{2L}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|Y_{1}(\tau)-Y_{2}(\tau)\big|^{2}\Big].

Together with (
9.17
), the fact that
\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{1}(\tau)\big]=z=\mathbb{E}\big[Y_{2}(\tau)\big]
(as
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are martingales by (
9.3
)), and again the bound
\big|\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F_{i}(w)\big|\leq BL^{-1}
, this yields

\displaystyle\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(\tau)\big)\Big]\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)\Big]\bigg|+\displaystyle\frac{B}{L}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|Y_{2}(\tau)-z_{0}\big|\cdot\big|Y_{2}(\tau)-Y_{1}(\tau)\big|\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\displaystyle\frac{B}{2L}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|Y_{1}(\tau)-Y_{2}(\tau)\big|^{2}\Big],

and hence

(9.18)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|&\leq\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)\Big]\bigg|\\
&\qquad+\displaystyle\frac{2B}{L}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\big|z_{0}-Y_{1}(\tau)\big|+\big|Y_{2}(\tau)-z_{0}\big|\Big)\cdot\big|Y_{1}(\tau)-Y_{2}(\tau)\big|\bigg].\end{aligned}

Since
F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)=F_{2}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)
on the event
\mathscr{A}
(by (
9.9
)), and since
\big|F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{2}(\tau)\big)\big|\leq 10BL
(as
\big|\nabla F_{1}(w)\big|\leq B
,
\big|\nabla F_{2}(w)\big|\leq B
, and the diameter of
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
is at most
5L
), it follows from (
9.8
) that

(9.19)

\displaystyle\bigg|\mathbb{E}\Big[F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)\Big]\bigg|=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\textbf{1}_{\mathscr{A}^{\complement}}\cdot\Big|F_{1}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)-F_{2}\big(Y_{1}(\tau)\big)\Big|\bigg]\leq 10c_{1}^{-1}BLe^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}}

Moreover, from (
9.16
), (
9.8
), and the fact that
\big|Y_{i}(\tau)-z_{0}\big|\leq M+1\leq L^{1/2}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
on
\mathscr{A}
(and again the fact that the diameter of
\mathfrak{R}_{0}
is at most
5L
), we deduce the existence of constants
c_{2}=c_{2}(\varepsilon,B)>0
and
C_{6}=C_{6}(\varepsilon,B)>1
such that

(9.20)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}&\bigg[\Big(\big|Y_{1}(\tau)-z_{0}\big|+\big|Y_{2}(\tau)-z_{0}\big|\Big)\cdot\big|Y_{1}(\tau)-Y_{2}(\tau)\big|\bigg]\\
&\leq 2L^{1/2}\cdot C_{5}L^{3/8}(A+e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}})+\big(c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}}+2^{-L^{1/8}})\cdot 2(5L)^{2}\leq C_{6}L^{7/8}A+C_{6}e^{-c_{2}L^{1/8}},\end{aligned}

where we have used the fact that, on
\mathscr{A}
, we have
|Y_{1}(\tau)-z_{0}\big|+\big|Y_{2}(\tau)-z_{0}\big|\leq 2M=2L^{1/2}
. Inserting (
9.19
) and (
9.20
) into (
9.18
) yields

\displaystyle\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|\leq 2C_{6}BL^{-1/8}A+2C_{6}BL^{-1}e^{-c_{2}L^{1/8}}+10c_{1}^{-1}BLe^{-c_{1}L^{1/8}},

which implies the lemma.
∎

Appendix A
Concentration Bounds for the Height Function

In this section we establish a concentration bound for non-intersecting Brownian bridges; it (and its proof) is analogous to the one that appears in the context of random tilings (see
[
16
, Theorem 21]
, for example). For any family of continuous curves
\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([a,b]\big)
, we recall from (
3.6
) that the associated height function
\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{H}^{\bm{x}}:[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by
\mathsf{H}(t,w)=\#\big\{j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket:x_{j}(t)>w\big\}
. In what follows, we also recall the measure
\mathfrak{Q}_{f;g}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}}
from
Definition

1.1
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer;
T>0
and
r,B\geq 1
be real numbers;
\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
be
n
-tuples; and
f,g:[0,T]\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
be measurable functions with
f\leq g
. Sample non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{x}\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,T]\big)
from the measure
\mathfrak{Q}_{f;g}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}}
. Fix real numbers
t\in[0,T]
and
w\in\big[f(t),g(t)\big]
. Denoting the event
\mathscr{E}=\big\{\mathsf{H}(t,w)\leq B\big\}
, there exists a deterministic number
\mathfrak{Y}=\mathfrak{Y}(\bm{u};\bm{v};f;g;T;t;w;B)\geq 0
such that

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\mathsf{H}(t,w)-\mathfrak{Y}\big|\geq rB^{1/2}\Big]\leq 2e^{-r^{2}/4}+2\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[\mathscr{E}^{\complement}\big].

In particular, setting
B=n
, we have
\mathbb{P}\big[\big|\mathsf{H}(t,w)-\mathfrak{Y}\big|\geq rn^{1/2}\big]\leq 2e^{-r^{2}/4}
.

To establish
A.1
, we proceed through a discretization. For any integer
\mathsf{S}\geq 0
, a
discrete path
is a function
\mathsf{p}:\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
such that
\mathsf{p}(t)-\mathsf{p}(t-1)\in\{0,1\}
, for each
t\in\llbracket 1,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket
. A
discrete path ensemble
on
\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket
is an ordered sequence of discrete paths
\bm{\mathsf{p}}=(\mathsf{p}_{1},\mathsf{p}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{p}_{k})
, with
\mathsf{p}_{j}:\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
. Given integer sequences
\bm{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},\ldots,u_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
and
\bm{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we say that
\bm{\mathsf{p}}

starts
at
\bm{u}
and
ends
at
\bm{v}
if
\mathsf{p}_{j}(0)=u_{j}
and
\mathsf{p}_{j}(\mathsf{S})=v_{j}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, respectively; observe that this is only possible if
u_{j}\leq v_{j}\leq u_{j}+\mathsf{S}
for each
j
. Given two functions
f,g:\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
, we further say that the ensemble
\bm{\mathsf{p}}

remains above
f
and
remains below
g
if
f(s)<\mathsf{p}_{j}(s)
and
\mathsf{p}_{j}(s)<g(s)
for each
(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket
, respectively. See the left side of
Figure

11
for a depiction.

Figure 11
.
Shown to the left is a discrete path ensemble
{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}=({\mathsf{p}}_{1},{\mathsf{p}}_{2},{\mathsf{p}}_{3})
. Shown to the right is a level set decomposition
\mathscr{D}=\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{S}_{3}
; there,
(3,2,0)
is an exit sequence of
\mathcal{S}_{3}
.

Denoting
\bm{\mathsf{p}}(t)=\big(\mathsf{p}_{1}(t),\mathsf{p}_{2}(t),\ldots,\mathsf{p}_{k}(t)\big)
for any
t
, we additionally say that the ensemble
\bm{\mathsf{p}}
is
non-intersecting
if
\bm{\mathsf{p}}(t)\in\mathbb{W}_{k}
, for each
t\in\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket
. Given a non-intersecting path ensemble
\bm{\mathsf{p}}=(\mathsf{p}_{1},\mathsf{p}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{p}_{k})
on
\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket
, we define its height function
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}:\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
by, for any
(t,w)\in\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}
, setting

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(t,w)=\#\big\{j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket:\mathsf{p}_{j}(t)>w\big\}.

Given the above notation, we can state the discrete variant of
A.1
that quickly implies it. Observe that, in the below concentration bound, the error estimates and probabilities do not depend on the size
\mathsf{S}
of the domain; this is in contrast to analogous statements in the context of tilings (for example, in
[
16
, Theorem 21]
, the error in the height function grows at least as
\mathsf{S}^{1/2}
), though the proof is similar.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
n,\mathsf{S}\geq 1
be integers;
r,B\geq 1
be real numbers;
\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\mathbb{W}_{n}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be two
n
-tuples of integers; and
f,g:\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
be functions with
f\leq g
. Let
\bm{\mathsf{p}}=(\mathsf{p}_{1},\mathsf{p}_{2},\ldots,\mathsf{p}_{n})
denote a uniformly random discrete path ensemble conditioned to start at
\bm{u}
; end at
\bm{v}
; remain above
f
and below
g
; and to be non-intersecting (assuming at least one such ensemble exists). Fixing
(t,w)\in\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}
, and denoting the event
\mathscr{E}(\bm{\mathsf{p}})=\big\{\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(t,w)\leq B\big\}
, there exists a deterministic real number
\mathfrak{Y}=\mathfrak{Y}(\bm{u};\bm{v};f;g;t;w;B)\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(t,w)-\mathfrak{Y}\big|\geq rB^{1/2}\Big]\leq 2e^{-r^{2}/4}+2\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[\mathscr{E}(\bm{\mathsf{p}})^{\complement}\big].

Proof.

This proof follows the discussion in
[
3
, Section 3.2]
. Let
\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{p}_{1}^{\prime},\mathsf{p}_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,\mathsf{p}_{n}^{\prime})
denote a non-intersecting discrete path ensemble with the same law as, but independent from,
\bm{\mathsf{p}}
. Denote
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{D}(\mathsf{S})=\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
; for any subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
, the boundary
\partial\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{S}
denotes the set of vertices
(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}
that are adjacent
11
11

11

Here, two vertices
(s,y)
and
(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
are adjacent if
(s-s^{\prime},y-y^{\prime})\in\big\{(0,1),(1,0),(1,1),(0,-1),(-1,0),(-1,-1)\big\}
(in this way, we view vertices as on a triangular lattice, instead of on a square one).
to a vertex in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{S}
. Define the function
F:\mathscr{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
by setting
F(s,y)=\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(s,y)-\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(s,y)
, for each
(s,y)\in\mathscr{D}
.

A family
\bm{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathcal{S}_{0},\mathcal{S}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{S}_{k})
of mutually disjoint subsets of
\mathscr{D}
satisfying
\bigcup_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{S}_{j}=\mathscr{D}
is a
level set decomposition
of
\mathscr{D}
with respect to
F
if the following two properties hold. First, for any two vertices
(s,y),(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{j}
of the same subset,
F(s,y)=F(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. Second, for any two adjacent vertices
(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}_{j}
and
(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S}_{j^{\prime}}
with
j\neq j^{\prime}
(namely, in different subsets),
F(s,y)\neq F(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
; observe in this case that
\big|F(s,y)-F(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\big|=1
, as we either have that both
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(s,y)-\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(s,y)-\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\{0,1\}
hold or that both
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(s,y)-\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\{0,-1\}
and
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(s,y)-\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\{0,-1\}
hold. We set
F(\mathcal{S}_{j})=F(s,y)
, for any
(s,y)\in\mathcal{S}_{j}
. In the below, we condition on the level set decomposition
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
of
\mathscr{D}
with respect to
F
.

Further observe that
F(s,y)=0
for any
(s,y)\in\llbracket 0,\mathsf{S}\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}
that either satisfies
(s,y)\in\partial\mathscr{D}
(since
\bm{\mathsf{p}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}
both start and end at the same
n
-tuples
\bm{u}
and
\bm{v}
, respectively), or that satisfies
y<f(s)
(as then
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(s,y)=n=\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(s,y)
) or
y\geq g(s)
(as then
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(s,y)=0=\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(s,y)
). Since this family of vertices is connected, there is one infinite set in
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
, which we let be
\mathcal{S}_{0}
. It has the property that
\mathscr{D}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{0}
is finite, so the number of elements
k+1
in
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
is finite.

Next, given
i,j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
with
i\neq j
, we say that
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is
adjacent
to
\mathcal{S}_{j}
if there exists a vertex in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
that is adjacent to a vertex in
\mathcal{S}_{j}
. In this case, we say that
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is
exterior adjacent
to
\mathcal{S}_{j}
if
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is contained in the infinite connected component of
\mathscr{D}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{j}
; we otherwise call
\mathcal{S}_{i}

interior adjacent
to
\mathcal{S}_{j}
. In particular,
\mathcal{S}_{0}
is exterior adjacent to any
\mathcal{S}_{j}
adjacent to it. It is quickly verified (see
[
3
, Lemma 12]
) that, for every
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, there is at most one element of
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
that is exterior adjacent to
\mathcal{S}_{j}
. See the right side of
Figure

11
for a depiction.

An
exit sequence
of any
\mathcal{S}_{j}\in\bm{\mathcal{S}}
is an ordered sequence
(i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
of integers in
\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
so that
i_{0}=j
,
i_{m}=0
, and
\mathcal{S}_{i_{a}}
is exterior adjacent to
\mathcal{S}_{i_{a-1}}
, for each
a\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
; here,
m=m(j)=m(j;\bm{\mathcal{S}})
depends on
j
and
\bm{\mathcal{S}}
. Then, it is quickly verified (see the proof of
[
3
, Lemma 14]
) that
F(\mathcal{S}_{j})
has the same law as
\sum_{a=1}^{m}\xi_{a}
, where the
\xi_{a}
are mutually independent
\{-1,1\}
-Bernoulli random variables with
\mathbb{P}[\xi_{a}=1]=\frac{1}{2}=\mathbb{P}[\xi_{a}=-1]
. Hence, by Chernoff’s inequality,

(A.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\Big|F(\mathcal{S}_{j})-\mathbb{E}\big[F(\mathcal{S}_{j})\big]\Big|\geq rm^{1/2}\bigg|\bm{\mathcal{S}}\bigg]\leq 2e^{-r^{2}/2}.

Now fix
j\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
to be such that
(t,w)\in\mathcal{S}_{j}
. Observe on the event
\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{E}(\bm{\mathsf{p}})\cap\mathscr{E}(\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime})=\big\{\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(t,w)\leq B\big\}\cap\big\{\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(t,w)\leq B\big\}
that
m(j;\bm{\mathcal{S}})\leq 2B
. Indeed, define a sequence
(w_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{m})
by first setting
w_{0}=w
and then defining
w_{i+1}
for
i\in\llbracket 0,m-1\rrbracket
inductively as follows. Let
j_{i}\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
be such that
(t,w_{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{j_{i}}
(so
j_{0}=j
), and let
w_{i+1}>w_{i}
be the maximum integer such that
(t,w_{i+1})\notin\mathcal{S}_{j_{i}}
but
(t,w_{i+1}-1)\in\mathcal{S}_{j_{i}}
(assuming
j_{i}\neq 0
; if instead
j_{i}=0
then the sequence stops). Then,
(j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{m})
is an exit sequence for
\mathcal{S}_{j}
. Moreover, for each
i\in\llbracket 0,m-1\rrbracket
, we either have
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(t,w_{i+1})=\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(t,w_{i})-1
or
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(t,w_{i+1})=\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(t,w_{i})-1
, since
F(t,w_{i-1})\neq F(t,w_{i})
and since
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}(t,y)
and
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}(t,y)
are nonincreasing in
y
. On the event
\mathscr{E}
, at most
B
such decrements can occur for
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}}
and for
\mathsf{H}^{\bm{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}}
, meaning that
m\leq 2B
.

Combining this estimate
\textbf{1}_{\mathscr{E}}\cdot m(j;\bm{\mathcal{S}})\leq 2B
with (
A.2
) and a union bound, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\Big|F(\mathcal{S}_{j})-\mathbb{E}\big[F(\mathcal{S}_{j})\big]\Big|\geq r(2B)^{1/2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg[\textbf{1}_{\mathscr{E}}\cdot\Big|F(\mathcal{S}_{j})-\mathbb{E}\big[F(\mathcal{S}_{j})\big]\Big|\geq r(2B)^{1/2}\bigg|\bm{\mathcal{S}}\bigg]+\mathbb{P}\big[\mathscr{E}^{\complement}\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2e^{-r^{2}/2}+2\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[\mathscr{E}(\bm{\mathsf{p}})^{\complement}\big],

which gives the lemma.
∎

Proof of
A.1
.

The second statement follows from the first, since
\mathsf{H}(t,w)\leq n
holds deterministically. The first statement follows from
A.2
and the fact that non-intersecting random discrete paths converge to non-intersecting Brownian motions (namely, the
\big(T^{1/2}\mathsf{S}^{-1/2}\cdot\mathsf{p}_{j}(\lfloor sT^{-1}\mathsf{S}\rfloor)\big)
from
A.2
over
(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,T]
converges in law to the
\big(x_{j}(s)\big)
over
(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,T]
from
A.1
).
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of Results From
Section

2

B.1.
Proof of
2.7

In this section we establish
2.7
. We begin with the following lemma that bounds non-intersecting Brownian bridges constrained to lie below a linear lower boundary; see the left side of
Figure

12
for a depiction. In the below, we let
\bm{0}_{n}=(0,0,\ldots,0)\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
(where
0
appears with multiplicity
n
).

Figure 12
.
The left panel depicts
B.1
. The right panel depicts its proof by coupling
\bm{x}
with the non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{z}
.

Lemma B.1
.

There exist constants
c>0
and
C>1
such that the following holds. Fix an integer
n\geq 1
; real numbers
A\geq 0
,
T>0
, and
v\geq AT
; and define
g:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting
g(x)=-Ax
, for each
x\in[0,T]
. Let
\bm{v}=(-v,-v,\ldots,-v)\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
(where
-v
appears with multiplicity
n
), and sample non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,T]\big)
under the measure
\mathfrak{Q}_{-\infty;g}^{\bm{0}_{n};\bm{v}}
. Then, for any real numbers
t\in[0,T]
and
B\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[x_{n}(t)\leq-vtT^{-1}-Bt^{1/2}\log|9t^{-1}T|\Big]\leq Ce^{Cn-cB^{2}n}.

Proof.

In view of the invariance of non-intersecting Brownian bridges under affine transformations and diffusive scaling (recall
2.1
and
2.2
), we may assume that
T=1
. Then, set
\theta=\frac{B}{8}\cdot t^{1/2}\log|9t^{-1}|
and
v^{\prime}=(t^{-1}+1)\theta+v
; denote
\bm{u}=(-\theta,-\theta,\ldots,-\theta)\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
and
\bm{v}^{\prime}=(-v^{\prime},-v^{\prime},\ldots,-v^{\prime})\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
. Sample non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,T]\big)
and
\bm{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,T]\big)
from the measures
\mathfrak{Q}_{-\infty;g}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{Q}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}^{\prime}}
, respectively; see the right side of
Figure

12
. By
2.4
, we may couple
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
so that

(B.1)

\displaystyle\big|y_{j}(s)-x_{j}(s)\big|\leq(t^{-1}s+1)\theta,\qquad\text{for each $(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,1]$}.

Now, let us compare
\bm{y}
and
\bm{z}
. To that end, first observe from
2.6
that there exist constants
c\in(0,1)
and
C_{1}>1
such that

(B.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}\bigcap_{s\in[0,1]}\Big\{z_{j}(s)\geq-\displaystyle\frac{B}{8}\cdot s^{1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|-(t^{-1}s+1)\theta-vs\Big\}\Bigg]\geq 1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-cB^{2}n};\\
&\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcap_{j=1}^{n}\bigcap_{s\in[0,1]}\Big\{z_{j}(s)\leq\displaystyle\frac{B}{8}\cdot s^{1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|-(t^{-1}s+1)\theta-vs\Big\}\Bigg]\geq 1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-cB^{2}n}.\end{aligned}

Observe that
\frac{B}{8}\cdot s^{1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|-(t^{-1}s+1)\theta-vs\leq-As
, for each
s\in[0,1]
. Indeed, if
s\leq t
, this follows from the facts that
v\geq A
and
t^{1/2}\log|9t^{-1}|\geq s^{1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|
when
0<s\leq t\leq 1
; for
s>t
, this follows from the facts that
v\geq A
and
t^{-1/2}\log|9t^{-1}|\geq s^{-1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|
when
0<t\leq s\leq 1
. Hence, the second statement of (
B.2
) indicates that
z_{j}(s)
remains below
g
with probability at least
1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-cB^{2}}
. It follows that we may couple
\bm{z}
and
\bm{y}
to coincide on an event of this probability
1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-cB^{2}}
. Together with (
B.1
), the first part of (
B.2
), and a union bound, this gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[x_{n}(t)\geq-4\theta-vt-\displaystyle\frac{B}{8}\cdot t^{1/2}\log|9t^{-1}|\Big]\geq 1-2C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-cB^{2}n},

which yields the lemma, since
\theta=\frac{B}{8}\cdot t^{1/2}\log|9t^{-1}|
.
∎

We then deduce the following variant of
2.7
that proves high probability Hölder bounds for a single choice of
(j,s,t)
, which quickly implies
2.7
. We detail the proof of the first part and outline that of the second, which is similar.

Lemma B.2
.

Adopt the notation of
2.7
.

(1)

Under the assumption of part
1
of
2.7
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)-sT^{-1}(v_{j}-u_{j})\big|\geq s^{1/2}\big(B\log|2s^{-1}T|+\kappa^{-1}(A+B)\big)\Big]\leq Ce^{Cn^{2}-cB^{2}n}.

(2)

Under the assumption of part
2
of
2.7
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)-sT^{-1}(v_{j}-u_{j})\big|\geq s^{1/2}\big(B\log|9s^{-1}T|+2\big(A+B)\big)\Big]\leq Ce^{Cn^{2}-cB^{2}n}.

Proof of
B.2
(
1
).

By
2.2
and
2.1
, we may assume that
(a,b)=(0,1)
and that
(u_{j},v_{j})=(0,0)
. Since
(sT^{-1})^{1/2}\leq 1
(as
s\leq T
), it suffices to show that

(B.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{P}\big[x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\geq Bs^{1/2}\log|2s^{-1}|+\kappa^{-1}s(A+B)\big]\leq Ce^{Cn^{2}-cB^{2}n};\\
&\mathbb{P}\big[x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\leq-Bs^{1/2}\log|2s^{-1}|-\kappa^{-1}s(A+B)\big]\leq Ce^{Cn^{2}-cB^{2}n}.\end{aligned}

We only establish the first bound in (
B.3
), as then that of the second would follow by symmetry (namely, by reflecting the bridges into the line
\big\{t=\frac{1}{2}\big\}
). To that end, we first claim that there exist constants
c_{1}\in(0,1)
and
C_{1}>1
such that

(B.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,1]}x_{j}(r)\leq A+B\Bigg]\geq 1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-c_{1}B^{2}n}.

To see this, define
\bm{u}^{\prime},\bm{v}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
by setting

\displaystyle u_{k}^{\prime}=u_{k}+\displaystyle\frac{B}{2}+A;\qquad v_{k}^{\prime}=v_{k}+\displaystyle\frac{B}{2}+A,

for each
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. Then, sample non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{n})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,1]\big)
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}=(y_{1}^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,y_{n}^{\prime})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,1]\big)
according to the measures
\mathfrak{Q}_{f}^{\bm{u}^{\prime};\bm{v}^{\prime}}
and
\mathfrak{Q}^{\bm{u}^{\prime};\bm{v}^{\prime}}
, respectively. Then, by
2.3
, we may couple
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
so that

(B.5)

\displaystyle x_{k}(r)\leq y_{k}(r),\qquad\text{for each $(k,r)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,1]$}.

Moreover, by
2.6
, there exist constants
c_{1}\in(0,1)
and
C_{2}>1
such that

(B.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\displaystyle\inf_{r\in[0,1]}y_{n}^{\prime}(r)\geq\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,1]}f(r)+\displaystyle\frac{B}{4}\Bigg]\geq 1-C_{2}e^{C_{2}n-c_{1}B^{2}n};\\
&\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,1]}y_{j}^{\prime}(r)\leq A+B\Bigg]\geq 1-C_{2}e^{C_{2}n-c_{1}B^{2}n},\end{aligned}

where in the first inequality we used that
\min\{u_{n}^{\prime},v_{n}^{\prime}\}=\min\{u_{n},v_{n}\}+A+\frac{B}{2}\geq\sup_{r\in[0,1]}f(r)+\frac{B}{2}
, and in the second inequality we used the fact that
u_{j}^{\prime}=A+\frac{B}{2}=v_{j}^{\prime}
(recalling that
u_{j}=v_{j}=0
).

The first bound in (
B.6
) indicates that
\bm{y}^{\prime}
lies above
f
with probability at least
1-C_{2}e^{C_{2}n-c_{1}B^{2}n}
. Hence, we may couple
\bm{y}
and
\bm{y}^{\prime}
so that they coincide with probability at least
1-C_{2}e^{C_{2}n-c_{1}B^{2}n}
. This, together with the second bound in (
B.6
), (
B.5
), and a union bound then yields (
B.4
).

Figure 13
.
The left panel depicts the proof of
B.2
(
1
). The right panel depicts that of
B.2
(
2
).

Now, to show the first bound in (
B.3
), we condition on
x_{k}(r)
for
(k,r)\in\llbracket j+1,n\rrbracket\times[0,1]
and for
(k,r)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[t+s,1]
, and set
\bm{u}^{\prime\prime},\bm{v}^{\prime\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n-j+1}
by letting
u_{k}^{\prime\prime}=x_{j}(0)=0
and
v_{k}^{\prime\prime}=x_{j}(t+s)
, for each
k\in\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket
. Sample non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{j})\in\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,t+s]\big)
from the measure
\mathfrak{Q}^{\bm{u}^{\prime\prime};\bm{v}^{\prime\prime}}(n^{-1})
. Then,
2.3
yields a coupling between
\bm{x}
and
\bm{z}
such that
x_{k}(r)\geq z_{k}(r)
, for each
(k,r)\in\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket\times[0,1]
; see the left side of
Figure

13
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[x_{j}(t)\leq x_{j}(

\displaystyle t+s)-Bs^{1/2}\log|2s^{-1}|-\displaystyle\frac{s(A+B)}{\kappa}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg[z_{j}(t)\leq z_{j}(t+s)-Bs^{1/2}\log|2s^{-1}|-\displaystyle\frac{s(A+B)}{\kappa}\bigg]\leq 2C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-c_{1}B^{2}n},

where in the last equality we used
2.6
and the facts that
s+t\geq t\geq\kappa
and that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{v_{j}^{\prime\prime}-u_{j}^{\prime\prime}}{s+t}=(s+t)^{-1}\cdot z_{j}(t+s)\leq(s+t)^{-1}\cdot\max_{r\in[0,1]}x_{j}(r)\leq\displaystyle\frac{A+B}{\kappa},

on the event
\big\{\max_{r\in[0,1]}x_{j}(r)\leq A+B\big\}
. This, with (
B.4
), establishes the first bound in (
B.3
) and thus the first statement of the lemma.
∎

Proof of
B.2
(
2
).

Since the proof of the second part of the lemma is similar to that of the first part, we only outline it. We may again assume that
(a,b)=(0,1)
and
(u_{j},v_{j})=(0,0)
. We may further assume by symmetry that
t\leq\frac{1}{2}
. It suffices (again since
(sT^{-1})^{1/2}\leq 1
) to show that

(B.7)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathbb{P}\big[x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\geq Bs^{1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|+2s(A+B)\big]\leq Ce^{Cn^{2}-cB^{2}n};\\
&\mathbb{P}\big[x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\leq-Bs^{1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|-2s(A+B)\big]\leq Ce^{Cn^{2}-cB^{2}n},\end{aligned}

and by symmetry it suffices to show the latter. Following the derivation of (
B.4
), we again find that there exist constants
c_{1}\in(0,1)
and
C_{1}>1
such that

(B.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[0,1]}x_{j}(r)\leq A+B\Bigg]\geq 1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-c_{1}B^{2}n}.

Now condition on
x_{k}(r)
for
(k,r)\in\llbracket j+1,n\rrbracket\times[0,1]
and for
(k,r)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,t]
; set
\widetilde{g}:[t,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by letting
\widetilde{g}(r)=x_{j}(t)+(t-r)A
, for each
r\in[t,1]
; and set
\bm{u}^{\prime},\bm{v}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{j}
by letting
u_{k}^{\prime}=x_{j}(t)
and
v_{k}^{\prime}=\min\{x_{j}(1),\widetilde{g}(1)\}=\min\{0,\widetilde{g}(1)\}
(where we used that
x_{j}(1)=v_{j}=0
) for each
k\in\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket
. Then sample non-intersecting Brownian bridges
\bm{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{j})\in\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([t,1]\big)
from the measure
\mathfrak{Q}_{-\infty;\tilde{g}}^{\bm{u}^{\prime};\bm{v}^{\prime}}(n^{-1})
; see the right side of
Figure

13
. Since
\big|\partial_{t}g(r)\big|\leq A
for each
r\in[t,1]
, and since
g(t)\geq x_{j}(t)=u_{j}^{\prime}
, we have
g\geq\widetilde{g}
. Thus,
2.3
yields a coupling between
\bm{x}
and
\bm{y}
such that

(B.9)

\displaystyle x_{k}(r)\geq y_{k}(r),\qquad\text{for each $(k,r)\in\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket\times[t,1]$}.

Applying
B.1
to
\bm{y}
yields constants
c_{1}\in(0,1)
and
C_{2}>1
such that

(B.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[y_{j}(t+s)\geq y_{j}(t)-\displaystyle\frac{(u_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j}^{\prime})s}{1-t}-Bs^{1/2}\log|9s^{-1}|\bigg]\geq 1-C_{2}e^{C_{2}n-c_{1}B^{2}n}.

If
v_{j}^{\prime}=\widetilde{g}(1)=u_{j}^{\prime}-A(1-t)
, then
u_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j}^{\prime}=A(1-t)\leq A
. If instead
v_{j}^{\prime}=0
, and (
B.8
) implies

\mathbb{P}[u_{j}^{\prime}\leq A+B]\geq 1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-c_{1}B^{2}n}
; since
t\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we have in either case that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{u_{j}^{\prime}-v_{j}^{\prime}}{1-t}\leq 2(A+B)\bigg]\geq 1-C_{1}e^{C_{1}n-cB^{2}n}.

Together with (
B.10
), (
B.9
), and a union bound, this gives the second bound in (
B.7
), which implies the second statement of the lemma.
∎

Proof of
2.7
(Outline).

The proof follows quickly from
B.2
and (the proof of)
[
19
, Lemma 3.3]
, very similarly to the proof of
[
19
, Proposition 3.5]
given
[
19
, Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3]
, so further details are omitted.
∎

B.2.
Proofs of
2.10
, and
2.11

In this section we establish first
2.10
, and next
2.11
.

Proof of
2.10
.

By
[
8
, Lemma 3]
, we have
\big|m_{t}^{\mu}(z)\big|\leq t^{-1/2}
for any
z\in\overline{\Lambda}_{t;\mu}
. By (
2.6
), this yields
\varrho_{t}(x)\leq\pi^{-1}t^{-1/2}
, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, confirming the first statement of the lemma.

To verify the latter, fix
x\in\mathbb{R}
and set
z\in\partial\Lambda_{t}
such that
M(z)=z-tm_{0}(z)=x\in\mathbb{R}
(where such a
z
exists by
2.8
). Then, (
2.6
) and (
2.11
) give

(B.11)

\displaystyle\varrho_{t}(x)=\pi^{-1}\Imaginary m_{t}\big(z-tm_{0}(z)\big)=\pi^{-1}\Imaginary m_{0}(z).

Let
z_{0}\in\partial\Lambda_{t}
be such that
M(z_{0})=x_{0}
. Repeatedly applying the relation
\partial_{x}=\big(\frac{\partial_{z}}{\partial_{x}}\big)^{-1}\partial_{z}=(1-tm_{0}^{\prime})^{-1}\partial_{z}
, it follows that there exists a constant
C=C(k)>1
such that

(B.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|\partial_{x}^{k}\varrho_{t}(x_{0})\big|&\leq C\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket}\big|\partial_{z}^{k^{\prime}}m_{0}(z_{0})\big|^{2k/k^{\prime}}\cdot\displaystyle\max_{k^{\prime\prime}\in\llbracket 0,2k\rrbracket}\big|1-tm_{0}^{\prime}(z_{0})\big|^{-k^{\prime\prime}}.\end{aligned}

To bound the first term on the right side, observe since
|z_{0}-y|\geq\Imaginary z_{0}
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
that

(B.13)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{z}^{k}m_{0}(z_{0})\big|\leq k!\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{|z_{0}-y|^{k+1}}\leq\frac{k!}{(\Imaginary z_{0})^{k-1}}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{|z_{0}-y|^{2}}=k!\cdot\frac{\Imaginary m_{0}(z_{0})}{(\Imaginary z_{0})^{k}}\leq\frac{k!}{t(t\delta\pi)^{k-1}},

where in the last two statements we used the facts that

(B.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\qquad\Imaginary m_{0}(z_{0})=\pi\varrho_{t}(x_{0})\geq\pi\delta;\qquad\Imaginary z_{0}=t\Imaginary m_{0}(z_{0});\\
&\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{|z_{0}-y|^{2}}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Imaginary z_{0}}\cdot\Imaginary\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{z_{0}-y}=\displaystyle\frac{\Imaginary m_{0}(z_{0})}{\Imaginary z_{0}}=t^{-1},\end{aligned}

Here, the first statement follows from (
B.11
) and the assumption
\varrho_{t}(x_{0})\geq\delta
