; and the second follows from the fact that
M(z_{0})=z_{0}-tm_{0}(z_{0})=x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
; and the third follows from the definition of
m_{0}
.

To estimate the last term on the right side of (
B.12
), observe that

(B.15)

\displaystyle\big|1-tm^{\prime}_{0}(z_{0})\big|

\displaystyle\geq 1-t\Real m^{\prime}_{0}(z_{0}),

and that

\displaystyle\Real m^{\prime}_{0}(z_{0})=\Real\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{(y-z_{0})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(|y-z_{0}|^{2}-2(\Imaginary z_{0})^{2}\big)\cdot\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{|y-z_{0}|^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}-2(\Imaginary z_{0})^{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{|y-z_{0}|^{4}},

where in the last equality we used the third statement of (
B.14
). Inserting this into (
B.15
), we find

(B.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big|1-tm^{\prime}_{0}(z_{0})\big|&\geq 2t(\Imaginary z_{0})^{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{|y-z_{0}|^{4}}\geq 2t(\Imaginary z_{0})^{2}\left(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(y)dy}{|y-z_{0}|^{2}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{2(\Imaginary z_{0})^{2}}{t}\geq 2t(\pi\delta)^{2},\end{split}

where in the second statement we used the fact that
\varrho_{0}
is a probability density; in the third we used the third statement of (
B.14
); and in the fourth we used the first two statements of (
B.14
). The lemma then follows from inserting (
B.13
) and (
B.16
) into (
B.12
).
∎

Proof of
2.11
.

Denote the Stieltjes transform of
\mu_{0}
by
m_{0}=m_{\mu_{0}}
. We will use (
B.12
) to estimate the
x
-derivatives of
\varrho_{t}
, to which end we must upper bound derivatives of
m_{0}
and lower bound
|1-tm_{0}^{\prime}|
. To implement this, we claim that there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(k)>1
such that, for any integer
j\in\llbracket 0,k-1\rrbracket
,

(B.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\big|\partial_{z}^{j}m_{0}(z)\big|\leq C_{1}B^{j+2}(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{-j-1},\qquad\text{for any $z\in\overline{\mathbb{H}}$ such that $|\Real z|\leq\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}$};\\
&\big|1-tm_{0}^{\prime}(z)\big|\geq\displaystyle\frac{t\delta\mathfrak{c}}{2\Imaginary z},\qquad\qquad\quad\text{for any $z\in\overline{\mathbb{H}}$ such that $|\Real z|\leq\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}$ and $\Imaginary z\in[0,\mathfrak{c}]$}.\end{aligned}

To see this, fix
j\in\llbracket 0,k-1\rrbracket
and observe that (
2.5
) implies for
|\Real z|<\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
that

(B.18)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|\partial_{z}^{j}m_{0}(z)\big|=j!\Bigg|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(x)dx}{(x-z)^{j+1}}\Bigg|&\leq j!\Bigg|\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}\frac{\varrho_{0}(x)dx}{(x-z)^{j+1}}\Bigg|+2^{j+1}j!\mathfrak{c}^{-j-1}\\
&\leq\bigg|\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}\frac{\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{0}(x)dx}{x-z}\bigg|+2^{j+1}j!\mathfrak{c}^{-j-1}+2^{j+1}(j+1)!B^{j+1}(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{-j}.\end{aligned}

Here, to obtain the second statement we used the facts that
|\Real z|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
and that
\varrho_{0}
is the density of a probability measure. To obtain the third, we integrated by parts
j
times, using the estimate on the boundary terms,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}i!\bigg|\frac{\partial_{x}^{j-i}\varrho_{0}(\mathfrak{c})}{(z-\mathfrak{c})^{i}}-\frac{\partial_{x}^{j-i}\varrho_{0}(-\mathfrak{c})}{(z+\mathfrak{c})^{i}}\bigg|\leq\displaystyle\frac{2^{j}}{\mathfrak{c}^{j}}\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{j}i!\Big(\big|\partial_{x}^{j-i}\varrho_{0}(\mathfrak{c})\big|+\big|\partial_{x}^{j-i}\varrho_{0}(-\mathfrak{c})\big|\Big)\leq\displaystyle\frac{2^{j+1}(j+1)!B^{j+1}}{(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{j}},

where the first bound holds again since
|\Real z|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
and the second holds since
\big|\partial_{x}^{i}\varrho_{0}(x)\big|\leq B^{i+1}\delta^{-i}
for any
i\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
. Then,

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{z}^{j}m_{0}(z)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}\bigg|\frac{\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{0}(x)-\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{0}(\Real z)}{z-x}\bigg|dx+\big|\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{0}(\Real z)\big|\cdot\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}\displaystyle\frac{dx}{x-z}\Bigg|+\mathcal{O}\big(B^{j+1}(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{-j}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}\bigg|\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{0}(x)-\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{0}(\Real z)}{x-z}\bigg|dx+\mathcal{O}\big(B^{j+1}(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{-j}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathfrak{c}\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq\mathfrak{c}}\big|\partial_{x}^{j+1}\varrho_{0}(x)\big|+\mathcal{O}\big(B^{j+1}\big(\mathfrak{c}\delta)^{-j-1}\big)\leq\mathcal{O}\big(B^{j+2}(\mathfrak{c}\delta)^{-j-1}\big),

where in the first and second estimates we used (
B.18
), the bound
\big|\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{0}(x)\big|\leq B^{j}\delta^{-j}
for
|x|\leq\mathfrak{c}
(as
|\Real z|\leq\mathfrak{c}
), and the fact that
\big|\PV\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}(x-z)^{-1}dx\big|\leq 2
(as
|\Real z|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
); in the fourth, we used the same bound on derivatives of
\varrho_{0}
, but with
j
replaced by
j+1
. Here, the implicit constant only depends on
j
, verifying the first statement of (
B.17
).

To verify the second, observe for
|\Real z|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
and
\Imaginary z\in[0,\mathfrak{c}]
that

\displaystyle\big|1-tm^{\prime}_{0}(z)\big|\geq 2t(\Imaginary z)^{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(x)dx}{|x-z|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq 2t\delta(\Imaginary z)^{2}\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}\frac{dx}{|x-z|^{4}}

\displaystyle\geq 2t\delta(\Imaginary z)^{2}\displaystyle\int_{-\mathfrak{c}/2}^{\mathfrak{c}/2}\displaystyle\frac{dx}{\big(|x|+\Imaginary z\big)^{4}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}\cdot t\delta(\Imaginary z)^{2}\bigg((\Imaginary z)^{-3}-\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}+\Imaginary z\Big)^{-3}\bigg)\geq\frac{t\delta\mathfrak{c}}{2\Imaginary z},

where in the first statement we used the first bound of in (
B.16
); in the second we used the fact that
\varrho_{0}(x)\geq\delta
for
|x|\leq\mathfrak{c}
; in the third we changed variables from
x
to
x-\Real z
and used the bound
|\Real z|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
; in the fourth we performed the integration; and in the fifth we used the fact that
\Imaginary z\leq\mathfrak{c}
. This verifies the second statement of (
B.17
).

To apply (
B.17
), let
C_{2}=C_{2}(k)>10
denote a sufficiently large constant (to be determined later) and fix real numbers

(B.19)

\displaystyle x_{0},t\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\text{such that}\quad|x_{0}|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4},\quad\text{and}\quad t\in\Big[0,\frac{(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{20}}{C_{2}B^{20}}\Big].

By
2.8
, there exists a complex number
z_{0}=z_{0}(x_{0},t)\in\partial\Lambda_{t}
such that
z_{0}-tm_{0}(z_{0})=x_{0}
. We claim that
|\Real z_{0}|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
. To see this, first observe that
z_{0}=z_{0}(x_{0},t)
is continuous in
t
. Indeed, since the map
M(z)=z-tm_{0}(z)
is a homeomorphism from
\overline{\Lambda}_{t}
(recall (
2.10
)) to
\overline{\mathbb{H}}
by
2.8
that is uniformly continuous in
t
, its inverse
M^{-1}
is also continuous in
t
. Thus,
z_{0}(x_{0},t)=M^{-1}(x_{0})
is continuous in
t
. Now, assume to the contrary that
\big|\Real z_{0}(x,t)\big|>\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
. Then since
z_{0}(x_{0},0)=x_{0}\in\big[-\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4},\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4}\big]
, there exists some
s\in[0,t]
such that
\big|\Real z_{0}(x_{0},s)\big|=\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
. Since
|x_{0}|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4}
, it follows that
\big|tm_{0}(z_{0})\big|=|z_{0}-x_{0}|\geq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4}
at
z_{0}=z_{0}(x_{0},s)
. This contradicts the fact (from the
j=0
case of (
B.17
) and (
B.19
)) that

\displaystyle\big|tm_{0}(z_{0})\big|\leq\frac{(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{20}}{C_{2}B^{20}}\cdot C_{1}B^{2}(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{-1}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{8},

for
C_{2}>C_{1}
, and so
|\Real z_{0}|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
. Further observe that
\Imaginary z_{0}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
, since otherwise the third statement of (
B.14
) would yield (as
C_{2}>4
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(x)dx}{|x-z_{0}|^{2}}<4\mathfrak{c}^{-2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\varrho_{0}(x)dx=4\mathfrak{c}^{-2}<\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}B^{20}}{(\delta\mathfrak{c})^{20}},

which contradicts (
B.19
).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{t}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{0}(x)dx}{|x-z_{0}|^{2}}\leq B\int_{-\mathfrak{c}}^{\mathfrak{c}}\frac{dx}{|x-z_{0}|^{2}}+4\mathfrak{c}^{-2}\leq\frac{B\pi}{\Imaginary z}+4\mathfrak{c}^{-2},

where in the first statement we used the third part of (
B.14
); in the second we used the facts that
\sup_{|x|\leq\mathfrak{c}}\varrho(x)\leq B
, that
\varrho
is a probability density, and that
|\Real z_{0}|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
; and in the third we used the equality
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x-z|^{-2}dx=\pi(\Imaginary z)^{-1}
. Since
t\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}^{2}}{8}
, it follows that
t(\Imaginary z)^{-1}\geq(2B\pi)^{-1}
. Inserting this into the second bound of (
B.17
) (which applies as
|\Real z_{0}|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
and
\Imaginary z_{0}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
) gives

(B.20)

\displaystyle\big|1-tm_{0}^{\prime}(z_{0})\big|\geq\displaystyle\frac{\delta\mathfrak{c}}{4\pi B}.

Inserting the first bound of (
B.17
) (at
z=z_{0}
, which applies since
|\Real z_{0}|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
) and (
B.20
) into (
B.12
), it follows that there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(k)>1
such that

(B.21)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{x}^{j}\varrho_{t}(x_{0})\big|\leq C_{3}(B^{8}\delta^{-6}\mathfrak{c}^{-6})^{j+1},\qquad\text{for any $j\in\llbracket 0,k-1\rrbracket$}.

Before proceeding to bound the
t
-derivative of
\varrho_{t}
, let us quickly establish the first statement of (
2.14
). To that end, observe that

\displaystyle\big|\varrho_{t}(x_{0})-\varrho_{0}(x_{0})\big|

\displaystyle=\pi^{-1}\big|\Imaginary m_{t}(x_{0})-\Imaginary m_{0}(x_{0})\big|

\displaystyle=\pi^{-1}\Big|\Imaginary m_{t}\big(z_{0}-tm_{0}(z_{0})\big)-\Imaginary m_{0}(x_{0})\Big|

\displaystyle=\pi^{-1}\big|\Imaginary m_{0}(z_{0})-\Imaginary m_{0}(x_{0})\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}B^{16}\delta^{-12}\mathfrak{c}^{-12}\cdot|z_{0}-x_{0}|\leq C_{3}B^{16}\delta^{-12}\mathfrak{c}^{-12}\cdot t\big|m_{0}(z_{0})\big|\leq\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{4},

where in the first statement, we applied (
2.6
); in the second, the definition of
z_{0}
; in the third statement, (
2.11
); in the fourth, the first bound in (
B.17
) at
j=1
; in the fifth, the fact that
z_{0}-x_{0}=tm_{0}(z_{0})
; and in the sixth, the bound on
t
from (
B.19
) (and making
C_{2}>4C_{3}
sufficiently large) with first bound in (
B.17
) at
j=0
. Since
\varrho_{0}(x_{0})\geq\delta
(as
|x|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4}\leq\mathfrak{c}
), this implies the first statement of (
2.14
).

To prove the second statement of (
2.14
), observe by (
B.21
) that it suffices to bound the
t
-derivatives of
\varrho_{t}
. Recall from (
2.12
) that
\partial_{t}\varrho_{t}(x)=\pi\partial_{x}\big(\varrho_{t}(x)H\varrho_{t}(x)\big)
, where
H\varrho_{t}(x)
is the Hilbert transform of
\varrho_{t}
(recall (
2.7
)). For any real number
x\in\big[-\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4},\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{4}\big]
and integer
j\in\llbracket 0,k-2\rrbracket
, we can then bound the derivatives of Hilbert transform by

(B.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big|\partial_{x}^{j}H\varrho_{t}(x)\big|&=\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\partial_{x}^{j}\bigg(\PV\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varrho_{t}(x-y)dy}{y}\bigg)\Bigg|\\
&=\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\partial_{x}^{j}\bigg(\PV\displaystyle\int_{-\mathfrak{c}/2}^{\mathfrak{c}/2}\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{t}(x-y)dy}{y}\bigg)+\partial_{x}^{j}\displaystyle\int_{|y|>\mathfrak{c}/2}\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{t}(x-y)dy}{y}\Bigg|\\
&=\pi^{-1}\Bigg|\partial_{x}^{j}\bigg(\PV\int_{-\mathfrak{c}/2}^{\mathfrak{c}/2}\frac{\big(\varrho_{t}(x-y)-\varrho_{t}(x)\big)dy}{y}\bigg)+\partial_{x}^{j}\displaystyle\int_{|y-x|>\mathfrak{c}/2}\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{t}(y)dy}{x-y}\Bigg|\\
&\leq\mathfrak{c}\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{|y|\leq\mathfrak{c}}\big|\partial_{x}^{j+1}\varrho_{t}(y)\big|+2^{j}j!\mathfrak{c}^{-j}=\mathcal{O}\big((B^{8}\delta^{-6}\mathfrak{c}^{-6})^{j+2}\big),\end{split}

where in the first and second statements we used (
2.7
); in the third we used the fact that
\PV(a)=0
for any constant
a
in the first integral, and we changed variables from
y
to
x-y
in the second; in the fourth we used the fact that
\big|\partial_{x}^{j}(x-y)^{-1}\big|\leq j!2^{j+1}\mathfrak{c}^{-j-1}
for
|x-y|\geq\frac{\mathfrak{c}}{2}
and that
\varrho_{t}
is a probability measure; and in the fifth we applied (
B.21
). Combining this with (
B.21
) and (
2.12
) then yields the second bound in (
2.14
).
∎

B.3.
Proof of
2.4

In this section we establish
2.4
, to which end we discretize in time (leaving space continuous). For any integer
T\geq 1
, a
(
T
-step) Gaussian walk
starting at
u\in\mathbb{R}
is a probability measure on
(T+1)
-tuples
\big(x(0),x(1),\ldots,x(T)\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{T}
with
x(0)=u
such that, for each
j\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket
, the jump
x(j)-x(j-1)
is a centered Gaussian random variable of variance
1
. A
(
T
-step) Gaussian bridge
from
u
to
v
is a Gaussian walk starting at
u
, conditioned to end at
v
(so
x(T)=v
). The following definition, similar to
Definition

1.1
, provides notation for non-intersecting Gaussian bridges.

Definition B.3
.

Fix integers
T,n\geq 1
; two
n
-tuples
\bm{u},\bm{v}\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
; and two functions
f,g:\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\mathfrak{G}_{f;g}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}}
denote the law on sequences
\bm{x}(t)=\big(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\ldots,x_{n}(t)\big)
, with
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
, given by
n
independent
T
-step Gaussian walks, conditioned on satisfying the following three properties.

(1)

The
x_{j}
do not intersect, that is,
\bm{x}(t)\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
for each
t\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket
.

(2)

The
x_{j}
start at
u_{j}
and end at
v_{j}
, that is,
x_{j}(0)=u_{j}
and
x_{j}(T)=v_{j}
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
.

(3)

The
x_{j}
are bounded below by
f
and above by
g
, that is,
f<x_{j}<g
for each
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
.

We assume
f(0)\leq u_{n}\leq u_{1}\leq g(0)
and
f(T)\leq v_{n}\leq v_{1}\leq g(T)
, even when not stated explicitly.

Analogous to
2.1
, the following states that non-intersecting Gaussian walks are invariant under affine transformations.

Remark B.4
.

Nonintersecting Gaussian walks satisfy the following invariance property under affine transformations. Adopt the notation of
Definition

B.3
, and fix real numbers
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Define the
n
-tuples
\widetilde{\bm{u}},\widetilde{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
and functions
\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}:\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
by setting

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{j}=u_{j}+\alpha,\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{v}_{j}=v_{j}+T\beta+\alpha,\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\text{for each $j\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket$};

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}(t)=f(t)+t\beta+\alpha,\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{g}(t)=g(t)+t\beta+\alpha,\qquad\text{for each $t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket$}.

Sampling
\widetilde{\bm{x}}=\big(\widetilde{x}_{1}(t),\widetilde{x}_{2}(t),\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{n}(t)\big)
under
\mathfrak{G}_{\tilde{f},\tilde{g}}^{\tilde{\bm{u}};\tilde{\bm{v}}}
, there is a coupling between
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
and
\bm{x}
such that
\widetilde{x}_{j}(t)=x_{j}(t)+\beta t+\alpha
for each
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
.

As in
2.1
, this follows from the analogous affine invariance of a single Gaussian random bridge, together with the fact that affine transformations do not affect the non-intersecting property.

The following lemma states height monotonicity (the analog of
2.3
) for non-intersecting Gaussian bridges. Its proof is very similar to that of
2.3
(from
[
18
]
) and is thus omitted.

Lemma B.5
.

Fix integers
T,n\geq 1
; four
n
-tuples
\bm{u},\widetilde{\bm{u}},\bm{v},\widetilde{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
; and functions
f,\widetilde{f},g,\widetilde{g}:\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
with
f\leq g
and
\widetilde{f}\leq\widetilde{g}
. Sample non-intersecting Gaussian bridges
\bm{x}(t)
and
\widetilde{\bm{x}}(t)
from the measures
\mathfrak{G}_{f;g}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}}
and
\mathfrak{G}_{\tilde{f};\tilde{g}}^{\tilde{\bm{u}};\tilde{\bm{v}}}
, respectively. If

\displaystyle f\leq\widetilde{f};\quad g\leq\widetilde{g};\quad\bm{u}\leq\widetilde{\bm{u}};\quad\bm{v}\leq\widetilde{\bm{v}},

then there exists a coupling between
\bm{x}
and
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
such that
\bm{x}(t)\leq\widetilde{\bm{x}}(t)
, for each
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
.

The following lemma is a discrete (in time) variant of
2.4
and quickly implies it.

Lemma B.6
.

Adopt the notation and assumptions of
B.5
, and fix some real number
B\geq 0
. If
u_{j}\leq\widetilde{u}_{j}\leq u_{j}+B
and
v_{j}\leq\widetilde{v}_{j}\leq v_{j}+B
for all
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, then the following two statements hold.

(1)

If
f(t)\leq\widetilde{f}(t)\leq f(t)+B
and
g(t)\leq\widetilde{g}(t)\leq g(t)+B
for each
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
, then there is a coupling between
\bm{x}
and
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
so that
x_{j}(t)\leq\widetilde{x}_{j}(t)\leq x_{j}(t)+B
for each
(t,j)\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
.

(2)

If
\bm{u}=\widetilde{\bm{u}}
, and
f(t)\leq\widetilde{f}(t)\leq f(t)+\frac{tB}{T}
and
g(t)\leq\widetilde{g}(t)\leq g(t)+\frac{tB}{T}
for each
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
, then there is a coupling between
\bm{x}
and
\widetilde{\bm{x}}
so that
x_{j}(t)\leq\widetilde{x}_{j}(t)\leq x_{j}(t)+\frac{tB}{T}
for each
(t,j)\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
.

Proof of
2.4
.

This follows from
B.6
with the convergence of Gaussian walks to Brownian bridges; we omit further details.
∎

Similarly to the proof
[
18
, Lemma 2.6 and Lemma 2.7]
, that of
B.6
makes use of a local Glauber dynamic, which in our setting is defined as follows.

Definition B.7
.

Fix integers
T,n\geq 1
and two functions
f,g:\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. For
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
, let
\bm{y}(t)=\big(y_{1}(t),y_{2}(t),\ldots,y_{n}(t)\big)\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
be a family of
n
non-intersecting
T
-step walks. The
Glauber dynamics
is the discrete-time Markov chain whose state
\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{y}(t)=\big(\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{1}(t),\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{2}(t),\ldots,\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{n}(t)\big)
at time
k\geq 0
is determined as follows. Below, for each
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
, let
y_{0}(t)=g(t)
and
y_{n+1}(t)=f(t)
.

Set
\mathcal{P}^{0}\bm{y}=\bm{y}
. For
k\geq 1
, let
(a,b,d)\in\llbracket 0,T-2\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be such that
k=dn(T-1)+an+b
. For
(t,j)\neq(a+1,b)
, set
\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{j}(t)=\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{j}(t)
. For
(t,j)=(a+1,b)
, set
\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{b}(a+1)
to be the middle (
t=1
) value of a
2
-step Gaussian random bridge, conditioned to start at
\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b}(a)
; end at
\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b}(a+2)
; be above
\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b+1}(a+1)
; and be below
\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b-1}(a+1)
.

Remark B.8
.

It follows from the Gibbs property (for non-intersecting Gaussian bridges) that
\mathfrak{G}_{f;g}^{\bm{y}(0);\bm{y}(T)}
is a stationary measure for the Glauber dynamics
\mathcal{P}
.

The following lemma indicates that the Glauber dynamics converge to the stationary measure
\mathfrak{G}_{f;g}^{\bm{y}(0);\bm{y}(T)}
in the long-time limit; its proof is given in
Section

B.4
below.

Lemma B.9
.

Adopt the notation of
Definition

B.7
, and assume that
\max_{t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket}\big(|f(t)|+|g(t)|\big)<\infty
. Then, the law of
\mathcal{P}^{2n(T-1)k}\bm{y}
converges as
k
tends to
\infty
to
\mathfrak{G}_{f;g}^{\bm{y}(0);\bm{y}(T)}
.

Now let us briefly outline the proof of
B.6
.

Proof of
B.6
(Outline).

We only discuss the second statement of the lemma, as the proof of the first is entirely analogous. We will assume throughout that
\big|f(t)\big|+\big|g(t)\big|<\infty
for each
t\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket
, as the cases when
f(s)=-\infty
or
g(s)=\infty
for some
s\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket
can then be obtained from a limiting procedure (in particular, one can consider a sequence
f_{k}
of lower boundaries such that
f_{k}(s)
always remains finite but tends to
-\infty
as
k
tends to
\infty
, and similarly for
g
).

Let us first confirm the lemma when
(T,n)=(2,1)
, in which case we will verify the following more general result. Fix a real number
A\geq 0
and assume that

\displaystyle x_{1}(0)\leq\widetilde{x}_{1}(0)\leq x_{1}(0)+A;\qquad x_{1}(0)\leq\widetilde{x}_{1}(2)\leq x_{1}(2)+A+B;

\displaystyle f(1)\leq\widetilde{f}(1)\leq f(1)+A+\displaystyle\frac{B}{2};\quad g(1)\leq\widetilde{g}(1)\leq g(1)+A+\displaystyle\frac{B}{2}.

Then, there exists a coupling between
x_{1}
and
\widetilde{x}_{1}
such that

(B.23)

\displaystyle x_{1}(1)\leq\widetilde{x}_{1}(1)\leq x_{1}(1)+A+\displaystyle\frac{B}{2}.

Indeed, this is a consequence of the fact (which follows quickly from
B.5
and
B.4
, or can also be verified directly) that
\mathbb{P}\big[x_{1}(1)\geq r\big]\leq\mathbb{P}\big[\widetilde{x}_{1}(1)\geq r\big]\leq\mathbb{P}\big[x_{1}(1)+A+\frac{B}{2}\geq r\big]
, for any real number
r\in\mathbb{R}
. This shows (
B.23
), whose
A=0
special case yields the second part of
B.6
at
(T,n)=(2,1)
.

Thus, we assume in what follows that either
T\geq 3
or
n\geq 2
. Fix two ensembles
\bm{y}(t)=\big(y_{1}(t),y_{2}(t),\ldots,y_{n}(t)\big)\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
and
\widetilde{\bm{y}}(t)=\big(\widetilde{y}_{1}(t),\widetilde{y}_{2}(t),\ldots,\widetilde{y}_{n}(t)\big)\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
of
n
non-intersecting
T
-step walks, with starting points
\bm{y}(0)=\bm{u}=\widetilde{\bm{y}}(0)
, and ending points
\bm{y}(T)=\bm{v}
and
\widetilde{\bm{y}}(T)=\widetilde{\bm{v}}
, such that, for each
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle\bm{y}(t)\leq\widetilde{\bm{y}}(t)\leq\bm{y}(t)+\displaystyle\frac{tB}{T};\qquad f(t)\leq y_{n}(t)\leq y_{1}(t)\leq g(t);\qquad\widetilde{f}(t)\leq\widetilde{y}_{1}(t)\leq\widetilde{y}_{n}(t)\leq\widetilde{g}(t).

Such initial data exist due to the assumptions of the second part of the lemma.

We next run the Glauber dynamics on
\bm{y}
(with boundaries
(f,g)
) and
\widetilde{\bm{y}}
(with boundaries
(\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g})
), coupled in the following way. If we update the pair
\big(\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{j}(t),\mathcal{P}^{k-1}\widetilde{y}_{j}(t)\big)
, then using (
B.23
) (with the
(A;B)
there given by
\big(\frac{(t-1)B}{T};\frac{2B}{T}\big)
here), we couple
\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{j}(t)\leq\mathcal{P}^{k}\widetilde{y}_{j}(t)\leq\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{j}(t)+\frac{tB}{T}
. By induction on
k
, this ensures that
\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{j}(t)\leq\mathcal{P}^{k}\widetilde{y}_{j}(t)\leq\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{j}(t)+\frac{tB}{T}
holds for each
(t,j)\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
and
k\geq 0
. Letting
k
tend to
\infty
and taking any limit point of this coupling, the lemma then follows from
B.9
.
∎

B.4.
Proof of
B.9

In this section we establish
B.9
. We begin by recalling a general statement on convergence to stationarity of infinite-dimensional Markov chains.

Let
\Omega
be a measurable space with
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
; let
\mathscr{P}(\Omega)
denote the space of probability measures on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
. Given any two probability measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega)
, recall that the total variation distance between them is defined by

(B.24)

\displaystyle d_{\TV}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\displaystyle\sup_{A\in\mathcal{F}}\big|\nu_{1}(A)-\nu_{2}(A)\big|.

Assumption B.10
.

Adopting the above notation, let
\mathsf{K}:\Omega\times\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be a Markov transition kernel. For any function
\varphi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and measure
\mu
on
\Omega
, define the function
\mathfrak{\mathsf{K}}\varphi:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and measure
\mathsf{K}\mu
on
\Omega
by setting

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}\varphi(x)=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\varphi(y)\mathsf{K}(x,dy);\qquad\mathsf{K}\mu(A)=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathsf{K}(x,A)\mu(dx),

for any
x\in\Omega
and measurable set
A\in\mathcal{F}
. Assume that there exist constants
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
B\geq 0
, and
R>\frac{2B}{1-\gamma}
; a potential function
V:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
; and a probability measure
\nu
on
\Omega
, such that the following two conditions hold.

(1)

For each
x\in\Omega
, we have
\mathsf{K}V(x)\leq\gamma V(x)+B
, for each
x\in\Omega
.

(2)

For each
x\in\Omega
with
V(x)\leq R
, and any measurable set
A\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
\mathsf{K}(x,A)\geq\alpha\nu(A)
.

The following result provides a convergence theorem for such Harris chains
[
38
]
. It appears in
[
57
]
, though it is stated as written below in
[
37
]
.

Lemma B.11

(
[
37
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Adopt
B.10
, and fix a measure
\mu
on
\Omega
. Then, the Markov process defined by
\mathsf{K}
has a unique stationary measure
\mu_{0}
, and
\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\|\mathsf{K}^{m}\mu-\mu_{0}\|_{\TV}=0
.

We next apply
B.11
to the Glauber dynamics
\mathcal{P}
of
Definition

B.7
. Throughout the remainder of this section, we adopt the notation of that definition. These include the functions
f,g:\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
; the family
\bm{y}
of
n
non-intersecting
T
-step walks
\bm{y}(t)=\big(y_{1}(t),y_{2}(t),\ldots,y_{n}(t)\big)\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
(over
t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
), and the associated Markov operator
\mathcal{P}
(which we also interpret as a kernel) for the Glauber dynamics. We also set
\bm{u}=\bm{y}(0)
and
\bm{v}=\bm{y}(T)
, which are fixed throughout the dynamics. Then the state space for the Glauber dynamics
\mathcal{P}
can be viewed as

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}=\Big\{\big(\bm{y}(t)\big)_{t\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket}\in(\mathbb{W}_{n})^{T-1}:\displaystyle\min_{t\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket}\big(y_{n}(t)-f(t)\big)>0,\displaystyle\min_{t\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket}\big(g(t)-y_{1}(t)\big)>0\Big\}.

The associated potential function
V_{0}
is defined by

\displaystyle V_{0}(\bm{y})=\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\displaystyle\max_{t\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket}\Big(\big|y_{j}(t)\big|+1\Big).

We then have the following two lemmas verifying
B.10
for the Glauber dynamics.

Lemma B.12
.

There exist constants
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
B=B(\bm{u},\bm{v},f,g)\geq 0
such that, for any family
\bm{y}
of
n
non-intersecting
T
-step walks with
\bm{y}(0)=\bm{u}
and
\bm{y}(T)=\bm{v}
, we have
\mathcal{P}V_{0}(\bm{y})\leq\gamma V_{0}(\bm{y})+B
.

Proof.

This follows from taking
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
and
B=\max_{t\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket}\big(|f(t)|+|g(t)|\big)+1
, which applies since
f(t)\leq y_{j}(t)\leq g(t)
holds for any
(t,j)\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
. In particular,
\mathcal{P}V_{0}(\bm{y})\leq B\leq\gamma V_{0}(\bm{y})+B
holds deterministically.
∎

Lemma B.13
.

There exists a constant
\alpha=\alpha(\bm{u},\bm{v},f,g)>0
such that the following holds. Letting
\nu_{0}
denote the Lebesgue measure on the set
\Omega_{0}
, we have
\mathcal{P}^{2n(T-1)}(\bm{y},A)\geq\alpha\nu_{0}(A)
, for each
\bm{y}\in\Omega_{0}
and any measurable subset
A\subseteq(\mathbb{W}_{n})^{T-1}
.

Proof (Outline).

For any integer
T^{\prime}\geq 2
; two
n
-tuples
\bm{u}^{\prime},\bm{v}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
; and two functions
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}:\llbracket 0,T^{\prime}\rrbracket\rightarrow\overline{\mathbb{R}}
, the density of the measure
\mathfrak{G}_{f^{\prime};g^{\prime}}^{\bm{u}^{\prime};\bm{v}^{\prime}}
on sequences
\bm{\mathsf{x}}(t)=\big(\mathsf{x}_{1}(t),\mathsf{x}_{2}(t),\ldots,\mathsf{x}_{n}(t)\big)
is

(B.25)

\displaystyle C\cdot\textbf{1}_{\bm{\mathsf{x}}\in\Omega_{0}}\cdot\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Bigg(\textbf{1}_{\mathsf{x}_{j}(0)=u_{j}^{\prime}}\textbf{1}_{\mathsf{x}_{j}(T^{\prime})=v_{j}^{\prime}}\displaystyle\prod_{t=1}^{T^{\prime}}\exp\bigg(-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathsf{x}_{j}(t)-\mathsf{x}_{j}(t-1)\big)^{2}\bigg)\displaystyle\prod_{t=1}^{T^{\prime}-1}d\mathsf{x}_{j}(t)\Bigg),

for some normalization constant
C=C(f^{\prime},\bm{u}^{\prime},\bm{v}^{\prime})>0
. Observe that there exist some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(f^{\prime},g^{\prime},\bm{u}^{\prime},\bm{v}^{\prime})>0
such that
C>c_{1}
(that is, the normalization constant is bounded below), since the property
\bm{\mathsf{x}}\in\Omega_{0}
is an open condition. Further observe that the density (
B.25
) is uniformly bounded (since
\big|f(t)\big|+\big|g(t)\big|
is). Thus, there exists a constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(f^{\prime};\bm{u}^{\prime};\bm{v}^{\prime})>0
such that the measure
\mathfrak{G}_{f^{\prime}}^{\bm{u}^{\prime};\bm{v}^{\prime}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on this set, and its Radon–Nikodym derivative is bounded above by
c_{1}^{-1}
and below by
c_{1}
.

Adopting the notation of
Definition

B.7
, we fix an integer
k\geq 1
and apply this in the case
(n^{\prime},T^{\prime})=(1,2)
;
(\bm{u}^{\prime},\bm{v}^{\prime})=(u_{1}^{\prime},v_{1}^{\prime})=\big(\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b}(a),\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b}(a+2)\big)
; and
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})=\big(f^{\prime}(1),g^{\prime}(1)\big)=\big(\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b+1}(a+1),\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b-1}(a+1)\big)
. We then deduce (using the fact that the density (
B.25
) is bounded below by a constant multiple of the Lebesgue measure on
\Omega_{0}
) that there is a constant
c_{2}=c_{2}(\bm{u},\bm{v},f,g,R)>0
such that

(B.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{b}(a+1)\in S_{k}\big]\geq c_{2}\displaystyle\int_{S_{k}}dy,

for any measurable set
S_{k}\subseteq\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b+1}(a+1)\leq x\leq\mathcal{P}^{k-1}y_{b-1}(a+1)\big\}
. Applying this
n(T-1)
times yields a constant
c_{3}=c_{3}(\bm{u},\bm{v},f,g,R)>0
such that

(B.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{P}^{k+n(T-1)}\bm{y}\in S\big]\geq c_{3}\cdot\nu_{0}(S),

for any measurable set

\displaystyle S\subseteq\Big\{\bm{x}=\big(\bm{x}(t)\big)_{t\in\llbracket 1,T-1\rrbracket}\in(\mathbb{W}_{n})^{T-1}:\mathcal{P}^{k}y_{j+1}(t)\leq x_{j}(t)\leq g(t),\quad\text{for all $(t,j)$}\Big\}.

Applying (
B.27
) twice, first at
k=0
and then at
k=n(T-1)
, yields the lemma.
∎

Given these lemmas, we can quicky establish
B.9
.

Proof of
B.9
.

That
\mathfrak{G}_{f}^{\bm{u};\bm{v}}
is stationary for
\mathcal{P}
follows from
B.8
. Thus, the lemma follows from
B.11
, using
B.12
and
B.13
to verify
B.10
.
∎

B.5.
Proof of
2.18

In this section we establish
2.18
. We begin with the following concentration bound, which follows quickly from combining results of
[
61
,
10
]
, for the eigenvalues of a GUE random matrix.

Lemma B.14

(
[
61
, Corollary 5]
,
[
10
, Corollary 1.6]
)
.

There exists a constant
C>1
such that the following holds. Letting
n\geq 1
be an integer and
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
denote the eigenvalues of an
n\times n
GUE matrix
\bm{G}=\bm{G}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg[\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\Big\{\big|\lambda_{j}-\gamma_{\semci;n}(j)\big|\geq(\log n)^{8}\cdot n^{-2/3}\cdot\min\{

\displaystyle j,n-j+1\}^{-1/3}\Big\}\Bigg]\leq Ce^{-(\log n)^{5}}.

Now we can establish
2.18
.

Proof of
2.18
.

By
2.1
, we may assume throughout that
u=v=0
. We may also assume by shifting that
a=-b=T
, so that
(a,b)=(-T,T)
for some
T\leq n^{D}
.

We next equate the law of
\bm{x}(t)
with the eigenvalues of a suitably scaled GUE matrix. To that end, let
\bm{\lambda}(t)=\big(\lambda_{1}(t),\lambda_{2}(t),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(t)\big)\in\mathbb{W}_{n}
denote Dyson Brownian motion with starting data
\bm{0}_{n}
. From the second part of
2.15
,
\bm{\lambda}(t)
prescribes the law of
n
non-intersecting Brownian motions (of variance
n^{-1}
) run for time
t
, conditoned to never intersect; from the first part of
2.15
, it also prescribes the eigenvalues of an
n\times n
GUE matrix, scaled by
t^{1/2}
.

Next, recall that a Brownian bridge
B(t)
on the interval
[-T,T]
, conditioned to start and end at
0
(that is,
B(-T)=B(T)=0
), has the same law as
\frac{T-t}{(2T)^{1/2}}\cdot W\big(\frac{T+t}{T-t}\big)
, where
W:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a Brownian motion. This, implies (see also
[
19
, Equation (2)]
) that

(B.28)

\displaystyle\text{$x_{j}(t)$ has the same law as $(2T)^{-1/2}(T^{2}-t^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\lambda_{j}$};

Given (
B.28
), the proofs of both parts of the lemma are very similar, obtained by applying
B.14
on a suitable mesh of
t
; we therefore only detail the proof of the first statement of the lemma. To that end, let
C_{0}>1
be the constant from
B.14
. For any integer
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
and real number
t\in[-T,T]
, define the events

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}(t)=\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\bigg\{\Big|x_{j}(t)-(2T)^{-1/2}(T^{2}-t^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\gamma(j)\Big|\geq(\log n)^{8}\cdot T^{1/2}n^{-2/3}\cdot\min\{j,n-j+1\}^{-1/3}\bigg\};

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{0}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\bigcup_{a\leq t<t+s\leq b}\Big\{\big|x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\big|\geq s^{1/2}n^{5}\log|4s^{-1}T|\Big\};\quad\mathscr{G}_{1}=\bigcup_{j=-\lfloor Tn^{20(D+1)}\rfloor}^{\lfloor Tn^{20(D+1)}\rfloor}\mathscr{G}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{j}{n^{20(D+1)}}\Big),

where we have abbreviated
\gamma(j)=\gamma_{\semci;n}(j)
. By (
B.28
),
B.14
, (the
B=n^{5}
case of)
2.6
, a union bound, and the fact that
T\leq n^{D}
, there exist constants
c>0
and
c_{1}=c_{1}(D)>1
such that

(B.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{G}_{0}\cap\mathscr{G}_{1}]\leq 3C_{0}n^{20(D+1)}Te^{-c_{1}(\log n)^{5}}+c^{-1}e^{n/c-cn^{10}}\leq 2c_{1}^{-1}e^{-c(\log n)^{5}},

for sufficiently large
n
. Moreover observe, since the function
(T-t^{2}T^{-1})^{1/2}
is
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder on
[-T,T]
with constant
2^{1/2}
, on the event
\mathscr{G}_{0}^{\complement}\cap\mathscr{G}_{1}^{\complement}
we have for each
j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
that

(B.30)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[a,b]}\bigg|x_{j}(t)-\Big(\displaystyle\frac{T}{2}-\frac{t^{2}}{2T}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot\gamma(j)\bigg|\\
&\quad\leq\displaystyle\max_{n^{20(D+1)}t\in\llbracket-Tn^{20(D+1)},Tn^{20(D+1)}\rrbracket}\bigg|x_{j}(t)-(2T)^{-1/2}(T^{2}-t^{2})^{1/2}\cdot\gamma(j)\bigg|\\
&\qquad+\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[a,b]}\Bigg(\sup_{s\in[0,n^{-20(D+1)}]}\big|x_{j}(t+s)-x_{j}(t)\big|\Bigg)\\
&\qquad+\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[a,b]}\Bigg(\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,n^{-20(D+1)}]}\Big|\big(T^{2}-(t+s)^{2}\big)^{1/2}-(T^{2}-t^{2})^{1/2}\Big|\Bigg)\\
&\quad\leq(\log n)^{8}\cdot T^{1/2}n^{-2/3}\cdot\min\{j,n-j+1\}^{-1/3}+2n^{-10D-5}\cdot\log\big(10(T+1)Tn^{20(D+1)}\big)\\
&\quad\leq 2(\log n)^{8}\cdot T^{1/2}n^{-2/3}\cdot\min\{j,n-j+1\}^{-1/3}+n^{-D},\end{aligned}

for sufficiently large
n
. The lemma then follows from (
B.29
) and (
B.30
). ∎

Appendix C
Proofs of Results From
Section

3

C.1.
Proofs of
3.3
,
3.5
, and
3.7

In this section we establish first
3.3
, then
3.5
, and next
3.7
.

Proof of
3.3
.

Denote the bridge-limiting measure process on
[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]
with boundary data
(\mu_{\tilde{a}},\mu_{\tilde{b}})
by
(\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\star})_{t\in[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]}
. Recalling the ensemble
\bm{x}^{n}\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([a,b]\big)
, condition on
\bm{x}(t)
for
t\notin[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]
. Then, the law of the restriction
\bm{y}^{n}=\bm{x}^{n}|_{[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]}\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]\big)
of
\bm{x}
to
[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]
is given by
\mathfrak{Q}^{\bm{x}^{n}(\tilde{a});\bm{x}^{n}(\tilde{b})}(An^{-1})
. Then
3.1
implies that, with probability
1-o(1)
(that is, tending to
1
as
n
tends to
\infty
),

(C.1)

\displaystyle\text{the measure $A\cdot\emp\big(\bm{y}^{n}(t)\big)$ converges under the L\'{e}vy metric to $\mu_{t}^{\star}$, uniformly in $t\in[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]$}.

In particular, (
C.1
) holds for
t\in\{\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\}
, and so (with probability
1-o(1)
), the ensemble
\bm{y}
satisfies the conditions of
3.1
, with
(a,b,\mu_{a},\mu_{b})
there equal to
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b},\mu_{\tilde{a}},\mu_{\tilde{b}})
here. Hence, with probability
1-o(1)
, the measure
A\cdot\emp\big(\bm{y}(t)\big)
converges to
\widetilde{\mu}_{t}^{\star}
, uniformly in
t\in[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]
. Together with (
C.1
), this yields the lemma.
∎

Proof of
3.5
.

If
\bm{x}^{n}=(x_{1}^{n},x_{2}^{n},\ldots,x_{n}^{n})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([a,b]\big)
is as in
3.1
, then by
2.2
the ensemble
\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{n}=(\widetilde{x}_{1}^{n},\widetilde{x}_{2}^{n},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{n}^{n})\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times\mathcal{C}\big([0,1]\big)
defined by setting

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{j}(t)=A^{-1/2}(b-a)^{-1/2}\cdot x_{j}\big(a+(b-a)t\big),\qquad\text{for each $(j,t)\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\times[0,1]$},

is as in
3.1
, with the
(a,b;A;\mu_{a},\mu_{b})
there equal to
(0,1;1;\widetilde{\mu}_{0},\widetilde{\mu}_{1})
here. Then, recalling that
\nu_{t}^{n}=\emp\big(\bm{x}^{n}(t)\big)
and denoting
\widetilde{\nu}_{t}=\emp\big(\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{n}(t)\big)
for each
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{t}^{n}(I)=\nu_{a+t(b-a)}^{n}\big(A^{1/2}(b-a)^{1/2}\cdot I\big),\qquad\text{for any interval $I\subseteq\mathbb{R}$}.

Letting
n
tend to
\infty
and using
3.1
, this verifies the first statement of (
3.5
). Together with (
3.3
) and (
3.4
), this also quickly implies the second and third statements of (
3.5
).
∎

Proof of
3.7
.

The first statement of the lemma is due to
[
35
, Corollary 2.8(b)]
, and the second to
[
35
, Theorem 2.7]
(see also
[
7
, Proposition 2.6(6)]
). For the third, the estimates on
\varrho_{t}^{\star}
and
\partial_{x}\varrho_{t}^{\star}
follow from the second part of the lemma, together with
2.10
.

For the fourth, let us first show that
\varrho_{t}(x)
is
\frac{1}{7}
-Hölder in
x
. In particular, we claim that there exists a constant
C_{1}>1
such that, for any
t\in(0,1)
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x-x^{\prime}|
sufficiently small (in a way only dependent on
t
), we have

(C.2)

\displaystyle\big|\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x^{\prime})-\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\big|\leq C_{1}|x-x^{\prime}|^{1/7}\big(t(1-t)\big)^{-1}.

Assume to the contrary that this is false, so there exist
t\in(0,1)
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
such that (
C.2
) does not hold; we may suppose by symmetry that
x^{\prime}>x
. Then, since
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\geq 0
and
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x^{\prime})\geq 0
, we either have
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)>C_{1}(x^{\prime}-x)^{1/7}(t-t^{2})^{-1}
or
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x^{\prime})\geq C_{1}(x^{\prime}-x)^{1/7}(t-t^{2})^{-1}
; let us assume the former holds. By the third part of the lemma, we have for some constant
C_{2}>1
that

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{x}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(y)\big|\leq C_{2}(x^{\prime}-x)^{-6/7}\big(t(1-t)\big)^{2},\qquad\text{whenever $\varrho_{t}^{\star}(y)\geq\big(t(1-t)\big)^{-1}(x^{\prime}-x)^{1/7}$}.

Integrating this bound over a maximal subset of
[x,x^{\prime}]
on which
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(y)\geq(t-t^{2})^{-1}|x-x^{\prime}|^{1/7}
, we find for any such
y_{0}
that

(C.3)

\displaystyle\big|\varrho_{t}^{\star}(y_{0})-\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\big|\leq C_{2}|y_{0}-x|\cdot(x^{\prime}-x)^{-6/7}\big(t(1-t)\big)^{2}\leq C_{2}(x^{\prime}-x)^{1/7}\big(t(1-t)\big)^{2}.

This implies for
C_{1}>C_{2}+2
that
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(y)\geq|x-x^{\prime}|^{1/7}(t-t^{2})^{-1}
for any
y\in[x,x^{\prime}]
, so (
C.3
) applies at
y_{0}=x^{\prime}
. This yields (
C.2
), which is a contradiction, so (
C.2
) holds for any
(t,x)\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Now fix any point
(t_{0},x_{0})\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
and sequence
(t_{1},x_{1}),(t_{2},x_{2}),\ldots\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
with
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}(t_{j},x_{j})=(t_{0},x_{0})
. For any real number
\delta>0
, we have by the continuity of the measures
\mu_{t}^{\star}
in
t
that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\int_{x_{0}-\delta}^{x_{0}+\delta}\varrho_{t_{j}}(x)dx=\int_{x_{0}-\delta}^{x_{0}+\delta}\varrho_{t_{0}}(x)dx.

By (
C.2
), this implies for sufficiently large
j
(dependent on
\delta
) that

\displaystyle\big|\varrho_{t_{0}}(x_{0})-\varrho_{t_{j}}(x_{j})\big|

\displaystyle\leq(2\delta)^{-1}\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}-\delta}^{x_{0}+\delta}\Big(\big|\varrho_{t_{0}}(x)-\varrho_{t_{0}}(x_{0})\big|+\big|\varrho_{t_{j}}(x_{j})-\varrho_{t_{j}}(x_{0})\big|\Big)dx\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+(2\delta)^{-1}\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}-\delta}^{x_{0}+\delta}\varrho_{t_{0}}(x)dx-\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}-\delta}^{x_{0}+\delta}\varrho_{t_{j}}(x)dx\Bigg|\leq 4C_{1}\big(t_{0}(1-t_{0})\big)^{-1}\delta^{1/7}+\delta,

which upon letting
\delta
tend to
0
yields the continuity of
\varrho_{t}(x)
in
(t,x)
.
∎

C.2.
Proofs of
3.12
,
3.13
, and
3.23

In this section we establish first
3.12
, then
3.13
, and next
3.23
.

Proof of
3.12
.

Expanding the second and first equations in (
3.17
) yields

(C.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varrho+u\partial_{x}\varrho+\varrho\partial_{x}u=0;\qquad u\partial_{t}\varrho+\varrho\partial_{t}u+2u\varrho\partial_{x}u+u^{2}\partial_{x}\varrho-\pi^{2}\varrho^{2}\partial_{x}\varrho=0.

The first equation of (
C.4
) is equivalent to the imaginary part of (
3.19
). Moreover, subtracting the second equation of (
C.4
) from the first one multiplied by
u
then yields

\displaystyle\varrho(\partial_{t}u+u\partial_{x}u-\pi^{2}\varrho\partial_{x}\varrho)=0.

Since
\varrho_{t}(x)>0
for
(t,x)\in\Omega
, this implies
\partial_{t}u+u\partial_{x}u-\pi^{2}\varrho\partial_{x}\varrho=0
on
\Omega
, which is equivalent to the real part of (
3.19
); this verifies (
3.19
).
∎

Proof of
3.13
.

Let us first show that
H^{\star}
is locally Lispchitz; that this holds in directions parallel to the
x
-axis follows from the definition (
3.3
) and the bound on
\varrho_{t}^{\star}
from the third part of
3.7
. It remains to show that
H^{\star}
is locally Lipschitz in directions parallel to the
t
-axis.

To that end, fix points
(t_{1},x_{0}),(t_{2},x_{0})\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
with
t_{1}<t_{2}
; by the second part of
3.7
, there exists some real number
x_{1}>x_{0}
such that
\varrho_{t}(x)=0
for each
x>x_{1}-1
and
t\in(0,1)
. For any real number
\varepsilon>0
, let
\varphi_{\varepsilon}:(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a smooth approximation of the indicator function
\textbf{1}_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\cdot\textbf{1}_{x\in[x_{0},x_{1}]}
. Explicitly, fix a smooth function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\supp\psi\subseteq[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
and with total integral
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\psi(t,x)dtdx=1
, and define
\psi_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting
\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\varepsilon^{-2}\psi(\varepsilon^{-1}t,\varepsilon^{-1}x)
for each
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, set

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)=\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{1}}\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-s,x-w)dwds.

Since
(u^{\star},\varrho^{\star})
weakly solves the equation
\partial_{t}\varrho^{\star}=\partial_{x}(u^{\star}\varrho^{\star})
, we have

(C.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\partial_{t}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dxdt+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dxdt=0.

The continuity of
\varrho
(due to the fourth part of
3.7
), and the facts that
\psi_{\varepsilon}
has total integral equal to
1
and that
\supp\psi_{\varepsilon}\subseteq[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]\times[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
, imply

(C.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\partial_{t}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dxdt\\
&=\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{1}}\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\big(\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-t_{1},x-w)-\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-t_{2},x-w)\big)dwdxdt\\
&=\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{1}}\big(\varrho_{t_{1}}^{\star}(x)-\varrho_{t_{2}}^{\star}(x)\big)dx=H_{t_{1}}^{\star}(x_{0})-H_{t_{2}}^{\star}(x_{0}),\end{aligned}

where we used the fact that
H_{t_{j}}^{\star}(x_{1})=0
for each
j\in\{1,2\}
, since
\varrho_{t_{j}}^{\star}(x)=0
for
x>x_{1}
.

Next,
[
35
, Corollary 2.8(c)]
states that, for any constant
\omega\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)
, there exists a constant
C=C(\omega,\mu_{0},\mu_{1})>1
satisfying

(C.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[\omega,1-\omega]}\big|u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\big|<C.

We further have

(C.8)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}&\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\partial_{x}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dxdt\\
&=-\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{x_{1}}u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\partial_{w}\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-s,x-w)dwdsdxdt\\
&=\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\big(\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-s,x-x_{0})-\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-s,x-x_{1})\big)dsdxdt\\
&=\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}u_{t+s}^{\star}(x+x_{0})\varrho_{t+s}^{\star}(x+x_{0})\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dsdxdt,\end{aligned}

where in first equality we used the fact that
\partial_{x}\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-s,x-w)=-\partial_{w}\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-s,x-w)
; in the second we performed the integration; and in the third we used the fact that
u_{t}^{\star}(x)\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)\psi_{\varepsilon}(t-s,w-x_{1})=0
for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
(as
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)=0
for
x\geq x_{1}-1
and
\supp\psi_{\varepsilon}\subseteq[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]\times[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]
) and changed variables from
(t,x)
to
(t+s,x+x_{0})
. Now, since
\psi_{\varepsilon}
is nonnegative with total integral equal to
1
, inserting (
C.8
) and (
C.6
) into (
C.5
), and using (
C.7
) yields
\big|H_{t_{1}}^{\star}(x_{0})-H_{t_{2}}^{\star}(x_{0})\big|\leq C_{1}
for some constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(t_{1},t_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})>1
. In particular,
H_{t}^{\star}
is locally Lipschitz.

It remains to verify (
3.20
); its first statement follows from (
3.3
), so it remains to confirm its second. To that end, the Lebesgue differentiation theorem yields, for almost all
(s,x_{0})\in(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}u_{t+s}^{\star}(x+x_{0})\varrho_{t+s}^{\star}(x+x_{0})\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dxdt=u_{s}^{\star}(x_{0})\varrho_{s}^{\star}(x_{0}).

With (
C.7
), this implies for almost every
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}u_{t+s}^{\star}(x+x_{0})\varrho_{t+s}^{\star}(x+x_{0})\psi_{\varepsilon}(t,x)dsdxdt=\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}u_{s}^{\star}(x_{0})\varrho_{s}^{\star}(x_{0})ds.

Together this, (
C.5
), (
C.6
), and (
C.8
) gives for almost all
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
that
H_{t_{2}}^{\star}(x_{0})-H_{t_{1}}^{\star}(x_{0})=\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}u_{s}^{\star}(x_{0})\varrho_{s}^{\star}(x_{0})ds
, which establishes the second part of (
3.20
).
∎

Proof of
3.23
.

We only establish the first part of the lemma, as the proofs of the second and third are entirely analogous. To do this, it suffices to show that
H^{\star}
is smooth on
\Omega
, as from (
3.4
) this quickly implies that
G^{\star}
is smooth on
\Omega^{\inv}
.

To that end, fix some point
(t_{0},x_{0})\in\Omega
; we must show that
H^{\star}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
(t_{0},x_{0})
. Let
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in(a,b)
be real numbers such that
a^{\prime}<t_{0}<b^{\prime}
. Then,
3.3
implies that
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]}
is the bridge-limiting measure process on
[a^{\prime},b^{\prime}]
with boundary data
(\mu_{a^{\prime}};\mu_{b^{\prime}})
. Furthermore,
3.7
implies that the densities
\varrho_{a^{\prime}}
of
\mu_{a^{\prime}}
and
\varrho_{b^{\prime}}
of
\mu_{b^{\prime}}
are compactly supported and uniformly bounded. Hence,
3.3
, the second part of
3.11
, and
3.14
(with the
(a,b;\mu_{a},\mu_{b})
there equal to
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime};\mu_{a},\mu_{b})
here) imply that
\varrho_{t}^{\star}(x)
and
u_{t}^{\star}(x)
are smooth for
(t,x)
in a neighborhood of
(t_{0},x_{0})
. Together with (
3.20
) (again with
3.14
), this implies that
H^{\star}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
(t_{0},x_{0})
, verifying the lemma.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of Results From
Section

8

D.1.
Proof of
8.2

In this section we establish
8.2
. We first require the following definition from
[
50
]
, which defines a class of initial data for which it is possible to show quick convergence of Dyson Brownian motion. In what follows, we recall the Stieltjes transform
m_{\nu}
associated with a measure
\nu
from (
2.5
), and the empirical measure
\emp(\bm{a})
associated with a sequence
\bm{a}
from (
3.1
).

Definition D.1

(
[
50
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

Fix real numbers
E_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\zeta>0
. For each positive real number
n
, let
r=r_{n}
and
R=R_{n}
be two parameters satisfying
n^{\zeta-1}\leq r\leq n^{-\zeta}
and
n^{\zeta}r\leq R\leq n^{-\zeta}
. We call an
n
-tuple
\bm{d}=(d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

(r,R;\zeta)
-regular with respect to
E_{0}
if the following holds. Denoting
\nu=\nu^{n}=\emp(\bm{d})
, there exist constants
c\in(0,1)
and
C>1
(both independent of
n
) such that
\max_{j\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}|d_{j}|\leq n^{C}
and

\displaystyle c\leq\Imaginary m_{\nu^{n}}(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)\leq C,\qquad\text{for all $n\geq C$ and}\qquad(E,\eta)\in(E-R,E+R)\times(r,10).

Lemma D.2
.

On the event
\mathscr{A}_{0}
, the
n^{\prime}
-tuples
\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{-}(0)
,
\widetilde{\bm{w}}(0)
, and
\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{+}(0)
are
\big((n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-1};(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2};\frac{\omega}{2}\big)
-regular with respect to
0
.

Proof.

We only show the lemma for
\widetilde{\bm{w}}(0)
, as the proof is entirely analogous for
\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{-}(0)
and
\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{+}(0)
; throughout, we restrict to the event
\mathscr{A}_{0}
and denote
\widetilde{\nu}=\widetilde{\nu}^{n}=\emp\big(\widetilde{\bm{w}}(0)\big)
. Then, for any
z\in\mathbb{H}
,

\displaystyle m_{\nu}(z)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{w}_{j}(0)-z}.

Let
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
for some real numbers
E
and
\eta
with
|E|\leq(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2}
and
(n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-1}\leq\eta\leq 10
. Then,

\displaystyle\big|m_{\nu}(z)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z}\Bigg|+\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{\big|\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-\widetilde{w}_{j}(0)\big|}{\big|\widetilde{w}_{j}(0)-z\big|\cdot\big|\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z\big|}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z}\Bigg|+\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}\eta^{2}}\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket}\bigg|\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-\widetilde{w}_{j}(0)\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z}\Bigg|+\eta^{-2}(n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-2}\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z}\Bigg|+(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2},

where in the second bound we used the fact that
\big|\widetilde{w}_{j}(0)-z\big|\cdot\big|\widetilde{G}(0,\frac{j}{n^{\prime}})-z\big|\geq(\Imaginary z)^{2}=\eta^{2}
; for the third we used the fact that we are restricting to
\mathscr{A}_{0}
, together with (
8.12
); and in the fourth we used the fact that
\eta\geq(n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-2}
.

Now let
j_{0}\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket
be the integer (if it exists) such that
\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{j_{0}}{n^{\prime}}\big)\leq E<\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{j_{0}-1}{n^{\prime}}\big)
. Observe from (
8.13
) that
\partial_{y}\widetilde{G}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
and
\widetilde{G}(0,\frac{1}{2})=0
. Therefore, since
|E|\leq(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2}
, it follows for sufficiently large
n
that
j_{0}
exists and

\displaystyle\Big|j_{0}-\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}\Big|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-1}(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2}.

We will assume in what follows that
j_{0}\geq\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}
, as the proof in the alternative case when
j_{0}<\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}
is entirely analogous. Then,

\displaystyle\Bigg|

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{2n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=2j_{0}-n^{\prime}}^{n^{\prime}}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,2j_{0}n^{\prime-1}-jn^{\prime-1})-z}\bigg)\Bigg|+\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2j_{0}-n^{\prime}-1}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=2j_{0}-n^{\prime}}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})+\widetilde{G}(0,2j_{0}n^{\prime-1}-jn^{\prime-1})-2z}{\big(\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z\big)\big(\widetilde{G}(0,2j_{0}n^{\prime-1}-jn^{\prime-1})-z\big)}\Bigg|+(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/8},

where in the second bound we used the fact that for
j<2j_{0}-n^{\prime}\leq 4\varepsilon^{-1}(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega/2}
we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-z\bigg|

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-E

\displaystyle\geq\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\frac{j_{0}-1}{n^{\prime}}\Big)\geq\frac{\varepsilon(j_{0}-j-1)}{2n^{\prime}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}-\displaystyle\frac{10(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2}}{\varepsilon}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\geq(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/8},

by (
8.13
). We additionally have for sufficiently large
n
that

(D.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bigg|\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)+\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{2j_{0}-j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-2z\bigg|&\leq\bigg|\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)+\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{2j_{0}-j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-2\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{j_{0}}{n^{\prime}}\Big)\bigg|+2\eta+\displaystyle\frac{4}{\varepsilon n^{\prime}}\\
&\leq\displaystyle\frac{2B_{0}n^{\prime}}{n}\cdot\Big(\displaystyle\frac{j_{0}-j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)^{2}+3\eta=\displaystyle\frac{2B_{0}(j-j_{0})^{2}}{nn^{\prime}}+3\eta,\end{aligned}

where in the first inequality we used the facts that
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
with
\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\big)\leq E<\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{j-1}{n^{\prime}}\big)
and
-2\varepsilon^{-1}\leq\partial_{y}\widetilde{G}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
(by (
8.13
)), and in the second we used the facts that
[\widetilde{G}]_{2}\leq\frac{2B_{0}n^{\prime}}{n}
(again by (
8.13
)) and that
\eta\geq(n^{\prime})^{\omega-1}
. We also have that

(D.2)

\displaystyle\bigg|\widetilde{G}\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-z\bigg|\geq\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon|j-j_{0}|}{4n^{\prime}}+\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{4};\qquad\bigg|G\Big(0,\displaystyle\frac{2j_{0}-j}{n^{\prime}}\Big)-z\bigg|\geq\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon|j-j_{0}|}{4n^{\prime}}+\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{4},

where in both inequalities we again used the facts that
\partial_{y}\widetilde{G}\leq-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Together, (
D.1
) and (
D.2
) yield

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{j=2j_{0}-n^{\prime}}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})+\widetilde{G}(0,2j_{0}n^{\prime-1}-jn^{\prime-1})-2z}{\big(\widetilde{G}(0,jn^{\prime-1})-z\big)\big(\widetilde{G}(0,2j_{0}n^{\prime-1}-jn^{\prime-1})-z\big)}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\sum_{j=2j_{0}-n^{\prime}}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{\frac{2B_{0}|j-j_{0}|^{2}}{nn^{\prime}}+3\eta}{\frac{\varepsilon^{2}|j-j_{0}|^{2}}{16(n^{\prime})^{2}}+\frac{\eta^{2}}{16}}

\displaystyle\leq 48B_{0}\varepsilon^{-2}\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n^{\prime}}\displaystyle\frac{n^{-1}j^{2}+\eta n^{\prime}}{j^{2}+(\eta n^{\prime})^{2}}\leq 48B_{0}\varepsilon^{-2}\Bigg(\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\eta n^{\prime}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{j^{2}}{n(\eta n^{\prime})^{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta n^{\prime}}\Big)+\displaystyle\sum_{j=\eta n^{\prime}}^{n^{\prime}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}+\displaystyle\frac{\eta n^{\prime}}{j^{2}}\Big)\Bigg)\leq C,

for some constant
C=C(\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0})>1
; this yields the lemma.
∎

The following result of
[
49
]
12
12

12

It was stated there for Dyson Brownian motion associated with the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble (GOE), but it applies equally well to that associated with the GUE, as stated here.
(see also earlier results from
[
50
]
) states that, if one runs Dyson Brownian motion on initial data that is
(r;R;\zeta)
-regular with respect to a point
E_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, then its local statistics after run for some time
t
between
r
and
R^{2}
are given by the sine kernel. In what follows, we recall notation on free convolutions from
Section

2.2
.

Lemma D.3

(
[
49
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

For any integer
k\geq 1
, real numbers
\zeta,\varsigma>0
, and compactly supported, smooth function
F\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, there exist constants
c=c(k,\zeta,\varsigma)>0
and
C=C(k,\zeta,\varsigma,F)>1
such that the following holds. Adopt the notation and assumptions of
Definition

D.1
; fix some real number
t\in[n^{\varsigma}r,n^{-\varsigma}R^{2}]
; define the measure
\nu_{t}=\nu\boxplus\varrho_{\semci}^{(t)}
; and denote the the density of
\nu_{t}
by
\varrho_{t}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Fix some real number
E\in\big[E_{0}-\frac{R}{2},E_{0}+\frac{R}{2}\big]
; let
\bm{d}(t)=\big(d_{1}(t),d_{2}(t),\ldots,d_{n}(t)\big)
denote Dyson Brownian motion, run for time
t
, with initial data
\bm{d}
; and denote its
k
-point correlation function by
p_{\bm{d}(t)}^{(k)}
(recall (
1.5
)). Then, we have

(D.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Bigg|\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}F(\bm{a})p_{\bm{d}(t)}^{(k)}\left(E+\displaystyle\frac{\bm{a}}{n\varrho_{t}(E)}\right)d\bm{a}-\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}F(\bm{a})p_{\sin}^{(k)}(\bm{a})d\bm{a}\Bigg|<Cn^{-c}.\end{aligned}

Now we can outline the proof of
8.2
.

Proof of
8.2
(Outline).

We only address the case when
\mathfrak{a}=0
, as the proof is entirely analogous if
\mathfrak{a}\neq 0
. Recalling the notation from
Section

8.2
(and, in particular, (
8.11
)), denote the measure
\nu_{0}=(n^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\delta_{\tilde{w}_{j}(0)}\in\mathscr{P}_{0}
. For any real number
s\geq 0
, denote its free convolution with the semicircle law by
\nu_{s}=\nu_{0}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(s)}
, and denote the associated density by
\varrho_{s}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, which satisfies
\nu_{s}(dx)=\varrho_{s}(x)dx
.

Observe that
\widetilde{s}_{0}=(n^{\prime})^{\omega-1}
(by (
8.1
) and (
8.11
)) and that
\widetilde{G}\big(\widetilde{s}_{0},\frac{1}{2}\big)=0
(by (
8.13
)). This, together with
D.2
and
D.3
(with the parameters
(n;E_{0},E;t;r,R;\zeta,\varsigma)
there equal to
\big(n^{\prime};0,0,\widetilde{s}_{0};(n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-1},(n^{\prime})^{-\omega/2};\frac{\omega}{2},\frac{\omega}{4}\big)
here) then imply that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}F(\bm{a})p_{\tilde{\bm{w}}(\tilde{s}_{0})}^{(k)}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\bm{a}}{n^{\prime}\varrho_{\tilde{s}_{0}}(0)}\bigg)d\bm{a}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}F(\bm{a})p_{\sin}^{(k)}(\bm{a})d\bm{a}.

It follows from applying the change of variables replacing
\bm{a}
with
\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\cdot\bm{a}
, and using (
8.11
), and that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}F(\bm{a})p_{\breve{\bm{w}}(s_{0})}^{(k)}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{\bm{a}}{n\varrho_{\tilde{s}_{0}}(0)}\bigg)d\bm{a}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}F(\bm{a})p_{\sin}^{(k)}(\bm{a})d\bm{a}.

Since
\theta=\partial_{y}G(t_{0},y_{0})=\partial_{y}\widetilde{G}\big(\widetilde{s}_{0},\frac{1}{2}\big)
and since
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}\big(\widetilde{s}_{0},\frac{1}{2}\big)=\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)
(as
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\widetilde{s}_{0}=0
and
[\widetilde{G}]_{1}=[G]_{1}\leq B_{0}
), it therefore suffices to show that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\varrho_{\tilde{s}_{0}}(0)=\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)
.

Stated alternatively, since the approximate density of
\widetilde{w}_{j}(0)
around
0
is
\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big)
, this amounts to showing that the effect of applying the free convolution with
\mu_{\semci}^{\tilde{s}_{0}}
for
\widetilde{s}_{0}
small has a negligible effect on this density. This is a standard statement about free convolutions that has been proven and applied numerous times in the random matrix literature; we therefore do not repeat its proof here and refer, for example, to
[
42
, Lemmas 3.3 and 3.4]
for very similar arguments.
∎

D.2.
Proof Outline of
8.3

In this section we provide a brief outline for the proof of
8.3
, using results of
[
50
]
.

Proof of
8.3
(Outline).

We only outline the proof of the first statement of (
8.5
), as that of the second is entirely analogous. Following
[
50
, Equation (4.10)]
, we define the processes
\bm{\xi}(s)=\big(\xi_{1}(s),\xi_{2}(s),\ldots,\xi_{n^{\prime}}(s)\big)
and
\bm{\xi}^{-}(s)=\big(\xi_{1}^{-}(s),\xi_{2}^{-}(s),\ldots,\xi_{n^{\prime}}^{-}(s)\big)
by for each pair
(j,s)\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket\times[0,ns_{0}]
setting

(D.4)

\displaystyle\xi_{j}(s)=n\cdot\breve{w}_{j}\Big(\frac{s}{n}\Big);\qquad\xi_{j}^{-}=n\cdot\breve{w}_{j}^{-}\Big(\frac{s}{n}\Big),

which satisfy the stochastic differential equations

(D.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\xi_{i}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{dt}{\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)}+dB_{i}(t);\qquad\partial_{t}\xi_{i}^{-}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{dt}{\xi_{i}^{-}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)}+dB_{i}(t).

We further define for any distinct integers
j,k\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket
the quantity

\displaystyle\Delta_{jk}=(2\cdot\textbf{1}_{j<k}-1)\cdot(n^{\prime})^{-500}.

Then following
[
50
, Equation (4.16)]
we define the processes
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}=\big(\widehat{\xi}_{1}(s),\widehat{\xi}_{2}(s),\ldots,\widehat{\xi}_{n^{\prime}}(s)\big)
and
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{-}(s)=\big(\widehat{\xi}_{1}^{-}(s),\widehat{\xi}_{2}^{-}(s),\ldots,\widehat{\xi}_{n^{\prime}}^{-}(s)\big)
obtained by “regularizing” the denominators on the right sides of (
D.5
), namely, as the (strong) solutions to the stochastic differential equations

(D.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widehat{\xi}_{i}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{dt}{\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)+\Delta_{ij}}+dB_{i}(t);\qquad\partial_{t}\widehat{\xi}_{i}^{-}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n^{\prime}\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{dt}{\xi_{i}^{-}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)+\Delta_{ij}}+dB_{i}(t),

with initial data
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}(0)=\bm{\xi}(0)
and
\widehat{\bm{\xi}}^{-}(0)=\bm{\xi}^{-}(0)
. It then follows from
13
13

13

Let us mention that there are two differences between the setup of

and that here. First,

]
considers the

.

[

, Lemma 4.4]
(together with

) that

we have

(D.7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[0,(n^{\prime})^{1-3\omega}]\\
j\in\llbracket n^{\prime}/2-(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega},n^{\prime}/2+(n^{\prime})^{1-\omega}\rrbracket\end{subarray}}\Big(\big|\xi_{j}(t)-\widehat{\xi}_{j}(t)\big|+\big|\xi_{j}^{-}(t)-\widehat{\xi}_{j}^{-}(t)\big|\Big)\leq(n^{\prime})^{-15}.

Now set
14
14

14

The choice of
K
in

]
was slightly different, but it can quickly be verified that the below statements will valid for

by only retaining the middle

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\widetilde{\xi}_{i}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n^{\prime}/2-K\leq j\leq n^{\prime}/2+K\\

j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{dt}{\xi_{i}^{-}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)+\Delta_{ij}}+dB_{i}(t),\end{aligned}

with initial data

To compare
\widehat{\bm{\vartheta}}(s)
and
\widetilde{\bm{\vartheta}}(s)
first observe, by (

) and the fact that
\big|G(t_{0}-s_{0},y)-G^{-}(y)\big|\leq n^{\omega/2-1}
(and that

), that we have
\big|\xi_{j}(0)-\xi_{j}^{-}(0)\big|\leq(n^{\prime})^{\omega}
