\big|\xi_{j}(s)-\xi_{j}^{-}(s)\big|\leq(n^{\prime})^{\omega}
for each
s>0
. Hence, subtracting (
D.8
) from (
D.6
) and integrating over
t\in[0,ns_{0}]
, we obtain

(D.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \big|\widetilde{\vartheta}_{i}(ns_{0})-\widehat{\vartheta}_{i}(ns_{0})\big|&=\Big|\big(\widetilde{\xi}_{i}(ns_{0})-\widetilde{\xi}_{i}^{-}(ns_{0})\big)-\big(\widehat{\xi}_{i}(ns_{0})-\widehat{\xi}_{i}^{-}(ns_{0})\big)\Big|\\
&\leq ns_{0}\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,ns_{0}]}\Bigg|\displaystyle\sum_{j:|j-n^{\prime}/2|>K}\bigg(\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)+\Delta_{ij}}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{\xi_{i}^{-}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)+\Delta_{ij}}\bigg)\Bigg|\\
&\leq ns_{0}\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,ns_{0}]}\displaystyle\sum_{j:|j-n^{\prime}/2|>K}\displaystyle\frac{\big|\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{i}^{-}(t)\big|+\big|\xi_{j}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)\big|}{\big|\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)+\Delta_{ij}\big|\big|\xi_{i}^{-}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)+\Delta_{ij}\big|},\\
&\leq 2(n^{\prime})^{2\omega}\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,ns_{0}]}\sum_{j:|j-n^{\prime}/2|>K}\displaystyle\frac{1}{\big|\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)\big|\big|\xi_{i}^{-}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)\big|},\end{aligned}

for any
i\in\big\llbracket\frac{n^{\prime}-K}{2},\frac{n^{\prime}+K}{2}\big\rrbracket
, where in the second bound we used the fact that
ns_{0}=(n^{\prime})^{\omega}
. By
2.17
, and the first statement of
8.6
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that with probability
1-c^{-1}e^{-c(\log n)^{2}}
we have
\big|\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)\big|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}|i-j|\geq(n^{\prime})^{-\omega}K
and
\big|\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)\big|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}|i-j|\geq(n^{\prime})^{-\omega}K
whenever
\big|j-\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}\big|>K
and
i\in\big\llbracket\frac{n^{\prime}-K}{2},\frac{n^{\prime}+K}{2}\big\rrbracket
. Inserting this into (
D.10
) yields

(D.11)

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{\vartheta}_{i}(ns_{0})-\widehat{\vartheta}_{i}(ns_{0})\big|\leq 2(n^{\prime})^{4\omega}K^{-2}=2(n^{\prime})^{4\omega-1/3},\qquad\text{for each $i\in\Big\llbracket\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}-K}{2},\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}+K}{2}\Big\rrbracket$},

where we have recalled that
K=(n^{\prime})^{1/6}
.

Following
[
50
, Equation (4.38)]
, we next define the process
\mathring{\bm{\vartheta}}(s)=\big(\mathring{\vartheta}_{n^{\prime}/2-K}(s),\ldots,\mathring{\vartheta}_{n^{\prime}/2+K}(s)\big)
as the solution to the differential equation

(D.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathring{\vartheta}_{i}(t)=\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n^{\prime}/2-K\leq j\leq n^{\prime}/2+K\\
j\neq i\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{\big(\mathring{\vartheta}_{i}(s)-\mathring{\vartheta}_{j}(s)\big)dt}{\big(\xi_{i}(t)-\xi_{j}(t)+\Delta_{ij}\big)\big(\xi_{i}^{-}(t)-\xi_{j}^{-}(t)+\Delta_{ij}\big)},

with initial data
\mathring{\bm{\vartheta}}(0)=\widetilde{\bm{\vartheta}}(0)=(0,0,\ldots,0)
, where the last equality follows from (
D.9
). Combining this initial condition with the explicit form of (
D.12
), it follows

(D.13)

\displaystyle\mathring{\vartheta}_{i}(s)=0,\qquad\text{for each $i\in\Big\llbracket\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}-K,\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}+K\Big\rrbracket$}.

Next,
[
50
, Lemma 4.8]
15
15

15

It was stated there when the
\bm{\xi}^{-}
paths come from applying Dyson Brownian motion to the eigenvalues of a GOE random matrix, but it is quickly verified that the proof and statement of that result applies equally well for the
\bm{\xi}^{-}
here (satisfying the regularity hypothesis shown in
D.2
).
implies (with a union bound over
2n^{\omega/15000}+1=2(n^{\prime})^{\omega/7500}+1\leq K^{\omega/1000}
indices) that, with probability at least
1-K^{-\omega/1000}
we have

(D.14)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j\in\llbracket n^{\prime}/2-n^{\omega/15000},n^{\prime}/2+n^{\omega/15000}\rrbracket}\big|\mathring{\vartheta}_{j}(ns_{0})-\widetilde{\vartheta}_{j}(ns_{0})\big|\leq K^{4\omega-1/2}.

Combining (
D.14
), (
D.13
), (
D.11
), and (
D.7
) yields with probability at least
1-n^{-\omega/15000}
that
\xi_{j}(ns_{0})-\xi_{j}^{-}(ns_{0})\leq K^{4\omega-1/2}+2(n^{\prime})^{4\omega-1/3}+(n^{\prime})^{-15}\leq(n^{\prime})^{\omega-1/12}
. Together with (
D.4
) (and the fact that
\omega\leq\frac{1}{20}
), this yields the lemma.
∎

D.3.
Proof Outline of
8.4

In this section we provide a brief outline for the proof of
8.4
.

Proof of
8.4
(Outline).

Throughout this proof, we fix an integer
j\in\big\llbracket\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}-n^{\omega/15000},\frac{n^{\prime}}{2}+n^{\omega/15000}\big\rrbracket
; we may also assume that
\chi<\omega
. It suffices to show that, for any real number
A\geq 1
,

(D.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})\big|\geq An^{5}\Big]<Ce^{-cAn};\qquad\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})\big|\geq An^{5}\Big]<Ce^{-cAn},

and that, for any coupling between
\breve{\bm{w}}^{-}
and
\breve{\bm{w}}^{+}
,

(D.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})-\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})\big|>n^{\chi-1}\Big]\leq Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}}.

Indeed, given these two bounds, it would then follow that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})-\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(

\displaystyle s_{0})\big|\cdot\textbf{1}_{|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})-\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})\big|>n^{\chi-1}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2n^{10}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})-\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})\big|>n^{\chi-1}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})\big|\cdot\textbf{1}_{|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})|>n^{10}}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})\big|\cdot\textbf{1}_{|\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})|>n^{10}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2Cn^{10}e^{-c(\log n)^{2}}+n^{5}\displaystyle\int_{n^{5}}^{\infty}\bigg(\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})\big|\geq n^{5}A\Big]+\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})\big|\geq n^{5}A\Big]\bigg)dA

\displaystyle\leq 2Cn^{10}e^{-c(\log n)^{2}}+2c^{-1}Cn^{5}e^{-cn},

verifying the lemma. The bound (
D.15
) is a quick consequence of
2.6
with the fact that Dyson Brownian motion
\bm{\lambda}(s_{0})
, run for time
s_{0}\geq 0
with initial data given by some
n
-tuple
\bm{a}
, has the same law as
(1+s_{0})\cdot\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}\big(\frac{s_{0}}{1+s_{0}}\big)
, where
\widetilde{\bm{\lambda}}
is a family of
n
non-intersecting Brownian bridges on
[0,1]
sampled from the measure
\mathfrak{Q}^{\bm{a};\bm{0}_{n}}
(where
\bm{0}_{n}=(0,0,\ldots,0)
consists of
n
zeroes).

To establish (
D.16
), it suffices to exhibit (deterministic) real numbers
\theta_{j}^{-},\theta_{j}^{+}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(D.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})-\theta_{j}^{+}\big|>n^{\chi/2-1}\Big]\leq Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}};\qquad\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})-\theta_{j}^{-}\big|>n^{\chi/2-1}\Big]\leq Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}}.

Indeed this,
8.3
, a union bound, would yield a constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(\chi,\varepsilon,\delta,B_{0},m)>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big[|\theta_{j}^{+}-\theta_{j}^{-}|>n^{3\chi/4-1}\big]\geq\mathbb{P}\big[|\theta_{j}^{+}-\theta_{j}^{-}|>2n^{\chi/2-1}+n^{-c_{1}-1}\big]\geq 1-c_{1}^{-1}n^{-c_{1}}>0.

Since
\theta_{j}^{+}
and
\theta_{j}^{-}
are deterministic, it follows that
|\theta_{j}^{+}-\theta_{j}^{-}|\leq n^{3\chi/4-1}
. Together with (
D.17
), a union bound, and the fact that
n^{\chi-1}>2n^{\chi/2-1}+n^{3\chi/4-1}
for sufficiently large
n
, this implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})-\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})\big|>n^{\chi-1}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})-\theta_{j}^{+}\big|>n^{\chi/2-1}\Big]+\mathbb{P}\Big[\big|\breve{w}_{j}^{-}(s_{0})-\theta_{j}^{-}\big|>n^{\chi/2-1}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2Ce^{-c(\log n)^{2}},

thereby confirming (
D.16
).

It remains to establish (
D.17
); let us only explain the first bound there, since the proof of the second is entirely analogous. This will follow from the concentration bound
2.17
. In particular, denote the measure
\nu_{0}^{+}=(n^{\prime})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{n^{\prime}}\textbf{1}_{n\breve{u}_{j}^{+}/n^{\prime}}
. For any real number
s\geq 0
, also define its free convolution
\nu_{s}^{+}=\nu_{0}^{+}\boxplus\mu_{\semci}^{(s)}
with the semicircle distribution; let
\varrho_{s}^{+}
denote its density with respect to Lebesgue measure, and for each
k\in\llbracket 1,n^{\prime}\rrbracket
let
\gamma_{k}^{+}(s)=\gamma_{k;n^{\prime}}^{\nu_{s}^{+}}
denote its classical location (recall
Definition

2.16
). By
2.17
(and
2.13
), denoting
\widetilde{s}_{0}=\frac{ns_{0}}{n^{\prime}}=n^{\omega-1}
(by (
8.1
)), there exist constants
c_{2}>0
and
C_{1}>1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\cdot\gamma_{j+\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{+}(\widetilde{s}_{0})-n^{-2}\leq\breve{w}_{j}^{+}(s_{0})\leq\displaystyle\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\cdot\gamma_{j-\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{+}(\widetilde{s}_{0})+n^{-2}\Big]\geq 1-C_{1}e^{-(\log n)^{2}}.

Taking
\theta_{j}^{+}=\frac{n^{\prime}}{n}\cdot\gamma_{j}(\widetilde{s}_{0})
, to prove (
D.17
) it therefore suffices to show for sufficiently large
n
that

\displaystyle\gamma_{j-\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{+}(\widetilde{s}_{0})-\gamma_{j+\lfloor(\log n)^{5}\rfloor}^{+}(\widetilde{s}_{0})\leq\displaystyle\frac{(\log n)^{10}}{n^{\prime}}.

This follows from the fact that
\varrho_{s_{0}}^{+}(x)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
for
x\in\big[\gamma_{j}^{+}(\widetilde{s}_{0})-(n^{\prime})^{2\omega},\gamma_{j}^{+}(\widetilde{s}_{0})+(n^{\prime})^{2\omega}\big]
, which can be deduced from the fact that
\widetilde{s}_{0}=n^{1-\omega}
is small (by (
8.1
)); that
\frac{n}{n^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\breve{w}}^{+}(0)=\frac{n}{n^{\prime}}\cdot\bm{\breve{u}}^{+}
approximates
G
to within
(n^{\prime})^{\omega/2-1}\ll\widetilde{s}_{0}
, for indices
k\in\big\llbracket\frac{n^{\prime}-n^{\prime\prime}}{2},\frac{n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}}{2}\big\rrbracket
(by (
8.4
) and (
8.2
)); and that
G\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})
. This last statement is a standard one about free convolutions that has been proven and applied numerous times in the random matrix literature; we therefore do not repeat its proof here and refer, for example, to
[
42
, Lemmas 3.3 and 3.4]
for very similar arguments.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs of Results From
Section

9

E.1.
Proofs of
9.1
,
9.2
, and
9.3

In this section we first recall several results from
[
33
]
about regularity properties of uniformly elliptic partial differential equations and then use them to establish
9.1
,
9.2
, and
9.3
. To that end, we fix an integer
d\geq 1
and an open subset
\mathfrak{R}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and we recall the notation from
Section

1.3
. For any function
f\in\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{R})
; real number
\alpha\in(0,1)
; and integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, define
[f]_{\alpha}=[f]_{\alpha;\mathfrak{R}}
,
[f]_{k,\alpha}=[f]_{k,\alpha;\mathfrak{R}}
, and
\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})}=\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})}=\|f\|_{k,\alpha}=\|f\|_{k,\alpha;\mathfrak{R}}
by

\displaystyle[f]_{\alpha}=\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}y,z\in\mathfrak{R}\\
y\neq z\end{subarray}}\displaystyle\frac{\big|f(y)-f(z)\big|}{|y-z|^{\alpha}};\qquad[f]_{k,\alpha}=\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}\\
|\gamma|=k\end{subarray}}[\partial_{\gamma}f]_{\alpha};\qquad\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})}=\|f\|_{k}+[f]_{k,\alpha}.

Define the Hölder space
\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})
to be the set of
f\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathfrak{R})
such that
[f]_{k,\alpha;\mathfrak{R}_{0}}<\infty
, for any compact subset
\mathfrak{R}_{0}\subset\mathfrak{R}
. Also define
\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})=\big\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathfrak{R}):[f]_{k,\alpha;\mathfrak{R}}<\infty\big\}
. Then
\|f\|_{k,\alpha}
is a norm on
\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
. By restriction, we have that
\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\mathfrak{U})
for any subset
\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}
. Observe that, for any
f,g\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})
, these norms satisfy

(E.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|fg\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})}&\leq\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})}\|g\|_{0}.\end{aligned}

We further say that the boundary
\partial\mathfrak{R}
of some open set
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}
, if for any point
z_{0}\in\partial\mathfrak{R}
, there exists a real number
r>0
and an injective function
\psi=\psi_{z_{0};r}:\mathcal{B}_{r}(z_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{z_{0};r;\psi;\mathfrak{R}}=\psi\big(\mathcal{B}_{r}(z_{0})\cap\mathfrak{R}\big)\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1};\qquad\psi\big(\mathcal{B}_{r}(z_{0})\cap\partial\mathfrak{R}\big)\subset\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1};

where

\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}\big(\mathcal{B}_{r}(z_{0})\big)

and

\psi^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k,\alpha}(\mathfrak{D}_{z_{0};r;\psi;\mathfrak{R}}).

We call this boundary
\mathcal{C}^{k}
if it is
\mathcal{C}^{k,0}
, and we call it smooth if it is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
for each integer
k\geq 1
.

The following lemma provides the
Schauder estimates
for solutions to uniformly elliptic partial differential equations with Hölder continuous coefficients; as in
9.2
and
9.4
, we state a form of it that addresses interior (
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
empty) and boundary regularity (
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{R}
) simultaneously. Although these bounds hold in all dimensions
d\geq 2
, we only state them for
d=2
.

Lemma E.1

(
[
33
, Theorem 6.6]
)
.

Fix real numbers
r>0
,
B>1
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and bounded open sets
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
has
\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}
boundary. There exists a constant
C=C(r,B,\alpha,\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>1
such that the following holds. For each
j,k\in\{1,2\}
, let
a_{jk},b_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})
denote functions such that
a_{xy}=a_{yx}
and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\|a_{jk}\|_{0,\alpha}<B;\qquad\displaystyle\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\|b_{j}\|_{0,\alpha}<B;\qquad\displaystyle\inf_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}a_{jk}(z)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\geq B^{-1}|\xi|^{2},

all hold for any
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})
denote a function, and suppose that
F\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})
satisfies the elliptic partial differential equation

(E.2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}a_{jk}(z)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F(z)+\displaystyle\sum_{j\in\{1,2\}}b_{j}(z)\partial_{j}F(z)=g,\quad\text{for each $z\in\mathfrak{R}$.}

Assume that there exists some function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
such that
F(z)=\varphi(z)
, for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\cap\partial\mathfrak{R}
. Then, recalling the set
\mathfrak{D}_{r}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}
from (
9.1
), we have

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}\leq C\big(\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}+|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})}+\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R})}\big).

We will be interested in properties of solutions to certain families of non-linear, uniformly elliptic partial differential equations. The following result provides a comparison principle for such solutions.

Lemma E.2

(
[
33
, Theorem 10.1]
)
.

Fix a constant
B>0
and a bounded open subset
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For each
j,k\in\{1,2\}
, let
a_{jk}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denote functions such that
a_{xy}=a_{yx}
and

(E.3)

\displaystyle B^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}a_{jk}(w)\xi_{j}\xi_{k}\leq B|\xi|^{2},\qquad\text{for any $\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$ and $w\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$.}

Moreover, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
f_{i}:\partial\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote a continuous function, and suppose that
F_{i}:\overline{\mathfrak{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
F_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
is a solution to the partial differential equation

(E.4)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}a_{jk}\big(\nabla F(z)\big)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F(z)=0,\quad\text{for each $z\in\mathfrak{R}$},

with boundary data
F_{i}|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}=f_{i}
. If
f_{1}(z)\geq f_{2}(z)
for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}
, then
F_{1}(z)\geq F_{2}(z)
for each
z\in\mathfrak{R}
.

The following lemma provides interior and boundary regularity estimates (which are stated simultaneously, as in
9.2
) for solutions
F
to equations of the form (
E.4
); it is known from
[
33
, Corollary 6.7]
,
[
33
, Theorem 8.29]
,
[
33
, Equation (13.41)]
, together with
[
5
, Chapter 3.6]
.

Lemma E.3

(
[
5
,
33
]
)
.

For a fixed integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>1}
; real numbers
r,B\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
; and bounded open subsets
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\mathcal{C}^{m+1}
boundaries, there exists a constant
C=C(r,B,m,\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>1
such that the following holds. For each
j,k\in\{1,2\}
, let
a_{jk}\in\mathcal{C}^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denote functions with
a_{xy}=a_{yx}
and

(E.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\|a_{jk}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq B;\qquad\displaystyle\inf_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}a_{jk}(z)\xi_{j}\xi_{k}\geq B^{-1}|\xi|^{2},

for any
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then for any continuous function
f\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\mathfrak{R})
such that
\|f\|_{0}\leq B
, the following two statements hold.

(1)

There exists a unique
F\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
satisfying the partial differential equation (
E.4
) with boundary data
F|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}=f
.

(2)

Suppose there exists a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})
such that
\|\varphi|\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+1}(\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})}\leq B
and
f(z)=\varphi(z)
for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\cap\partial\mathfrak{R}
. Letting
\mathfrak{D}_{r}=\big\{z\in\mathfrak{R}:d(z,\partial\mathfrak{R}\setminus\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>r\big\}
, we have
\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}\leq C
.

From the above statements, we can quickly establish
9.1
,
9.2
, and
9.3
.

Proof of
9.1
.

Observe that the second statement of the lemma follows from the first. Indeed, set
G^{-}(z)=F_{2}(z)-\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|
and
G^{+}(z)=F_{2}(z)+\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|
, for each
z\in\overline{\mathfrak{R}}
. Then,
G^{-}
and
G^{+}
solve (
1.2
) on
\mathfrak{R}
(since
F_{2}
does), and
G^{-}\leq F_{1}\leq G^{+}
on
\partial\mathfrak{R}
; hence,
G^{-}\leq F_{1}\leq G^{+}
on
\mathfrak{R}
, implying
\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|\leq\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{1}(z)-F_{2}(z)\big|
. Taking
(F_{1},F_{2})=(F,0)
yields
\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\big|F(z)\big|\leq\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|F(z)\big|
.

Now we establish the first statement of the lemma, which would follow from
E.2
, except that (
E.3
) might not be valid for a uniform constant
B>1
. To circumvent this, we restrict to a subset of
\mathfrak{R}
. More precisely, assume to the contrary that
F_{1}(z_{0})>F_{2}(z_{0})
for some
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{R}
. Then, since
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
and
F_{1}|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}\leq F_{2}|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}
, there exists some real number
\varepsilon>0
and open subset
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
with
\overline{\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}}\subset\mathfrak{R}
, such that
F_{1}(z)-\varepsilon\leq F_{2}(z)
for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
, and
F_{1}(z)-\varepsilon>F_{2}(z)
for each
z\in\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
.

Observe that
F_{1}-\varepsilon
solves (
1.2
). Furthermore, since
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\Adm(\mathfrak{R})
, we have by compactness that
\big|\partial_{y}F_{j}(z)\big|+\big|\partial_{y}F_{j}(z)\big|^{-1}\leq B
is uniformly bounded by some constant
B>1
, over
z\in\overline{\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}}
. It follows from the explicit form (
1.3
) of the coefficients
\mathfrak{d}_{ij}
that (
E.3
) holds (for a larger value of
B
, given by
\pi^{2}B^{4}
). Applying
E.2
then indicates that
F_{1}(z)-\varepsilon\leq F_{2}(z)
for all
z\in\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
. This is a contradiction for
z=z_{0}
, confirming the lemma.
∎

Proof of
9.2
.

For each
i,j\in\{t,y\}
, let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be smooth functions such that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}(u,v)=\mathfrak{d}_{ij}(u,v)
whenever
v\in[-\varepsilon^{-1},-\varepsilon]
, and such that (
E.5
) holds for some constant
\widetilde{B}=\widetilde{B}(\varepsilon)>1
. Then,
E.3
yields a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,r,B,m,\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>1
such that there is a unique solution
\widetilde{F}
to the equation

(E.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in\{t,y\}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}\big(\nabla\widetilde{F}(z)\big)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\widetilde{F}(z)=0,\qquad\text{for $z\in\mathfrak{R}$, with $\widetilde{F}|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}=f$},

and that this
\widetilde{F}
satisfies
\|\widetilde{F}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}\leq C
. Observe since
\partial_{x}F(z)\in[-\varepsilon^{-1},\varepsilon]
for each
z\in\mathfrak{R}
(as
F\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})
); since
F
satisfies (
1.2
); and since
\mathfrak{d}_{ij}(u,v)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}(u,v)
for
v\in[-\varepsilon^{-1},\varepsilon]
, that
F
also satisfies (
E.6
). Hence,
F=\widetilde{F}
, and so
\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}=\|\widetilde{F}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}\leq C
. ∎

We can also establish
9.3
as a quick consequence of results from
[
30
]
.

Proof of
9.3
.

This is a special case of
[
30
, Theorem 1]
; let us explain how to match our setup to the one there. First some point
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{R}
; it suffices to show that
F
is real analytic in some neighborhood of
z_{0}
. We may assume by translation in what follows that
z_{0}=0
; let
r>0
be a real number such that
\mathcal{B}_{4r}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}
. By
9.2
, there exists a constant
C>1
such that
\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2r})}\leq C
and
F\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathcal{B}_{2r})
.

Now, define the function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting
\Phi(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})=v_{2}+\pi^{2}v_{1}^{-4}v_{3}
; also define the set
\mathfrak{E}=\big\{(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\varepsilon\leq-v_{1}\leq\varepsilon^{-1},|v_{2}|\leq C,|v_{3}|\leq C\big\}
. Then we can reformulate the equation (
1.2
) as
\Phi(\partial_{y}F,\partial_{tt}F,\partial_{yy}F)=0
; since
F\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\mathfrak{R})
and
\|F\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathcal{B}_{2r})}\leq C
, we also have
\big(\partial_{y}F(z),\partial_{tt}F(z),\partial_{yy}F(z)\big)\in\mathfrak{E}
for any
z\in\mathcal{B}_{2r}
. On
\mathfrak{E}
,
\Phi
is real analytic; in particular, for any point
(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\in\mathfrak{E}
and integers
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{v_{1}}^{k_{1}}\partial_{v_{2}}^{k_{2}}\partial_{v_{3}}^{k_{3}}\Phi(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})\right|\leq\pi^{2}C\varepsilon^{-k-4}(k+3)!,

where we have denoted
k=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}\geq 0
. Together with the fact that
F\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathcal{B}_{2r})
, this verifies the assumptions in
[
30
, Theorem 1]
(in particular, see
[
30
, Equation (5)]
), with the
M_{k}
there equal to
k!
here. It follows that there exists some constant
A>1
such that, for any point
(t,y)\in\mathcal{B}_{r}
and integers
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
, we have

(E.7)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{t}^{k_{1}}\partial_{y}^{k_{2}}F(t,y)\big|\leq A^{k_{1}+k+2}(k_{1}+k_{2})!\leq A^{2}\cdot(2A)^{k_{1}+k_{2}}k_{1}!k_{2}!,

and hence
F
is real analytic on
\mathcal{B}_{r}
.
∎

E.2.
Proof of
9.4

In this section we establish
9.4
. To that end, we show the following proposition (which quickly implies
9.4
) bounding the derivatives of
F_{1}-F_{2}
assuming
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
satisfy the same nonlinear, elliptic partial differential equation with close boundary data. As in
9.4
, we state its interior and boundary forms simultaneously; its proof will be similar to that of
[
2
, Proposition A.5]
, which provides its interior variant (at
m=1
).

Lemma E.4
.

For any integer
m\geq 1
; real numbers
r>0
and
B>1
; and bounded open subsets
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
has
\mathcal{C}^{m+5}
boundary, there exists a constants
C=(B,m,r,\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})>1
such that the following holds. For each
j,k\in\{1,2\}
, let
a_{jk}\in\mathcal{C}^{m+4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
denote functions satisfying
a_{xy}=a_{yx}
and

(E.8)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\|a_{jk}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq B;\qquad\displaystyle\inf_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}a_{jk}(z)\xi_{j}\xi_{k}\geq B^{-1}|\xi|^{2},

for any
\xi=(\xi_{x},\xi_{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
f_{i}\in\mathcal{C}(\partial\mathfrak{R})
denote a continuous function such that
\sup_{z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}}\big|f_{i}(z)\big|\leq B
, and let
F_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{m+4}(\mathfrak{R})\cap\mathcal{C}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
denote the solution to

(E.9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}a_{jk}\big(\nabla F_{i}(x)\big)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F_{i}(z)=0,\quad\text{for each $z\in\mathfrak{R}$},

with boundary data
F_{i}|_{\partial\mathfrak{R}}=f_{i}
. Assume that there exist functions
\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{m+5}(\overline{\mathfrak{R}})
such that
\|\varphi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+5}(\mathfrak{R})}<B
and
F_{i}(z)=\varphi_{i}(z)
for each
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\cap\partial\mathfrak{R}
. Then, letting
\mathfrak{D}_{r}\subseteq\mathfrak{R}
be as in (
9.1
), we have

(E.10)

\displaystyle\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}(\mathfrak{D}_{r})}\leq C\big(\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}+\|\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m+2}(\mathfrak{R})}\big).

Proof of
9.4
(Outline).

This follows from
E.4
, very similarly to how
9.2
followed from
E.3
; we omit further details.
∎

To establish
E.4
, we will use the following lemma, which is an interpolation estimate that bounds derivatives of a function in terms of its higher (and lower) derivatives; its proof is similar to that of
[
33
, Lemma 6.35]
.

Lemma E.5
.

For any integers
k>j>0
and bounded open subset
\mathfrak{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
boundary, there exists a constant
C=C(k,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that the following holds. Let
F\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathfrak{R})
denote a function, and suppose that
0<A\leq B
are real numbers such that
A\geq\|F\|_{0}
and
B\geq[F]_{k;0;\mathfrak{R}}
. Then,

(E.11)

\displaystyle[F]_{j,0;\mathfrak{R}}\leq CA^{1-j/k}B^{j/k}.

Proof.

Since the proof of this lemma is similar to that of
[
33
, Lemma 6.35]
, we only establish it in the case
(j,k)=(1,2)
; the proof for general
k>j\geq 1
is entirely analogous.

Fix some constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(\mathfrak{R})>1
to be determined later, and assume to the contrary that there exists some
i\in\{1,2\}
and
z_{0}\in\mathfrak{R}
such that
\big|\partial_{i}F(z_{0})\big|>C_{0}(AB)^{1/2}
. Assume that
i=x
and
\partial_{i}F(z_{0})>C_{0}(AB)^{1/2}
, as the proofs in the alternative cases are entirely analogous. Further set
w=(1,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
w^{\prime}=(2^{-1/2},2^{-1/2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Since the boundary of
\mathfrak{R}
is
\mathcal{C}^{k+1}
, there exists a constant
t_{0}=t_{0}(\mathfrak{R})>0
such that either
z_{0}+tw\in\mathfrak{R}
for each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
;
z_{0}-tw\in\mathfrak{R}
for each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
;
z_{0}+tw^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{R}
for each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
; or
z_{0}-tw^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{R}
for each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. Let us assume that the third case holds, as the proofs in the remaining ones are very similar. Then, since
[F]_{2,0;\mathfrak{R}}\leq B
, we have
\big|\partial_{x}F(z_{0}+tw^{\prime})-\partial_{x}F(z_{0})\big|\leq Bt
for each
t\in[0,t_{0}]
.

Thus,
\partial_{x}F(z_{0}+tw^{\prime})>2^{-1/2}C_{0}(AB)^{1/2}-Bt
whenever
t\in[0,t_{0}]
, and so integration over
t
yields
\big|F(z_{0})-F(z_{0}+tw^{\prime})\big|>2^{-1/2}C_{0}(AB)^{1/2}t-\frac{Bt^{2}}{2}
, for any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
. Selecting
t=\big(\frac{A}{B}\big)^{1/2}t_{0}\leq t_{0}
yields
\|F(z_{0})-F(z_{0}+t_{0}w^{\prime})\big|>\big(2^{-1/2}C_{0}t_{0}-\frac{t_{0}^{2}}{2}\big)A
. In particular, selecting
C_{0}
sufficiently large so that
2^{-1/2}C_{0}t_{0}-t_{0}^{2}>3
, this implies that
\|F\|_{0;\mathfrak{R}}>A
, which is a contradiction, verifying (
E.11
).
∎

Now we can establish
E.4
.

Proof of
E.4
.

The proof of this proposition is similar to that of
[
2
, Proposition A.5]
, obtained by replacing the interior regularity, Schauder, and interpolation estimates (given by
[
2
, Lemma A.3]
,
[
2
, Lemma A.1]
, and
[
2
, Lemma A.6]
, respectively), by the boundary ones (given by
E.3
,
E.1
, and
E.5
, respectively). We only address the case
m=1
here (so that it suffices to bound
|\nabla F_{1}-\nabla F_{2}|
), as bounding the difference of the higher derivatives follows similarly by differentiating the equations below. Throughout, we assume that
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{R}
for notational simplicity; the proof in the case of more general
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
is entirely analogous.

Denoting

(E.12)

\displaystyle\varsigma=\max\Big\{\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})},\|\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathfrak{R})}\Big\}

we have

(E.13)

\displaystyle\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq\varsigma;\qquad\displaystyle\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\big|F_{i}(z)\big|\leq B,

where the second bound follows from
E.2
and the fact that
\big|f_{i}(z)\big|\leq B
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
and
z\in\partial\mathfrak{R}
.
Moreover, the
m=5
case of
E.3
yields a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(B,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that

(E.14)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\max_{i\in\{1,2\}}\|F_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{5}(\mathfrak{R})}<C_{1}.

Thus (
E.11
) (with the
F
there equal to
F_{1}-F_{2}
here; the
A
there equal to
\varsigma
here; the
B
there equal to
C_{1}
here; and the
(j,k)
there equal to either
(1,5)
or
(2,5)
), together with (
E.13
) and (
E.14
), yields a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(B,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that

(E.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\big|\nabla F_{1}(z)-\nabla F_{2}(z)\big|&\leq C_{2}\varsigma^{4/5};\qquad\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq C_{2}\varsigma^{3/5}.\end{aligned}

Thus, we may assume in what follows that
\varsigma<\delta
, for some sufficiently small constant
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,B)>0
(to be specified later), for otherwise the result follows from the fact that
\sup_{z\in\mathfrak{R}}\big|\nabla F_{1}(z)-\nabla F_{2}(z)\big|\leq C_{2}\varsigma^{4/5}
from (
E.15
). To improve the first bound in (
E.15
) to (
E.10
), define
g:\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by setting

\displaystyle g(z)=\varsigma^{-1/2}\big(F_{2}(z)-F_{1}(z)\big),\qquad\text{for each $z\in\overline{\mathfrak{R}}$.}

Then the first bound in (
E.13
) and (
E.15
) together yield

(E.16)

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq\varsigma^{1/2};\qquad\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq 2C_{2}\varsigma^{3/10};\qquad\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq C_{2}\varsigma^{1/10}.\qquad.

Next, setting
i=2
and using the fact that
F_{2}=F_{1}+\varsigma^{1/2}g
in (
E.9
), we obtain

(E.17)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j,k\leq 2}a_{jk}\big(\nabla F_{1}(z)+\varsigma^{1/2}\nabla g(z)\big)\big(\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F_{1}(z)+\varsigma^{1/2}\partial_{j}\partial_{k}g(z)\big)=0.

Then, subtracting the
i=1
case of (
E.9
) from (
E.17
), we deduce that

(E.18)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\Big(\mu_{jk}(z)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}g(z)+\nu_{jk}(z)\kappa_{jk}(z)\cdot\nabla g(z)\Big)\\
&\qquad\quad=-\varsigma^{1/2}\displaystyle\sum_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\Big(\big(\nabla g(z)\cdot\kappa_{jk}(z)\big)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}g(z)+\nu_{jk}(z)h_{jk}(z)+\varsigma^{1/2}h_{jk}(z)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}g(z)\Big),\end{aligned}

where for each
j,k\in\{1,2\}
, we have defined
\mu_{jk},\nu_{jk},h_{jk}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{R})
and
\kappa_{jk}:\mathfrak{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}
by setting

(E.19)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mu_{jk}(z)=a_{jk}\big(\nabla F_{1}(z)\big);\qquad\kappa_{jk}(z)=\nabla a_{jk}\big(\nabla F_{1}(z)\big);\qquad\nu_{jk}(z)=\partial_{j}\partial_{k}F_{1}(z);\\
&h_{jk}(z)=\varsigma^{-1}\Big(a_{jk}\big(\nabla F_{1}(z)+\varsigma^{1/2}\nabla g(z)\big)-a_{jk}\big(\nabla F_{1}(z)\big)-\varsigma^{1/2}\nabla g(z)\cdot\nabla a_{jk}\big(\nabla F_{1}(z)\big)\Big),\\
\end{aligned}

for each
z\in\mathfrak{R}
. Now, observe that (
E.1
), (
E.14
), and the fact that
\|a_{jk}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq B
together imply the existence of a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(B,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that

(E.20)

\displaystyle\|\mu_{jk}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq C_{3};\qquad\|\kappa_{jk}(z)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq C_{3};\qquad\|\nu_{jk}(z)\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq C_{3}.

Since the
a_{jk}
satisfy (
E.8
), it follows from (
E.18
), (
E.12
), and the
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
case of the Schauder estimate
E.1
that there exists a constant
C_{4}=C_{4}(B,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}+C_{4}\varsigma^{1/2}\displaystyle\max_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\Big\|\big(\nabla g(z)\cdot\kappa_{jk}(z)\big)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}g(z)\Big\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}

\displaystyle\qquad+C_{4}\varsigma^{1/2}\displaystyle\max_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\big\|\nu_{jk}(z)h_{jk}(z)\big\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}+C_{4}\varsigma^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+C_{4}\varsigma\displaystyle\max_{j,k\in\{1,2\}}\big\|h_{jk}(z)\partial_{j}\partial_{k}g(z)\big\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}.

Thus, by (
E.1
) and (
E.20
), there exists a constant
C_{5}=C_{5}(B,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that

(E.21)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}&\leq C_{5}\varsigma^{1/2}\Big(\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{R})}+\|g\|_{1,1/2;\mathfrak{R}}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}+\|h\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}+1\Big)\\
&\quad+C_{5}\varsigma\Big(\|h\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}+\|h\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}\Big)+C_{5}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})}.\end{aligned}

Now, (
E.16
) yields a constant
C_{6}=C_{6}(B,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that

(E.22)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \max\big\{\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{R})},\|g\|_{1,1/2;\mathfrak{R}},\|g&\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})},\|h\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathfrak{R})},\|h\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\big\}\\
&\quad\leq\max\big\{\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})},\|h\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{R})}\big\}<C_{6},\end{aligned}

where the bound on
\|h\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{R})}
follows from differentiating the definition (
E.19
) of
h
; (
E.14
); (
E.16
); the fact that
\|a_{jk}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq B
for each
j,k\in\{1,2\}
; and a Taylor expansion.

Inserting the first bound of (
E.16
); (
E.22
); and the fact that
\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\geq\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}
into (
E.21
) then yields a constant
C_{7}=C_{7}(B,\mathfrak{R})>1
such that

\displaystyle(1-C_{7}\varsigma^{1/2})\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq C_{7}\varsigma^{1/2}.

Thus, if
\delta<\min\big\{\frac{1}{4C_{7}^{2}},1\big\}
and
\varsigma<\delta
, we obtain that

(E.23)

\displaystyle\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq\|F_{1}-F_{2}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}=\varsigma^{1/2}\|g\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,1/2}(\mathfrak{R})}\leq 2C_{7}\varsigma,

from which the proposition follows.
∎

E.3.
Proof of
9.6

In this section we establish
9.6
.

Proof of
9.6
.

We first perform a rescaling, to which end we define the open rectangles

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}=L\cdot\mathfrak{R}=(0,1)\times(

\displaystyle-L,L);\qquad\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}=L\cdot\mathfrak{S}_{r}=(r,1-r)\times(r+1-L,L-r-1);

\displaystyle\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}=L\cdot\mathfrak{S}=(0,1)\times(1-L,L-1),

as well as the functions
\widehat{F}\in\mathcal{C}^{m}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})
and
\widehat{f}_{i},\widehat{g}_{i}:[-L,L]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for each
i\in\{0,1\}
by, for any
w\in[0,1]\times[-L,L]
and
x\in[-L,L]
, setting

(E.24)

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(w)=L\cdot F(L^{-1}w);\quad\widehat{f}_{i}(x)=L\cdot f_{i}(L^{-1}x);\quad\widehat{g}_{i}(x)=L\cdot g_{i}(L^{-1}x),\quad\text{so $\nabla\widehat{F}(w)=\nabla F(L^{-1}w)$},

which in particular implies that
\widehat{F}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})
. Further define the functions
\widehat{H}\in\mathcal{C}^{m}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})
and
\widehat{h}_{i}:[-L,L]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for each
i\in\{0,1\}
by, for any
(t,x)\in[0,1]\times[-L,L]
, setting

(E.25)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{i}(x)=\widehat{g}_{i}(x)-\widehat{F}(i,x)=\widehat{g}_{i}(x)-\widehat{f}_{i}(x);\qquad\widehat{H}(t,x)=\widehat{F}(t,x)+(1-t)\widehat{h}_{0}(x)+t\widehat{h}_{1}(x),

so that
\widehat{H}(i,x)=\widehat{g}_{i}(x)
for each
i\in\{0,1\}
and
x\in[-L,L]
. The scalings (
E.24
) (together with the assumptions of the lemma) then imply for any index
i\in\{0,1\}
and integer
j\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket
that

(E.26)

\displaystyle[\widehat{F}]_{j}\leq BL^{1-j};\quad[\widehat{g}_{i}]_{j}\leq BL^{1-j};\quad[\widehat{H}]_{j}\leq 3BL^{1-j};\quad\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})}\leq\|\widehat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|\widehat{h}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leq 2\delta.

It suffices to show the existence of a solution
\widehat{G}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{S}})
to the equation (
1.2
) such that

(E.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\widehat{G}(i,x)=\widehat{g}_{i}(x),\quad\text{for each $i\in\{1,2\}$ and $x\in[1-L,L-1]$};\\
&\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{G}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}(\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{r})}\leq C_{1}\cdot\big(\|\widehat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|\widehat{h}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\big);\\
&\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{G}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-5}(\widehat{\mathfrak{S}})}\leq C_{2}\cdot\big(\|\widehat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}+\|\widehat{h}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}\big),\end{aligned}

for some constants
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,r,B,k)>1
and
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
. Then, the function
G\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\mathfrak{S})
defined by
G(w)=L^{-1}\cdot\widehat{G}(Lw)
for each
w\in[0,L^{-1}]\times[L^{-1}-1,1-L^{-1}]
would satisfy the conditions of the lemma (where here, we again used the fact that
\nabla G(w)=\nabla\widehat{G}(Lw)
, as in (
E.24
); to deduce the last bound in the third part of the lemma, we also used
\|\widehat{h}_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}\leq L\|h_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}
for each
i\in\{0,1\}
,
\|F-G\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-5}}\leq L^{m-6}\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{G}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-5}}
, and the interpolation estimate
E.5
to deduce that
\|f_{i}-g_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}\leq C_{2}^{\prime}\|f_{i}-g_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}^{3/m}
for each
i\in\{0,1\}
and some constant
C_{2}^{\prime}=C_{2}^{\prime}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
, which holds since
\|f_{i}-g_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}}\leq\|f_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}}+\|g_{i}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m}}\leq 2B
).

Figure 14
.
Shown on the left is the open set
(0,1)\times\big[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\big]\subset\mathfrak{U}\subset(0,1)\times(-1,1)
. Shown on the right is the open set
\mathfrak{V}
as in (
E.28
).

To that end, let us fix an open set
\mathfrak{U}\subset(0,1)\times(-1,1)
with smooth boundary, such that
(0,1)\times\big[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\big]\subset\mathfrak{U}
; see the left side of
Figure

14
. For any real number
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we also denote its vertical translate
\mathfrak{U}_{a}=\mathfrak{U}+(0,a)
, and we define the domain
[0,1]\times[1-L,L-1]\subset\mathfrak{V}\subset[0,1]\times[-L,L]
by

(E.28)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{V}=\big([0,1]\times[1-L,L-1]\big)\cup\mathfrak{U}_{1-L}\cup\mathfrak{U}_{L-1}.

See the right side of
Figure

14
. As in the proof of
9.2
, for any indices
i,j\in\{t,x\}
we also fix fuctions
\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}\in\mathcal{C}^{m+3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
so that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}(u,v)=\mathfrak{d}_{ij}(u,v)
whenever
v\in\big[-\frac{2}{\varepsilon},-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\big]
, and such that (
E.5
) holds for some constant
\widetilde{B}=\widetilde{B}(\varepsilon)>1
.

Then,
E.3
yields a solution
\widehat{G}\in\mathcal{C}^{m-1}(\overline{\mathfrak{V}})
to the equation

(E.29)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{i,j\in\{t,x\}}\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}\big(\nabla\widehat{G}(z)\big)\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\widehat{G}(z)=0,\qquad\text{for $z\in\mathfrak{V}$, with $\widehat{G}|_{\partial\mathfrak{V}}=\widehat{H}|_{\partial\mathfrak{V}}$}.

We first verify that
\widehat{G}
satisfies the three properties in (
E.27
), and then we confirm that
\widehat{G}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\mathfrak{V})\subseteq\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{S}})
(where the last inclusion holds by restricting
\widehat{G}
, as
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}\subset\mathfrak{V}
) and that
\widehat{G}
solves the original equation (
1.2
). To confirm the first condition in (
E.27
), observe from the definition (
E.28
) of
\mathfrak{V}
that
\{0,1\}\times[1-L,L-1]\subset\partial\mathfrak{V}
, so for any
(i,x)\in\{0,1\}\times[1-L,L-1]
we have
\widehat{G}(i,x)=\widehat{H}(i,x)=\widehat{g}_{i}(x)
.

The second and third statements will follow from a suitable application of
E.4
. To implement this, first observe that

(E.30)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\mathfrak{V}}\big|\widehat{F}(w)-\widehat{G}(w)\big|\leq\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{V}}\big|\widehat{F}(w)-\widehat{G}(w)\big|=\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{V}}\big|\widehat{F}(w)-\widehat{H}(w)\big|\leq\|\widehat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|\widehat{h}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\leq 2\delta.

Here, the first inequality follows from the comparison principle
E.2
(applied twice, as in the proof of
9.1
, first with
(F_{1},F_{2})
there equal to
(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}-\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{V}}\big|\widehat{F}(w)-\widehat{G}(w)\big|)
and next with
(F_{1},F_{2})
there equal to
(\widehat{F},\widehat{G}+\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{V}}\big|\widehat{F}(w)-\widehat{G}(w)\big|)
here); the second follows from the boundary data (
E.29
) for
\widehat{G}
; and the third and fourth follow from the last bounds in (
E.26
).

Now, fix some
w_{0}=(t_{0},x_{0})\in\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
so that
\mathcal{B}_{r}(t_{0},x_{0})\subseteq\mathfrak{V}
, and apply
E.4
with the
(F_{1},F_{2})
there equal to
\big(\widehat{F}-\widehat{F}(0,x_{0});\widehat{G}-\widehat{F}(0,x_{0})\big)
here;
(B,m,r;\mathfrak{R})
there equal to
\big(8B+\widetilde{B},k;\frac{r}{2};\mathfrak{U}_{x_{0}}\big)
here; and
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}
there empty here (so we may take the
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
there to be
0
here). This yields a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\varepsilon,r,B,k)>1
(recalling that
\widetilde{B}
only depends on
\varepsilon
) such that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{G}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathcal{B}_{r/2}(w_{0}))}\leq C_{1}\cdot\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{U}_{x_{0}}}\big|\widehat{F}(w)-\widehat{G}(w)\big|\leq C_{1}\Big(\|\widehat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}+\|\widehat{h}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{0}}\Big),

where the last bound follows from (
E.30
). Ranging over
w_{0}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{r}
gives the second part of (
E.27
).

To establish the third part of (
E.27
), fix some
w_{0}\in(t_{0},x_{0})\in\mathfrak{V}
, and let
s\in[1-L,L-1]
be such that
\mathcal{B}_{r/4}(w_{0})\subset\mathfrak{U}_{s}
(which is guaranteed to exist by (
E.28
)). Then, apply
E.4
with the
(F_{1},F_{2})
equal to
\big(\widehat{F}-\widehat{F}(0,x_{0}),\widehat{G}-\widehat{F}(0,x_{0})\big)
here;
(B,m,r;\mathfrak{R},\mathfrak{R}^{\prime})
there equal to
\big(8B+\widetilde{B},m-5,\frac{r}{8};\mathfrak{U}_{s},\mathfrak{U}_{s}\big)
here; and
\varphi_{1}(t,x)
and
\varphi_{2}(t,x)
there equal to
\widehat{F}(t,x)-\widehat{F}(0,x_{0})
and
\widehat{H}(t,x)-\widehat{F}(0,x_{0})
here, respectively. This yields a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\varepsilon,r,B,m)>1
(which also depends on the open set
\mathfrak{U}
, but we omit this dependence since
\mathfrak{U}
is fixed) such that

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{G}\big\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-5}(\mathfrak{U}_{s})}\leq C_{2}\bigg(\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\partial\mathfrak{U}_{s}}\big|\widehat{F}(w)-\widehat{G}(w)\big|+\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{H}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}\bigg)\leq 2C_{2}\Big(\|\widehat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}+\|\widehat{h}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}\Big),

where in the last bound we used (
E.25
) and (
E.30
). Ranging over all
w\in\mathfrak{V}
yields

(E.31)

\displaystyle\big\|\widehat{F}-\widehat{G}\big\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-5}(\mathfrak{V})}\leq 2C_{2}\Big(\|\widehat{h}_{0}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}+\|\widehat{h}_{1}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{m-3}}\Big),

which since
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathfrak{V}
implies the third part of (
E.27
).

It remains to verify that
\widehat{G}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\mathfrak{V})\subseteq\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{S}})
and that
\widehat{G}
satisfies (
1.2
). Observe that it suffices to prove the former, as it implies the latter since
\widehat{G}
satisfies (
E.29
), and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{ij}(\nabla\widehat{G})=\mathfrak{d}_{ij}(\nabla\widehat{G})
on
\mathfrak{V}
if
\widehat{G}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\mathfrak{V})
. To show the former, observe from (
E.31
), (
E.30
), and the (
(F;j,k;\mathfrak{R})=(\widehat{F}-\widehat{G};1,2;\mathfrak{U}_{s})
case of the) interpolation estimate
E.5
that there exists a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\varepsilon,B,m)>1
(also implicitly dependent on
\mathfrak{U}
) such that the following holds. For sufficiently small
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,B,m)>0
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sup_{w\in\mathfrak{V}}\big|\nabla\widehat{F}(w)-\nabla\widehat{G}(w)\big|\leq C_{3}\delta^{1/2}.

Together with the fact that
\widehat{F}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon}(\widehat{\mathfrak{R}})
, it follows (after decreasing
\delta
again if necessary) that
\widehat{G}\in\Adm_{\varepsilon/2}(\mathfrak{V})
, thereby establishing the lemma.
∎
